The Proper Form

of an

Evangelical Lutheran

Local Congregation.

Independent of the State.

A collection of testimonies from the confessional writings of the Ev.-Luth. Church and from the private writings of orthodox teachers of the same.

Illustrated and

by resolution of the Evangelical Lutheran Pastoral Conference of St. Louis, Mo,

handed over to the public

from

C. F. W. Walther.

Second unabridged edition.

St. Louis, Mo.

Printed by Aug. Wiebusch u. Sohn.

Available from M. C. Barthel, Agent for the German Evangelical Lutheran Synod of Missouri, Ohio, et al. St. 1864.

Foreword.

The present paper was originally prepared as a paper for the sessions of the Synod of Missouri Western District in Crete, Will County, Illinois, on May 15, 1862, and the following days. But as time had not permitted more than the reading and discussion of the first sixteen paragraphs at those sessions, the members of the St. Louis Pastoral Conference now urged that the paper be published in print. Originally intended merely as a document for discussion and therefore lacking the necessary completeness and evenness of execution, the paper seemed to the speaker to be unsuitable for publication in book form. The conference, however, judged otherwise and considered the present to be a time in which a presentation of the right form of a local church independent of the state would be desirable and useful, even in the present imperfect form. Thus the speaker, distrusting his own judgment, has given in and presents his small work to the public.

While the book published eleven years ago, Die Stimme unserer Kirche in der Frage von Kirche und Amt [Church and Ministry] (Erlangen 1852, by A. Deichert), contained the doctrine on which the proper form of a particular church independent of the state is based, the present writing is intended to set forth the practical implementation of this doctrine, and to show that this doctrine by no means leads consistently to anarchistic,, ochlocratic [mob rule], Anabaptist and independentist conditions, as has often been expressed, but rather forms the firmest foundation on which a particular church in its right form can be built. At the same time, the present writing provides the factual proof that our old orthodox teachers, although living in a state church under a consistorial constitution, did not, on the basis of their doctrine of church, office, church government, etc., conceive of the form of a local church independent of the state in any other way than is presented here.

Finally, it should be remembered that the reader is not being presented with something untried for new constitutional experiments, but rather with a church organization that has already existed here for twenty-four years and under which, by God's grace, a not insignificant number of congregations, growing from year to year in one faith and confession and firmly united in the work of love, have been and are still being built up in rich blessing.

the

Lutheran writers quoted in this work, apart from Luther himself.

Arcularius, Daniel, born at Cassel, doctor and professor of theology at Marburg, died there in 1596.

Balduin, Friedrich, born in Dresden in 1575, professor of theology at Wittenberg from 1604, died there in 1627.

Brenz, Johannes, (reformer of Württemberg), born in Weil in Swabia in 1499, died as provost of Stuttgart in 1570.

Brochmand, Caspar Erasmus, born in Kögen on the island of Zealand (Denmark) in 1585, died as Bishop of Zealand in 1652.

Calov, Abraham, born at Mohrungen in East Prussia in 1612, died as professor and superintendent-general at Wittenberg in 1686.

Carpzov, Benedict, (the jurist), born in Wittenberg in 1595, died as privy councillor in Leipzig in 1666.

Carpzov, Joh. Benedict, brother of the former, born at Rochlitz in Saxony in 1607, died as professor of theology at Leipzig in 1657.

Chemnitz, Martin, (principal author of the Formula of Concord), born at Treuen-Briezen in the province of Brandenburg in 1522, died as superintendent at Brunswick in 1586.

Chyträus, David, (co-author of the Formula of Concord), born at Ingelfingen in Swabia in 1530, died as professor of theology at Rostock in 1600.

Dedekennus, Georg, born in Lübeck in 1564, died as pastor in Hamburg in 1628.

Preliminary remarks.

§ 1.

A local Evangelical-Lutheran congregation is an assembly of believing Christians in a particular place, in which God's Word is preached purely in accordance with the confession of the Evangelical-Lutheran Church and the holy sacraments are administered according to Christ's institution in accordance with the Gospel, but which always includes false Christians and hypocrites, and sometimes also public sinners.

Note 1: The fact that pure Word and Sacrament are the main characteristics of a true congregation or church is attested by the Augsburg Confession in Article 7, where it says: "It is also taught that there must always be and remain one holy Christian church. Confession in the 7th article, where it says: "It is also taught that there must always be and remain a holy Christian church, which is the assembly of all believers, in which the gospel is preached purely and the holy sacraments are administered according to the gospel." Cf. John 14:23: "If anyone loves me, he will keep my word; and my Father will love him, and we will come to him and make our home with him." John 10:27: "My sheep hear my voice." John 8:31-32: "If you continue in my word, you are my true disciples, and you will know the truth, and the truth will make you free."

Note 2: Luther testifies that teaching according to our confession makes a congregation like ours. When peace was to be promised to the Lutherans by the emperor, if they would not further "receive or cherish" those who accepted their doctrine, Luther declared:

"If we were to say yes

to this, it would be as much as if we said: Christ should not live, but be crucified again. Moreover, we must confess that the doctrine preached and delivered at Augsburg is the true and pure Word of God, and that all who believe and hold it will become children of God and be saved, whether they believe now or are enlightened hereafter; which confession is to last until the end of the world and the last day. For it is written: Whoever believes and calls upon (God) shall be saved. And not only of those who are yet to come, but also of the Christian church which preaches the word, and of ours who are its members. For it is written Gal. 6:16: As many as walk according to this rule, etc., by which saying no one is excluded. Therefore all who believe and live according to the doctrine of the Confession and Apology are our brethren according to such faith and doctrine, and their danger concerns us as much as ours. Nor can we abandon them as members of the true Church; they may join us if they wish; they may do so in silence or in public, they may live among us or in foreign lands." (Urtheil vom Reichsabschied. Luther's Werke, Hallische Ausgabe, Tom. XVI, 1856. 57 [StL 16, 1538])

Note 3: The fact that a truly Lutheran congregation does not include a specific constitution or specific ceremonies instituted by men is attested by the Augsburg Confession in Article 7, where it says:

"For this is enough for the true unity of the Christian churches, that the gospel is preached with one accord, according to pure understanding, and the sacraments are administered according to the divine word. And is it not necessary for the true unity of the Christian churches that uniform ceremonies, instituted by men, be held everywhere, as Paul says in Eph. 4[:4-6]: One body, one Spirit, just as you are called to one hope of your calling, one Lord, one faith, one baptism."

Thus Luther wrote to Georg Buchholzer, Provost of Berlin, in 1539:

"If your Lord, the Margrave and Elector etc., will have the gospel of Christ preached to you clearly and purely, without human addition, and the two sacraments of

Baptism and the Blood of Jesus Christ according to his institution, and let the invocation of the saints be dropped, so that they are not emergency helpers, mediators and intercessors, and let the sacramenta not be carried around in the procession, and let the daily masses of the dead be held, and not let water, salt and herbs be given, and sing pure responsoria and hymns, Latin and German, in the Circuitu or procession: so go around in God's name and wear a silver or gold creuce and choir cap or surplice of velvet, silk or canvas. And if your lord, the prince-elector, does not have enough of one surplice or surplice robe for you to wear, then put on three of them, just as Aaron the high priest put on three robes, one on top of the other, which were splendid and beautiful; hence the church robes were called ornata (ornaments) in the papacy. And if your Most Reverend Grace has not had enough of one circuitu or procession, that you go about ringing and singing, then go about seven times, as Joshua and the children of Israel went about Jericho, making a shout and blowing with trumpets. And if your lord, the Margrave, has a desire to do so, may Your Most Reverend Grace first leap and dance with harps, timpani, cymbals and bells, as David did before the ark of the Lord when it was brought into the city of Jerusalem, I am well pleased with that. For such pieces, if only abusus (abuse) remains of them, give or take nothing away from the gospel; but only that a necessity for salvation and conscience is not made of it. And if I could go so far with the pope and the papists, how I would thank God and be so happy! If the pope would let me go and preach these pieces freely, and would let me hang a breach around my neck with leave, I would carry it to please him." (Luther's Works, Walch's edition, XIX, 1251. 52. [StL 19, 1026])

Note 4: The fact that non-Christians are also always mixed in with a true church is attested by the Augsburg Conf. in the 8th article, where it says:

"Again, although the Christian church is really nothing else than the assembly of all believers and saints, yet, because in this life there are many false Christians and hypocrites, and also public sinners remain among the pious, the sacraments are nevertheless powerful, although the priests

through whom they are administered are not pious, as Christ himself indicates (Matt. 23): On the throne of Moses sit the Pharisees, etc." Cf. 1 Cor. 1:2. with 5:1-2, 6:5-8; 11:18-19, 15:12; 2 Cor. 12:21. The old Wittenberg theologian Wolfgang Franz therefore makes the following remark on this 8th article of the Augsburg Confession: "The 8th article of the A. C. is connected with the preceding article of the same of the church, because it is added solely to prove: 1. that in the true visible church hypocrites and evil are always found mixed in with the good." (Augustanae Conf. art. disputationibus confirm. Witteb. 1620. E e 3.) Already to the 7th article Francis had remarked: "If the visible church is the question, it is the assembly of the saints, in which the gospel is rightly taught and the sacraments rightly administered, and where in this life many hypocrites and evil are mixed in, both on the part of the preachers and the hearers. But the visible is divided into the particular, as of each house, village, city, diocese, province, nation, dominion, empire; and the general, which embraces all particulars together." (C c 2.) Here, however, we should not overlook what e.g. Carpzov († 1657) reminds us: "Something else is a crowd consisting of hypocrites and true and sincere believers; something else is a crowd to which hypocrites are added. The church as it is actually called is not a crowd consisting of hypocrites and non-saints, but it is a crowd with hypocrites and non-saints mixed in. As the Augsburg Confession carefully explains at the beginning of the 8th article (in the Latin text)." (Isag. in libb. symbol. p. 305) Furthermore, what the old Dannhauer pronounces: "Those (the hypocrites) are not members of the invisible church, nor of the true visible church, but of the visible in so far as they form a whole with others as its members." (Hodosoph. phaen. 2, p. 61.)

Finally, Calov writes:

"Although the hypocrites are in the group in which the church is, they are not actually in the group that is the church." (Syst. Tom. VIII, 253.)

Note 5 That even the name does not decide whether a congregation is truly Lutheran, Luther testifies in

a letter to the city council of Regensburg in 1534, in which he writes thus:

"But this I write, that princes may well promote the gospel among you, whether our confession and doctrine would be kept silent as a Lutheran name, but would be preached to the people from the text of Scripture, so that they may learn that it is Christ and his apostles' own doctrine and would be praised under the same name (without all human names)." (Erlang. Ausg. Vol. LV, p. 58. [StL 21b, 1913 f.])

Note 6 That even this does not yet make a church or congregation a Lutheran or orthodox one, that in it the pure Word of God or the ecclesiastical confession only rightly exists, Luther testifies in his writing "Councils and Churches" of 1539, in which he answers the question: "By what signs the Christian church is to be recognized", first of all thus:

"First of all, this Christian holy people is to be recognized by where it has the holy Word of God .... But we are talking about the outward word, preached orally through men, as through you and me. For Christ left such things behind him as an outward sign, whereby one should recognize his church or his holy Christian people in the world. We also speak of such a verbal word, since it is believed with earnestness and confessed publicly before the world, as he says in Matthew 10:32-33; Mark 8:9: "Whoever confesses me before men, him will I confess before my Father and his angels." .... Wherever you hear or see such a word preached, believed, confessed and acted upon, have no doubt that there must surely be there a true Ecclesia sancta catholica, a holy Christian people, 1 Pet. 2:9, even if there are very few of them; for God's word does not go forth alone, Isa. 55:11, but must have at least a fourth part or portion of the field." (Hall. A. XVI, 2785. 6. [StL 16, 2274 f.])

§ 2.

A congregation is independent of the state when the state leaves it to govern itself in everything.

Note 1: As important as it is when the authorities of a country, in which the orthodox church has its home, also belong to the

church, and as beneficial as this can be for the church (Isaiah 49:23: "And kings shall be thy nursing fathers, and their queens thy nursing mothers: they shall bow down to thee with their face toward the earth, and lick up the dust of thy feet; and thou shalt know that I am the Lord: for they shall not be ashamed that wait for me." John 19:38: "After this Pilate asked Joseph of Arimathea, who was a disciple of Jesus, secretly, for fear of the Jews, that he might take away the body of Jesus. And Pilate allowed it. For this reason he came and took down the body of Jesus."); so the independence of the Church from the state is not a deficiency or an irregular state, but the right, natural relationship in which the Church should always stand to the state. For according to God's Word, the church and the state are entirely different regimes (John 18:36: "My kingdom is not of this world. If my kingdom were of this world, my servants would fight so that I would not be handed over to the Jews; but now my kingdom is not from hence." 2 Cor. 10:4: "For the weapons of our knighthood are not carnal, but mighty in the sight of God to destroy the fortifications." Matthew 22:21: "Pray to Caesar the things that are Caesar's, and to God the things that are God's." Luke 12:13-14: "And one of the people said to him, 'Teacher, tell my brother to divide the inheritance with me. But he said to him, "Man, who made me judge or judge of your inheritance?"); the believing authorities are not in the church as authorities, but as believers, as brothers (Matthew 23:8: "Do not be called rabbis, for one is your Master, Christ; but you are all brothers."). Luke 22:25-26: "Worldly kings reign, and mighty men are called rulers; but not ye: but he that is greatest among you shall be as the youngest, and he that is chief as a servant." Gal. 3:28: "There is neither Jew nor Greek, there is neither bond nor free, there is neither male nor female; for you are all one in Christ Jesus."); the state is not in the church, but the church in the state (Rom. 13:1: "Let every man be subject to the ruler who has authority over him. For there is no authority without God; but where there is authority, it is ordained of God."

1 Timothy 2:1-2: "I exhort therefore, that, first of all, supplication, prayer, intercession, and giving of thanks, be made for all men, for kings, and for all rulers: that we may lead a quiet and peaceable life in all godliness and honesty."), and the members of the church are not subject to the state as a church, but as subjects and citizens, and are bound to obedience. (John 18:37: "I was born for this purpose and came into the world to bear witness to the truth. He who is of the truth hears my voice." Ephesians 5:23: "For the husband is the head of the wife, even as Christ also is the head of the church, and the Savior of the body.")

Note 2: These are noble doctrines which our Church has always held according to God's Word.

a. The fact that church and state are quite different regimes and therefore not to be mixed with each other is attested by the Augsburg Confession in the 28th article, where it says [11 f.]:

"The secular government deals with many other things than the Gospel. Which power does not protect souls, but body and goods against outward violence with the sword and bodily punishments. Therefore the two regimes, spiritual and temporal, should not be mixed and thrown together."

Later, when this mixing nevertheless became rampant even in the so-called Lutheran countries, Luther wrote to a preacher in Dresden in 1543:

"I can hope nothing good, dear Daniel, from the kind of investigation that has been carried out at your court. For if the courts want to rule the church as they please, God will give bad blessings and the latter will be worse than the former, because what does not proceed from faith is sin; but what is done without a call is done apart from controversy without faith, and perishes. Accordingly, they may either be pastors themselves, preach, baptize, visit the sick, administer the Lord's Supper, and perform all ministerial duties — or they may cease to mix the calling; rather, they may take care of their farm and leave the church to those who are called to it, who will also give an account of it to God. For it is not at all to be tolerated that others take care of it,

for which we have to give an account. The offices in the church and at court must be distinct, otherwise we will leave both. Satan still remains the adversary: under the pope he mixed the church with the secular government; in our time he wants to mix the secular government with the church. But we resist with God's help, and endeavor with all our might to keep the calls separate." (L. W. Hall. A. XXI, 1325. 26 [StL21, 2911 f.]. Latin in the Unschuldige Nachrichten. 1715. p. 408.)

It was therefore quite repugnant to Luther to take the authorities to his aid in the exercise of the ban. He wrote to Tilemann Schnabel in 1533:

"That you seem to hope that the execution will be done by the prince himself is very uncertain, and I would not like the civil authority to be mixed into this office, but to be separated in every way, so that the true and certain difference between the two authorities may exist." (Luther's unedited letters, edited by Schütz. II, 288.)

b. The fact that the believing rulers are not in the church as rulers, but as believers, as brothers, is attested in the first appendix of the Smalc. Articles, where it says:

"Kings and princes, as the most noble members of the church, should help and see that all kinds of error are done away with and that consciences are rightly instructed." (Fol. 156.)

Hereby, therefore, the princes are not ascribed rights and powers over the church, but rather duties towards it, and all the more so, the more blessedly their state could lend a helping hand to the church before other estates. Luther therefore wrote in an objection from 1536, which was also co-signed by Melanchthon, Bugenhagen, Jonas and Myconius:

"The appointment and election of orthodox church ministers is actually and originally not a matter for the authorities, but for the church. If the authorities are believers and members of the Church, they appoint, not because they are authorities, but because they are members of the Church. For my kingdom is not of this world." (Cited in Löscher's Unschuld. Nachrr. 1715, p. 383.)

It is true that Luther himself, together with his colleagues, requested the

Elector of Saxony to have a church visitation made in his country, but not by virtue of his princely, but of his loving calling as a Christian and member of the church; he, Luther, says himself in the preface to the instruction of the visitators: they had asked "that His Electoral Grace, out of Christian love (for they are not indebted to secular authorities) and for the sake of God, for the good of the Gospel and for the benefit and salvation of the wretched Christians in His Electoral Grace's lands, would graciously require and appoint a number of capable persons to such an office." (op. cit. X, 1905. 6. [StL 10, 1632])

Finally, Brenz writes on Matt. 18:17:

"That Christ saith, Tell the church, this is not to be understood of a large assembly of the church, in which is the civil magistracy, which upholds public discipline and respectability according to its call, but it is to be understood of a small assembly, of which the civil magistracy is not a member, and in which the magistracy either has no office, or is regarded as a private person, such an assembly being formed by those who accompanied Christ." (In scriptum Matthaei commentarius. Tubing. 1566. Fol. 602)

The godly princes of better times knew this quite well themselves and also recorded it. In the church orders they confirmed, they themselves distinguished exactly what was really church order and what was purely secular law. Prince Augustus' church order of 1580 therefore states, among other things:

"When the authorities .... would show mercy to a malefactor, and yet for the sake of the great offense it is necessary that it should not pass without public punishment, also without righteous repentance and recognition of his sin such a wicked person should not be admitted to the communion of the sacraments, and the authorities therefore also impose an external punishment on him as an abomination and example to others, that he must stand before the church door with a white staff, or the like, several Sundays in succession: this should not be counted as a church punishment, but, as it is in truth, as a secular punishment of the authorities, as the Apology of the Augsburg Confession evidently

testifies; so that the ministers of the church have nothing to do and therefore should not be practiced in the church, where people are not punished physically, but God's Word is preached and the sacraments are administered. For the authority of the ministers of the church does not extend further, for just as they are bound to retain the sins of the impenitent, so they are also bound to absolve every penitent sinner who recognizes his sins upon his confession." (p. 307)

c. That the state is not in, but apart from the church, but that the church is indeed in the state, is attested by the Apology of the Augsburg Confession, when it says in the 16th article [from Latin text [par. 54-55]:

“This whole article on the difference between the kingdom of Christ and the civil kingdom has been usefully explained in the writings of our own, namely, that Christ's kingdom is spiritual, that is, one which begins in the heart with the knowledge of God, the fear of God and faith, and eternal righteousness and eternal life, while outside it we make use of the civil ordinances of all nations among whom we live, just as we make use of medicine or architecture, or food, drink, and air." Johannes Brenz therefore expressly writes: "The state is not in the church, but the church in the state." (see Scholarly Men's Letters to the Kings of Denmark ed. Schumacher. Ill, 193. 4.)

d. Luther testified with great seriousness that the members of the Church are not obliged to obey the state as a church, but as citizens and subjects. He wrote to Melanchthon in Augsburg in 1530:

"The bishop as a prince can impose even less on the church, for that would mean mixing the two sovereignties into one, and he would be a real allotrioepiscopus or a bishop who interferes in other people's affairs; and if we let him have his way in this, we would be guilty of the same church robbery. Here we must rather give up our lives than permit such impiety and injustice. I am speaking of the Church as something special and distinct from the civil state. The bishop as prince can impose and command his subjects as subjects what he wants, if only it is pious and right; and the subjects must obey. For

they obey not as a church, but as citizens. For the same man leads a double person. So when Conrad von Tüngen, as Duke of Franconia, gives his Franks fasting or something else permitted, he compels those who recognize (him as) the duke to obey; but not those who recognize the bishop (him only as their bishop), namely those who are under other princes' territory, even if they belong to the Würzburg church. How Pomeranus compels his servant to his domestic law, but not the church at Wittenberg.... So if the emperor commanded all to fast throughout, then those who are in the church also obey, because the church is under the emperor according to the flesh, but does not obey as a church." (Luther's Werke. Hall. A. XVI, 1207. 8. [StL16, 1015; LW 49, 385])

Note 3: Although our old orthodox teachers also say that the church consists of people of all ranks and that no rank may be excluded in matters of church government, they do not mean to say that the ranks as such are in the church and have special rights therein, but that no rank, no matter how worldly it may appear, deprives the Christian of his spiritual and priestly character and his share in church rights. Therefore, while in one place our old teachers speak of three estates in the church, in other places they make a sharp and precise distinction between the ecclesiastical and the civil estates. Thus, for example, Luther wrote in a disputation of 1540:

"God has ordered three governments (hierarchies) in this world against the devil, namely the house and world government and the church." (A. l.c. X, 678. [StL10, 581])

It is clear from this that, according to Lutheran doctrine, the church is made up of people of all ranks, but that the domestic and magisterial rank itself does not belong in the church, but is ordered by God alongside it. This is in no way refuted by the consistories established on Luther's own advice, since they were originally without jurisdiction. Thus, for example, V. E. Löscher writes in his short "Historia der chursächsischen Kirchenordnung" for the year 1539:

"At that time the first Saxon consistory was also organized in Wittenberg, although it had no jurisdiction." Regarding the year 1542: "At that time there were

already superintendents in most towns, but without any special power."

In 1543:

"A consistory was established in Leipzig, but without jurisdiction, in which, as in Wittenberg, anyone could obtain information."

It was not until 1555, i.e. after Luther's death, that it was stated:

"Elector. Augustus published the Landesordnung, in which much of church matters were to be found, and ordered three Consistoria at Leipzig, Wittenberg and Meisten, together with some jurisdiction."

In 1580:

"The consistory at Meisten is transferred to Dresden and made the Oberconsistorio." (see Unschuld. Nachrr. Jahrgang 1703. p. 24-26.)

When, therefore, already in Luther's time, the Consistories, which had been erected on Luther's advice, were about to come to the end that in them the magistracy as such governed the Church through its lawyers, Luther declared:

"We must tear up the Consistory, for we do not want, in short, to have the lawyers and the Pope in it." (L. W. Hall. A. XXII, 2210. [StL XXII, 1511])

How high a cause Luther had for such a declaration, we see, among other things from the fact that the Lutheran canon lawyer Benedict Carpzov, on the basis of a consistorial decree of 1610, declares:

"The consistory causes those who abstain from Holy Communion to be driven to the use of this most holy meal by serious admonition, and if they remain obstinate, by threat of imprisonment or other punishment (adigi)." (Jurisprud. eccl. II, p. 451.)

§ 3.

In order to recognize when an Evangelical-Lutheran local congregation independent of the state is properly constituted, it is necessary to investigate two things in particular from God's Word: first, in what its rights and duties consist, and second, in what the proper exercise of these rights and duties consists.

13

Chapter I.  ^

On the rights of a local Lutheran congregation independent of the state.

§ 4.

All the rights to which a local Lutheran congregation is entitled are contained in the keys of the kingdom of heaven, which the Lord originally and directly gave to his whole church, and in such a way that they belong equally to everyone, the smallest as well as the largest. Matthew 16:15-19: "He said to them: Who do you say that I am? Then Simon Peter answered and said, "You are the Christ, the Son of the living God. And Jesus answered and said to him, "Blessed are you, Simon son of Jonah, for flesh and blood has not revealed this to you, but my Father in heaven. And I also say to you, you are Peter, and on this rock I will build my church, and the gates of hell shall not prevail against it. And I will give you the keys of the kingdom of heaven. Whatever you bind on earth will be bound in heaven, and whatever you loose on earth will be loosed in heaven." Matt. 18:17-20: "If he does not hear them, tell it to the church. If he does not listen to the church, consider him a Gentile and a tax collector. Verily I say unto you, Whatsoever ye shall bind on earth shall be bound in heaven: and whatsoever ye shall loose on earth shall be loosed in heaven. Furthermore, I say to you: Where two of you become one on earth, whatever they may ask, it shall be done for them by my Father in heaven. For where two or three are gathered together in my name, there am

I in the midst of them." John 20:22-23: "And when he had said this, he breathed on them and said to them: 'Receive the Holy Spirit. Whose soever sins ye remit, they are remitted unto them; and whose soever sins ye retain, they are retained."

Note 1: That all ecclesiastical rights and powers are comprehended in the keys of the kingdom of heaven is clearly explained in the Protestant Harmony of Chemnitz, Leyser and Gerhard with the following words:

"The handing over of the keys is above all the symbol of a certain entrusted power; for he who has the keys has access to everything. For example, when a man hands over the keys to his wife, he testifies that he recognizes her as his companion and entrusts her with the care of the household. In a similar way, the keys are given to housekeepers and stewards by their masters, whereby at the same time the power over the chambers, cellars, boxes and what is kept in them is transferred to them. In the same way, when princes are admitted to a city, they are given the keys by the citizens, which is an indication that they submit to their rule and acknowledge that they have the power to admit someone to the city or to exclude them from it. Christ here applies this image to the church, whose keys he promises to Peter and his fellow ministers, teaching that he wants to appoint them as his stewards and stewards, that they may open the treasures to the worthy and admit them to the possession and use of the same, but close them to the unworthy and unholy and reject them from the kingdom of God. 1 Cor. 4, 1. The word 'key of the kingdom of heaven' therefore means that authority, power and authority by which everything is done that is necessary for the kingdom of Christ or for the government of the church." (Harm. ev. to Matt. 16:19.)

Melanchthon also testifies to the same when he writes:

"It is evident that in Holy Scripture the ecclesiastical power and the keys mean one and the same thing."

(Corp. Reformatorum XII, 494.)

Likewise Quenstedt:

"By the keys is understood the ecclesiastical power, of which the right to call and appoint ministers is a part." (Theol. didact. — pol. IV, 402.)

Note 2 That these keys are originally and directly given to the whole church, that is, not indirectly only through the ordained preachers, and in such a way that they belong equally to every congregation, the smallest as well as the largest, is first of all testified to in clear words by our public church confessions. Thus it says in the first appendix of the Smalcald Articles:

"About this it must ever be confessed that the keys do not belong and are given to one man alone, but to the whole church, as this can be sufficiently proved with bright certain causes. For just as the promise of the Gospel belongs certainly and without means (in Latin it is: principaliter et immediate, i.e. originally and immediately) to the whole Church, so the keys belong without means to the whole Church, because the keys are nothing other than the office by which such a promise is communicated to anyone who desires it, just as it is in the work for eyes that the Church has power to ordain ministers. And Christ speaks in these words: What ye shall bind, etc., and signifies to whom he gave the keys, namely, to the church: Where two or three are gathered together in my name, etc."

The same is repeated in the second appendix with the words:

"Here belong the sayings of Christ, which testify that the keys are given to the whole Church and not to some but to persons (in Latin: non tantum certis personis, not only to certain persons), as the text says: Where two or three are gathered in my name, I am in the midst of them, etc."

This is also how the Church sings:

This is the power of the holy key:

Binds them and makes them single again,

The church carries them at its side,

The matron of Christianity.

(See our hymnal No. 192, V. 9.)

And in the Saxon, so-called Dresden Cross Cate­chism it says:

"The office of the keys

is the peculiar church power that Christ has given to his church on earth."

Our orthodox church teachers also repeat this teaching in countless places in their private writings. Thus Luther writes on John 20:22-23:

"Receive the Holy Spirit, for whom ye remit sins, they are remitted unto them," etc.: "Here it is clearly stated that he hath not the keys, but he that hath the Holy Ghost. Therefore the pope should be painted in his shield (I know well what) and the keys torn out. The coat of arms is another man's, for the pope is. But again, should I not have forgiveness of my sin before the confessor would have the Holy Spirit (and no one can be sure that he has the same): if I would be sure of my absolution and have a clear conscience? Then it would be as before. — Answer: I have put this on, so that one may have a right reason for this thing. *)   There is no doubt that no one binds or forgives sin, except he alone who has the Holy Spirit so surely that you and I know it; as these words of Christ here convince. But this is no one but the Christian church, that is, the assembly of all believers in Christ; it alone has these keys, and you should not doubt it. And whoever usurps the keys from it is a real, foul-mouthed Sacrilegus, a church robber, be it a pope or whoever wants it. Of the same church everyone is certain that it has the Holy Spirit.... It follows that the pope in his office should be a servant of all servants, as he boasts, and yet does not do; that even a child in the cradle has more right to the keys and all those who have the Holy Spirit than he does." (Booklet on Confession

_________

*The doctrine that the whole church or all believers have the power of the keys is thus so emphasized by Luther and by those who believe and confess with him, not because it indicates the order in which this power may or should be exercised, as the opponents of this doctrine always represent the matter in order to make it suspect; but this alone is the reason why this doctrine is so emphasized, so that, as Luther says, "this thing may have a right reason", so that one may know on what all ecclesiastical authority is founded and how inalienable it is to the church, even if the pastors fall away, become tyrants or die.

from 1521. XIX, 1051-54.[StL19, 845-847])

In another place Luther writes on Matt. 18:15-18:

"The keys are for the whole church of all Christians and for everyone who is a member of the same church, and this not only according to authority, but also according to custom and according to all kinds of ways that may be; so that we do no violence to the words of Christ, who speaks directly and in general to all: He shall be yours, etc.; item: all that you will bind." (Epistle to the council and congregation of the city of Prague, dated 1523. X, 1846. 47. [StL10, 1580])

Furthermore, Luther writes on the same passage Matt. 18:19-20:

"Here we hear that even two or three, gathered together in Christ's name, have all the power that St. Peter and all the apostles have. For the Lord Himself is there; as He also says in John 14:23: "He who loves me will keep my words, and my Father will love him, and we will come to him and make our abode with him. Hence it is that often one man who believed in Christ resisted a whole multitude, like Paphnutius in the Concilio Nicenaeus, and like the prophets resisted the kings of Israel, priests and all the people. In short, God wants to be unconnected with the multitude, greatness, height, power and what is personal among men, but wants to be alone with those who love and keep his word, even if they are mere stable boys. Why does he ask for high, great and mighty lords? He is the greatest, highest and most powerful alone.... We have here the Lord himself over all angels and creatures: who says that they shall all have equal authority, keys and office, even two bad Christians alone, gathered together in his name. Let not the pope and all devils make fools, liars and drunkards of us, but let us trample the pope underfoot and say that he is a desperate liar, blasphemer and idolatrous devil, who has snatched the keys to himself under St. Peter's name, when Christ has given them to all in common." (Against the Papacy of Rome, founded by the Devil, 1545. XVII, 1346. 47. [StL17, 1074 f.])

Finally, Luther writes on Matthew 16:19:

"The keys are given to him who

stands on this rock by faith, to whom the Father has given them. Now no one can be regarded as standing on the rock, for he who falls today will fall tomorrow, just as St. Peter fell. Therefore there is no one to whom the keys belong except the church, that is, those who stand on the rock. The Christian church alone has the keys, no one else, although the bishop or the pope may use them, but those who are commanded to do so by the congregation. A pastor exercises the office of the keys, baptizes, preaches, administers the sacrament, and performs other offices so that he may serve the church, not for his own sake" (that is, not in his own special power), "but for the sake of the church" (that is, as one to whom it is entrusted by the church, who does it on its behalf). "For he is a servant of the whole church, to whom the key has been given, even though he be a knave. For if he does it instead of the church, the church does it." (Church Postil, 1525. XI, 3070.[StL11, 2304])

Thus we read further in the Gospel Harmony of Chemnitz, Leyser and Gerhard:

"Christ has left to the Church the keys of the kingdom of heaven Matt. 18:18. And we do not care here for the mockery and derision of the Jesuits, who cry: "So with you cobblers and tailors, all cooks and craftsmen have and use the right of the keys, and so you build Babel yourselves and introduce a complete confusion."" I answer: Who will deny that in an emergency every believer can baptize, teach and absolve another believer from sins and thus open the entrance to the heavenly city for him, as it were, by means of the keys?... But except in an emergency, no one is permitted to do this unless he is a legally called and appointed minister of the church.... Nevertheless, every single believer, even the least of them, retains his right, which he has from Christ's bestowal of the keys. For just as all the citizens of a free imperial city, as many as inhabit it, have a common right and equal freedom as far as the republic is concerned, and just as they elect senators for the sake of order and appoint a

mayor to whom they hand over the keys and statutes of the city, so that he may administer them in the common name of all and govern the republic according to them, so do the citizens of the city of God. They have indeed a communion of all the saints, and all things are theirs, whether Paul or Peter, whether life or death, whether things present or things to come, 1 Cor. 3:22.They possess all things under the one Head, Christ, who has acquired all things necessary for the salvation of his church, and in it especially for every member, even the least, through his bloody merit: and yet for the sake of order they choose certain persons to whom they entrust the administration of the keys of the kingdom of heaven, as with us deacons, pastors, doctors, bishops or superintendents and the like, so that everything may be done properly and honestly with us according to Paul's teaching 1 Cor. 14." (On Matt. 18:18)

Johannes Wigand (Samland bishop, died 1587) writes:

"Every church in every place, that is, the whole body, as we now call it, of the laity as well as of the clergy together, has the power to elect, call and ordain efficient ministers, and to dismiss and expel false teachers or those who are harmful to the edification of the church through their shameful lives. This is clear from the testimonies of the power of the keys, for the keys are given to the whole Church." (Centur. I, p. 803.)

Here Wigand explains that because the keys are given to the whole church, every local congregation naturally also has them. Hence the Formula of Concord reads:

"Accordingly we believe, teach, and confess that | the congregation of God of every place and time, according to good cause, has power and authority to change, diminish, and increase these things without levity and annoyance, in a proper and due manner, as is at all times considered most useful, most beneficial, and best for good order, Christian discipline and discipline, evangelical prosperity, and the edification of the church." (Thor. Decl., X, par. 9.)

Note 3: That a local congregation, in order to have and exercise all ecclesiastical rights, must be externally connected with other congregations and be under one ecclesiastical government

with them, and thus be dependent on other congregations, is an error on which the papacy is founded. Luther therefore writes in his treatise Against the Papacy created by the Devil:

"We know that in Christendom it is done in such a way that all churches are equal, and that there is no more than one church of Christ in the world, as we pray: I believe one holy Christian church. The reason is this: for if there is one church in the whole world, it has no other gospel or holy scripture, no other baptism and no other baptism. Scripture, no other baptism and sacrament, no other faith and Spirit, no other Christ and God, no other Our Father and prayer, no other hope and eternal life, than we have here in our church at Wittenberg, and its bishops are like our bishops or pastors and preachers, neither master nor servant of the other; have one mind and heart, and all that belongs to the church all alike; without, as 1 Cor. 12:8 ff. and Rom. 12:6, one preacher, or even one Christian, may be of stronger faith, having other and more gifts than another; as: one can better expound the Scriptures, this one better govern, this one better judge the spirits, this one better comfort, this one have more tongues, and so on; but such gifts do not make for inequality or dominion in the churches, indeed, they do not make for Christians, Matt. 7:22-23, but must first be Christians." (From the year 1545. XVII, 1398. [StL 17, 1115])

Furthermore, Luther already said at the Leipzig Disputation:

"The church would probably remain standing if the village pastor were at the same time bishop, archbishop and pope" (that is, if he had no preacher over him) "and they were attached to each other merely by unanimity, as Cyprianus says and was the custom of the first | church." (XV, 1299. [StL XV, 1096])

The jurisdiction which persons outside the local church have over it and its pastors is only of human right. Calov testifies to this with the following words:

"In the Old Testament there was a certain ecclesiastical jurisdiction, e.g. Aaron's over the priests and Levites and doorkeepers, but in the New Testament we admit no ecclesiastical jurisdiction which is of divine right, except the general one that everything should be done properly and honestly in the church. However, according to human and consensual

(positive) law, the sovereign exercises jurisdiction either through consistories alone or also through superintendents, as was the case in Creta Titus, or in whatever other way one may deem good, if only the propriety of order is not violated... For this purpose consistories are ordained in well-constituted churches, before which the judgment of official and ecclesiastical as well as matrimonial matters, the investigation of the excesses of the ministers of the Word, the visitation and supervision of the congregations, etc. belong." (System. VIII, 288.)

Veit Ludwig von Seckendorf, the famous author of the incomparable work Historia Lutheranismi, i.e. History of Lutheranism, writes:

"It is therefore safest to stick to the reason given by Christ himself, that he supposed: Where two or three (let alone a larger church) are gathered together in his name, he wants to be in their midst, Matthew 18:20.From this it follows that even such an assembly and congregation has power in and of itself to do and order all that is required for the exercise of its worship, in which Christ has promised His gracious presence, in that such an assembly, although it has an inward fellowship and equality of confession or religion with other Christians, is nevertheless outwardly referred to no one else out of necessity and obligation, but has Christ with it in His word and sacraments, as well as the others; and therefore the proper and certain right of the profession of the divine service and preaching office must also be a piece that comes from the church and congregation, which is authorized to elect one or more skillful persons as presbyteris or elders and rulers in doctrine; since priests already exist in a congregation, they belong first and foremost to the profession and appointment of the others, as well as the person in authority, and no state can be excluded from it. If now, for example, a congregation in India or on a currently unknown island were to be converted to Christianity by a Christian who might have sailed there, it follows from what has been said so far and what theologians know how to maintain that such a commune

could itself call the preaching office and ministry according to God's word, and although it would thus become in the main a member of the general Christianity that agrees in doctrine, it would not be obliged to send its priests for ordination or consecration before a bishop or to a consistory and ministry, especially if it could not do so for reasons of distance or danger; less to be governed in external ecclesiastical matters by foreign places, and yet keep communion by the equality of doctrine and faith with all Christians without dependence in church government; although it would not be a sin or heresy if it wanted to adhere to a certain church and government, as some such Independents in England think, and stretch freedom too high. *) We have examples that our religious relatives in very remote countries, as in Moscow, where there are no churches of our confession for several hundred miles, have a congregation and a public worship service; similarly, there are many congregations under Turkish rule in Hungary, which have their pastors and Exercitia religionis (religious exercises); It cannot be demanded of them that they should necessarily be a member of the external church in other countries and be placed under a certain superintendent or consistory, but such a congregation has its full right to call the ministerii and ministers. The pastor whom they call does everything in such a parish that a bishop or superintendent has to do in a large diocese; for the size and number do nothing to increase or decrease the office itself. Thus, for example, the few disciples at Ephesus, whom Apollos at first instructed and Paul baptized, numbering twelve (Act 19:7), were both a church of Christ, and if

____________

*) It is therefore not the pure Lutheran doctrine that every Christian congregation is independent and autonomous in itself that is independentist in the evil, "historical" sense, as it is now often scolded, but that every Christian congregation should be and remain independent. The former preserves Christian freedom for important cases of conscience, the latter attacks it and violates love.

their number had not increased, this small commune would have had the same right as the larger ones had afterwards, of whose elders Paul says that the Holy Spirit had set them to feed, etc., Act 20:28, and does not direct them to Jerusalem to Peter or to himself, but only from his doctrine, by which he proclaimed to them all the counsel of God." (Christian State III, 11. § 3. 5.)

Hesshusius (died in Helmstädt in 1588) wrote:

"A small group of 10 or 20 people who confess Christ correctly has just as much authority in the kingdom of Christ as a church of many thousands." (On the office and authority of pastors. Edited by Dr. Schütz. Leipzig. 1854. p. 65.)

The Magdeburg Centuries say of the relation of the churches to one another in apostolic times:

"The more respectable churches had no more right than any of the smallest in each province, in teaching, in the administration of the sacraments, in the appointment of offices, in the judgment of true and false doctrines, and in similar duties required of all individual churches." (Ed. Baumgarten. I, p. 943.)

Note 4 That in every local Christian congregation there are also non-Christians who have no part in the power of the keys for their own persons, does not make the use of this power invalid or unlawful, any more than the administration of the office of unbelieving or unconverted ministers is made invalid or unlawful by their unbelief and unconversion; for just as these administer the preaching office not in their own but in the name of the church, so also they exercise the Christian rights not for their own persons but as instruments of the church or true believers. This is why the Apology of the Augsburg Confession states in the article on the church. Confession in the article on the church:

"Therefore we say and conclude according to Holy Scripture that the true Christian church is the multitude now and then in the world of those who truly believe the gospel of Christ and have the Holy Spirit. And yet we also confess that, as long as this life on earth lasts, there are many hypocrites and wicked people in the church among the true Christians, who are also members of the church as far as outward signs are concerned. For they have offices in the church, preach, administer the sacrament and bear the

title and name of Christians. And the sacraments, baptism, etc., are not without effect or power because they are administered by the unworthy and ungodly, for they are there for the sake of the church's profession, not for their own person, but as Christ, as Christ testifies: He who hears you hears me. So also Judas was sent to preach. Now when ungodly men preach and administer the sacraments, they administer them in Christ's stead. And this the word of Christ teaches us, that in such a case we should not allow the unworthiness of the ministers to deceive us."

What is said here about the official duties of ungodly preachers also applies to the exercise of rights that only true members of the church have for their person, namely voting, electing, etc., on the part of a hypocrite in the congregation.

When the Bohemians doubted, for the sake of the unbelievers among them, whether they were a true church and therefore whether their joint appointment of the church office in their midst was valid and lawful, Luther wrote to them:

"Therefore you should not doubt whether there is a church among you if there are already only ten or six who have the word" (in faith). For all that they do in this matter, even with the consent of the others who do not yet have the word, should certainly be considered to have been done by Christ, if only they would act in humility and with prayer, as we have said." (X, 1871. [StL 10, 1599 f.])

Also with regard to the exercise of ecclesiastical rights by dead church members, what Luther, as already mentioned, writes in the Church Postil applies:

"If he does it in the stead of the congregation, the church does it." (XI, 3070. [StL 11, 2304])

§ 5.

That with the keys of the kingdom of heaven every Evangelical-Lutheran local church has all the ecclesiastical power necessary for it, that is, the power and authority to perform all that is necessary for its government, is further confirmed by the fact that in the Holy Scriptures the true members of such a church, namely the

faithful Christians in it, are called: Priests and kings before God, or the holy royal priesthood, 1 Pet. 2:5, 9: "And ye also, as living stones, build yourselves a spiritual house, and an holy priesthood, to offer up spiritual sacrifices, acceptable to God by Jesus Christ.... You are the chosen generation, the royal priesthood, the holy nation, the people of his own possession, that you may proclaim the virtues of him who called you from darkness to his marvelous light." Revelation 1:6: "And hath made us kings and priests unto God and his Father: to him be glory and dominion for ever and ever. Amen;" Anointed ones, 1 John 2:20, 27: "And you have the anointing from him who is holy, and you know all things. And the anointing which you have received from him remains with you, and you must not allow anyone to teach you, but just as the anointing teaches you all things, it is true and is not a lie; and just as it has taught you, so remain with the same." Christ's trusted bride and home honor, 2 Cor. 11:2: "For I am jealous over you with godly jealousy. For I have entrusted you to one man, that I may present a pure virgin to Christ." Ps. 68:13: "The kings of hosts are friends among themselves, and the honor of the house divides the spoil." Christ's body, in and among whom Christ dwells, 1 Cor. 12:27: "But you are the body of Christ and members, each one according to his part." Matt. 18, 20: "For where two or three are gathered together in my name, there am I in the midst of them." Equal brothers, Matt. 23:8-11: "But do not be called Rabbi, for one is your Master, Christ; but you are all brothers. And call no man your father upon earth:

for one is your Father, which is in heaven. And do not let anyone call you master, for one is your Master, Christ. The greatest among you shall be your servant;" those whose all is, 1 Cor. 3:21-23: "Therefore let no man boast. All things are yours; whether Paul or Apollos, whether Cephas or the world, whether life or death, whether things present or things to come; all things are yours. But you are Christ's; but Christ is God's." The preachers, on the other hand, are their stewards, 1 Cor. 4:1: "For this everyone considers us to be Christ's servants and stewards of God's mysteries." Their servants, 2 Cor. 4:5."For we do not preach ourselves, but Jesus Christ, that he is the Lord, and we your servants for Jesus's sake." And finally, the church itself is presented as the highest judgment, Matthew 18:15-18: "But if your brother sins against you, go and punish him between you and him alone. If he hears you, you have won your brother. If he does not hear you, take one or two more to you, so that the whole matter may rest on the mouth of two or three witnesses. If he does not hear them, tell the congregation. If he does not hear the congregation, consider him a pagan and a tax collector. Truly I tell you, whatever you bind on earth will be bound in heaven, and whatever you loose on earth will be loosed in heaven."

Note 1: After the power of every true church "to elect and ordain ministers" has been shown in the second appendix to the Smalcald Articles,

"that the keys are given to the whole church and not to a few but to individuals", it goes on to say: "Finally, this is also confirmed by the saying of Peter, when he says: You are the royal priesthood. These words actually concern the

true church" (that is, the faithful and saints), "which, because it alone has the priesthood, must also have the power to elect and ordain ministers."

Just as in the Old Testament the priestly tribe, specially chosen and appointed by God, had the election and appointment of those who were to exercise the priestly office, and in general the care of the whole church system; so now in the New Testament, in which the believers are declared to be the royal priesthood, they have the exercise of all that power.

Note 2: That the keys of the kingdom of heaven, or the whole power of the church, belong to true Christians, even as to those anointed with the Holy Spirit, is testified to by Johann Gerhard, among others, when he writes:

"Even from this passage John 20:22, 23: 'Receive ye the Holy Ghost, to whom ye remit your sins,' etc.", it is not inconceivable that this power (the key) belongs to all those who are anointed with the Holy Spirit. But since all true Christians have the Holy Spirit, it follows that all have also been endowed with this authority." (Explicatio ev. dominio, p. 732.)

Note 3: Balduin testifies that the faithful also have the power of the keys as Christ's bride and honor, when he writes:

"The Church is Christ's bride John 3:24, and spouse Psalm 45:10, and the mistress of the house Psalm 68:13; as therefore the keys are given by the master of the house to the mistress of the house, so also Christ, the master of his house, which is the Church, has given the keys to his bride, who confers them on her servants, who are called stewards or administrators of the mysteries of God". (De casibus conscientiae, p. 1104.)

Dannhauer further writes:

"The Church is a holy community through the direct and inseparable possession of ecclesiastical rights and offices. For the church is first of all Christ's bride, who distributes the spoil (or the prey of Christ's battle) Ps. 68:13.The key-bearing housewife, to whom the keys are given by Peter, that he may not only faithfully care for the welfare of the church, but also present the person of the church, representing the church and standing in its

place; to whose hand the key is reserved after Peter's death; in which that power is rooted and can be perpetuated without interruption, when the pastors die or become wolves, and when the sons of Levi defile themselves." (Hodosoph. I, p. 79.)

Dannhauer also indicates here how important the doctrine that the church has the key power is, especially when the congregations are either completely deserted or when their pastors become false prophets and tyrants of conscience. According to this doctrine, they do not lose the office of the means of grace with the pastors, nor are they at the mercy of unfaithful shepherds; just as the housewife or "householder" does not lose the keys to her storerooms when her servants and maids die or become unfaithful. Luther therefore writes:

"When the pope is chosen, does he bring the keys with him, or not? If he brings them, he was pope before he was chosen; if he does not bring them, from whom does he receive them? Perhaps from an angel from heaven? Is it not from the Church? Similarly, when the pope dies, to whom does he leave the keys? Does he take them with him? If he does not take them with him, to whom does he leave them but to the Church from which he received them? What then can be said against this quite obvious experience, which the Gospel explains best, that the keys were given neither to Peter nor to his successor, but to the Church alone, from which he receives them as a servant, that he needs them as a priest? Where, then, is this, since it is thought that it was said to Petro alone: I will give you the keys? Yes, where is and where is it that the keys are to be given to the Roman Church alone? It is indeed necessary that the keys should be in every church." (Discussion of the power of the pope against Dr. Eck. XVIII, 948. 9. [StL 18, 737])

Note 4: That Christians, because they are the body of Christ and Christ dwells among them, must have the power of the keys, Luther testifies, in that he adds to the words of the Lord:

"Where two or three of you are gathered together in my name, there am I in the midst of them" (Matt. 18:19-20.), thus writes: "In which passages the most perfect law and custom

is most fully assigned and affirmed, that they may bind and loose. Unless we would deny Christ himself the right and custom of the keys, when he dwells in the midst of two." (Epistle to the council and congregation of the city of Prague. X, 1847. [StL 10, 1580])

Note 5. That Christians, because they are Christ's brethren and brothers among themselves, have the power of the keys or of the church, Luther testifies by writing:

"Therefore the bishop's consecration is nothing else than if he were to take one of the group in the place and person of the whole assembly, all of whom have equal authority, and command him to exercise the same authority for the others; just as if ten brothers, royal children, equal heirs, were to choose one to rule the inheritance for them; they would all be kings and have equal authority, and yet one is commanded to rule." (Schrift an den christlichen Adel deutscher Nation. X, 303. [StL 10, 271])

Furthermore:

"Therefore we are not only his (Christ's) children, but also his brothers, as he says in Ps. 22:23: I will proclaim your name to my baths, and Matt. 12:50: Whoever does my Father's will is my mother, sister, brother, etc.". That we are pastors and priests not only according to the law of filiation, but also according to the law of brotherhood. We want this hereditary and hereditary priesthood of ours to be taken, unhindered and undarkened, but rather drawn out, proclaimed and praised with all honor, so that it may shine and shine like the dear sun, and cast the devil and his larvae and abominations into the eyes, so that his angular consecration and Chresam may shine and stink worse than the devil's filth stinks. Therefore also the Holy Spirit in the N. T. has diligently prevented that the name sacerdos, is not given to any priest or minister or any apostle or to some other offices, but is only the name of the baptized and Christians, that is, an inherited, hereditary name from baptism; for none of us is born in baptism an apostle, preacher, teacher, pastor, but we are all born vain priests and ministers; then from such born ministers one takes and calls and elects them to such offices, who are to perform such ministry for the sake of us all."

(Writings on the private mass and ordination of priests from 1533. XIX, 1535. 36. [StL 19, 1259 f.])

Furthermore:

"Should there be no distinction among the people and should women also be priests? Answer: In the New Testament, priests ought not to wear a tonsure; not that it is evil in itself, for one of them may well let himself be so taken; but because no distinction is made between them and the common Christian man, which faith cannot suffer: so that those who are now called priests would all be laymen like the others, and only some ministers would be chosen by the congregation to preach. Thus there is only an outward difference, because of the office, to which one is called by the church; but for God there is no distinction; and it is only for this reason that some are drawn out of the group, that instead of the church they lead and carry out the office which they all have; not that one has more authority than the other.... Now those have established their own status, as that of God; they have gained such freedom that there is almost no greater distinction in the midst of Christendom, neither among us nor among the Turks." (Interpretation of the 1st Ep. of St. Peter of the year 1523. IX, 702. 703. [StL9, 1014])

Note 6 That the believing Christians also have church authority because the apostle says of them: "All things are yours, whether Paul or Apollos" etc., 1 Cor. 3:21-22. Calov, among others, testifies to this when he writes:

"It is well known that the right to call, as well as the keys and the discipline of the church, is entrusted to the church, according to Matt. 18:18: Truly I say to you, whatever you bind on earth, etc. 1 Cor. 3:21: It is all yours. 1 Cor. 4:1. Rom. 3:2. 9:4. 1 Cor. 5:1 ff. The Church, however, has not entrusted this to the holy office of preaching alone, but exercises it through itself and with the participation of all estates." (System. Vin, 334.)

Note 7: Our confession testifies that the highest judgment within its circle is given to the church or to all believers with the keys, when it says: "Christ gives the highest and last judgment to the church, saying: Tell it to the church. Matt. 18:17." (Smalc. Art. 1. Appendix.)

Chapter II.   ^

On the duties of a local Lutheran congregation independent of the state.

§ 6.

First of all, the church must ensure that the word of God dwells richly among her and that it goes forth in full measure, Col. 3:16: "Let the word of Christ dwell among you richly in all wisdom. Teach and admonish yourselves with psalms and hymns and spiritual songs, and sing to the Lord in your hearts."

Our Confession also testifies to this when it says: "Wherever the church is, there is the command to preach the gospel." (Smalc. Art. 2. Appendix.) Furthermore:

"The Christian church is not only in the company of outward signs, but is especially in communion with the eternal goods in the heart, such as the Holy Spirit, faith, fear and love of God. And yet this same church also has outward signs by which it is known, namely, where God's word is pure, where the sacraments are administered according to it; there is certainly the church, there are Christians, and this church alone is called Christ's body in Scripture." (Apology in the Art. of the Church.)

Furthermore:

"The right outward church adornment is also right preaching, right use of the sacraments and that the people are accustomed to it with earnestness and come together with diligence and duly, learn and pray." (Apology in the Art. of the Mass.)

Furthermore:

"The greatest, holiest, most necessary, highest divine service, which God has demanded in the first and other commandments as the greatest, is to preach God's word, for the office of preaching is the highest office in the church." (Ib. in the type of human ordinances in the church.)

Furthermore, Luther testifies in his 1539 treatise Councils and Churches:

"God's Word cannot be without God's people. Again, God's people cannot be without God's Word. Who else would preach it or hear it preached if there were no people of God? And what could or would

God's people believe if God's Word were not there? ... On the other hand, God's people or the Christian holy people are known by the holy sacrament of baptism, where they are taught, believed and used according to Christ's order. For this is also a public sign and a precious sanctuary by which God's people are sanctified.... Thirdly, God's people, or a Christian holy people, are known by the holy sacrament of the altar, where it is administered, believed in and received according to Christ's institution. For it is also a public sign and sacred sanctuary, left behind by Christ, by which his people are sanctified, so that they also practice and publicly confess that they are Christians, as they do with the Word and baptism." (XVI, 2786-88. [StL 16, 2276 f.])

§ 7.

On the other hand, the church must take care of the purity of doctrine and life in its midst and therefore exercise discipline on its members in both respects. Matthew 18:15-18: "But if your brother sins against you, go and punish him between you and him alone. If he listens to you, you have gained your brother. If he does not hear you, take one or two more to you, so that the whole matter may rest on the mouth of two or three witnesses. If he does not hear them, tell the congregation. If he does not hear the congregation, consider him a pagan and a tax collector. Truly I tell you, whatever you bind on earth will be bound in heaven, and whatever you loose on earth will be loosed in heaven." Romans 16:17: "But I urge you, brethren, to look out for those who cause division and offense apart from the teaching you have learned, and depart from them." 1 Cor 5:1-13: "There is a common cry that there is fornication among you, and such fornication as even the Gentiles do not know how to say that a man has his father's wife. And ye are puffed up, and have not rather borne sorrow, that he that did the work might

be done by you. I indeed, who am not present with the body, but am present with the spirit, have already judged as present him who has done these things: In the name of our Lord Jesus Christ, in your assembly with my spirit, and with the power of our Lord Jesus Christ, to deliver him to Satan for the destruction of the flesh, that the spirit may be saved in the day of the Lord JEsu. Your glory is not good. Do you not know that a little leaven leavens the whole lump? Therefore purge out the old leaven, that ye may be a new dough, as ye are unleavened. .... I wrote unto you in the epistle, that ye should have nothing to do with the harlots. I do not mean the harlots of this world, or the covetous, or the robbers, or the idolaters; otherwise you would have to leave the world. But now I have written to you that you should have nothing to do with them; for if anyone who lets himself be called a brother is a fornicator, or a covetous man, or an idolater, or a blasphemer, or a drunkard, or a robber, you should not eat with him. For what concern is it of those outside that I should judge them? Do you not judge those who are inside? But God will judge those who are outside. Put out from among yourselves those who are evil." 6:1-8: "How can anyone among you, if he has a bargain with another, quarrel before the unrighteous and not before the saints? Do you not know that the saints will judge the world? If then the world is to be judged by you, are you not good enough to judge lesser things? Do you not know that we will judge the angels? How much more over temporal things? But you, if you have things over temporal goods, take those

who are despised by the church and set them up as judges. To your shame I must say this. Is there no wise man among you, or not one who could judge between brother and brother? But one brother quarrels with another, and that before the unbelievers. It is already a fault among you that you argue with one another. Why do you not rather let yourselves be wronged? why do you not rather let yourselves be judged? Rather, you do wrong, and you dishonor your brothers." 2 Cor 2:6-11: "But it is enough that he has been punished by many, so that you should now forgive and comfort him all the more, so that he may not sink into too much sorrow. Therefore I exhort you to show your love for him. For this is why I have written to you, that I may know whether you are righteous to be obedient in all things. But to whom you forgive anything, I also forgive. For I also, if I forgive anyone anything, forgive it in Christ's stead for your sake, so that we may not be overtaken by Satan. For we are not unaware of what he has in mind." Gal. 6:1: "Brethren, if any man be overtaken in a fault, let him be corrected in a gentle spirit by you who are spiritual. And take heed to yourselves, lest you also be tempted." 1 Thessalonians 5:14: "But we exhort you, brethren, admonish the unruly, comfort the fainthearted, bear with the weak, be patient toward all men." 2 Thess. 3:6, 14-15: "Now we command you, brethren, in the name of our Lord Jesus Christ, that you withdraw yourselves from every brother who walks disorderly and not according to the law which he received from us. But if any man obey not our word, mark him with a letter, and have

nothing to do with him, that he may be ashamed. Yet do not regard him as an enemy, but admonish him as a brother." 2 John 10:11: "If anyone comes to you and does not bring this teaching, do not take him home, nor greet him. For whoever greets him makes himself partaker of his evil deeds."

Note 1: Our Confession testifies that the congregation has the care and thus the judgment over doctrine with the following words:

"As soon as the right judgment and knowledge (that is, judgment) is taken from the church, it cannot be possible that one could control false doctrine or unrighteous worship, and therefore many souls must be lost."

But that all Christians are to be understood here by the Church is evident from the following statement:

"And just as Christians are guilty of punishing all other errors of the pope, so they are also guilty of punishing the pope himself if he flees or refuses the right judgment and true knowledge of the Church." (Smalc. Art. 2. Appendix.)

Furthermore, Luther writes:

"To recognize and judge doctrine belongs before all and every Christian, and in such a way that he is cursed who infringes this right by a hair's breadth. For Christ himself has decreed such a right in invincible and many sayings, e.g. Matt. 7: Beware of the false prophets who come to you in sheep's clothing. He certainly says this word to the people against the teachers, and commands them to avoid their false teaching. But how can they avoid them without recognizing them, and how can they recognize them if they have no power to judge? But now he not only gives them the power to judge, but also grants them that this single passage can be enough against all the popes of all the fathers, all the councils, all the sayings of all the schools, which have granted the right to judge and conclude only to the bishops and clergy, but have robbed the people, that is, the church, the queen, of it in an ungodly and church-robbing manner. For there Christ stands and says: Beware of the false prophets. Almost all the syllables of the prophets agree with this. .... For just as

everyone believes rightly or wrongly at his own peril, so also everyone must take reasonable care that he believes rightly; that common sense and the necessity of salvation dictate that the judgment about the doctrine must necessarily be with the listener." Luther wants to say that to deny this is as much as to grant the judgment about the goods to the seller alone, but to deny it to the buyer. (Schrift gegen König Heinrich in England vom J. 1523. XIX, 424-426. [StL 19, 341 f.])

But that the church has just as much right and duty to take care of purity of life, Luther testifies when he writes:

"What then now in our times hinders the ban? Nothing, except that no one does in this matter what is due and proper to a Christian. You have a neighbor whose life and conduct you are well aware of and know, but your pastor is either unaware of it or not so well aware of it; for how can he know in particular what each person's life is like? Therefore, if you see that your neighbor is getting rich through unrighteous dealings or trade, if you see that he is committing fornication or adultery, or that he is industriously and negligently breeding and governing his servants, you should first admonish him and give him a Christian warning that he may be aware of his blessedness and avoid trouble. And oh, what a good and holy work you have done if you win him over in this way! But dear one, who will do it? For in the first place, truth is a hostile thing; he who tells the truth is resented. Therefore you would rather keep your neighbor's friendship and favor, especially if he is rich and powerful, than to anger him and make an enemy of him. Likewise, if the other, third or fourth neighbor does the same, then with the first admonition the other and the third also fall into the well, by which the neighbor could have been brought back to the right way, if only you had done with admonition what you owed and were obligated to do." (On Joel 3:17. VI, 2404. 2405. [StL 6, 1633])

Note 2 But that the congregation has the duty to exclude from itself and banish those who despise all admonition, Luther testifies when he writes:

"You hear here, Matt. 18.that there must be certain public sins, of certain known persons, where one brother sees the other sinning, and

such sins which are first punished fraternally and finally convicted publicly before the congregation; therefore, the bulls and letters of banishment, wherein it is thus written: Excommunicamus ipso facto, data sententia, trina tamen monitione praemissa; item, de plenitudine potestatis" (i.e. we hereby actually banish after the judgment has been passed, but after three prior warnings; again, we do this by virtue of the fullness of our authority), "that is called a sh...ban in German. I call it the devil's ban and not God's ban, since people are banned with wicked deeds before they are publicly convicted before the church against Christ's order. In the same way, all the bannings are the deception of officials and ecclesiastical judges, and the fact that people are banned from a church for 10, 20 or 30 miles with a piece of paper, even though they have never been punished, accused or convicted in the same church or before the parish priest; instead, a bat comes out of an official's corner without witnesses and without God's command. You must not be afraid of such a sh… [shit] ban. If a bishop or official wants to banish someone, go or send him to the congregation or to the parish priest, where he is to be banned, and do to him as is right according to these motes of Christ. — And I say all this because the church that is to banish such a person should know and be certain how he deserves to be banned and how he came to be banned, as the text of Christ indicates; otherwise it might be deceived and accept a lying ban and thus do injustice to its neighbor. That would be blaspheming the keys and dishonoring God and dishonoring love for one's neighbor, which is not to be suffered by a Christian community. For it also belongs to it if someone is to be banished from it, says Christ, and is not guilty of believing the official's note or the bishop's letters, indeed, it is guilty of not believing here; for one should not believe men in the things of God.
