So a Christian congregation is not the official's maid, nor the bishop's master, that he may say to her: there, Grete, there, Hans, keep this one or that one under my spell. Awe, yes, welcome to us, dear Official! In worldly authority such a thing would have an opinion, but here, as it concerns souls, the

church should also be with judge and wife. St. Paul was an apostle, nor did he want to banish the one who had taken his stepmother; he also wanted the church to be involved, 1 Cor. 5:1. 5." (Schrift von den Schlüsseln vom J. 1530. XIX, 1181. 82. [StL 19, 950 f.])

What is important, however, is what Ludwig Hartmann writes about the exercise of the ban in the following words:

"Those who cannot be separated without rebellion are not to be put under ban. Thus Augustine (in the writing against Cresconius in the 3rd book, 4th chapter) would rather have tolerated an "unrighteous" (otherwise rightly teaching) "church minister, who is not hidden and is evident to some good men, as a weed with Cyprian, than to see him separated from the congregation with the awakening of a seditious party. The same Augustine could not agree that all those in Africa given to drunkenness should be banned, because he saw that this vice was spread throughout Africa and that therefore, if all those given to drunkenness were banned, it would happen that no one or few would have communion in the church. We tolerate some, he says in the letter to Vincentius, whom we cannot exclude or punish; we do not leave the threshing floor of the Lord for the sake of the chaff, nor do we leave the flock of Christ for the sake of the goats, which in the end are to be separated. Thus, even if there is no competent presbytery or if the people do not consent to the just banishment, the solemn trial may be omitted, but a faithful minister must work towards it and watch with the rest of the pious and faithful that public offenses are punished and that the sacred is not thrown to the dogs or sows." (Pastorale ev. p. 874.)

Hence it is rejected as an error in the Formula of Concord, when the Schwenkfelder assert:

"that there is no true Christian community, since no public excommunication or due process of excommunication is held." (Thor. Decl., XII, “Erroneous Articles of the Schwenckfeldians,” par. 6)

§ 8.

Thirdly, the church must be concerned that all its members are well provided for in earthly things, that they do not lack the

necessities of life, nor are they abandoned in any need. Gal 6:10: "As we have therefore time, let us do good unto all men, but chiefly unto them which are of the household of faith." Deut. 15:4: "There shall be no beggar among you, for the Lord will bless you in the land which the Lord your God will give you to inherit." Romans 12:13: "Take care of the needs of the saints. Give hospitality gladly." Gal 2:9-10: "And recognizing the grace that was given to me, Jacob and Cephas and John, who were esteemed as pillars, gave me and Barnabah the right hand, and became one with us, that we should preach among the Gentiles, and they among the circumcision, only that we might remember the poor, which I also have been diligent to do." James 1:27: "Pure and undefiled worship before God the Father is this: To visit the fatherless and widows in their affliction, and to keep himself unspotted from the world." 1 Thessalonians 4:11-12: "And take heed that ye be quiet, and do your own work, and labor with your own hands, as we have commanded you; that ye walk uprightly toward them which are without, having need of none."

Thus Luther writes in his church epistle Über das Evangelium am Tag St. Stephani:

"In this history you see first of all how a Christian community should be organized; in addition, you see a true picture of a spiritual government which the apostles lead here. They care for souls, deal with preaching and praying, but also see to it that the body is cared for, raising up certain men to distribute the goods, as you have heard. Thus the Christian regime provides for people in body and soul, so that no one is in want, as Lucas says, and all are richly fed and well provided for, both in body and soul. This is a very fine picture and example, and it would be good if people were to begin to divide a city, such as this one, into four or five

parts, and give each part a preacher and a number of deacons who would "supply the same part with sermons and distribute the goods", visit sick people, and see to it that no one suffers want. But we do not have the people to do this, so I do not dare to do it until our Lord God makes Christians." (XI, 2754. 5. [StL 11, 2065])

In his Commentary on the Epistle to the Galatians Luther writes further:

"When a faithful shepherd or pastor has provided for his people with the preaching of the Gospel above all things, he should not leave any thing to him so diligently as that the poor may also be fed and preserved. For this is never lacking, wherever there is a church or congregation of God, there must certainly also be the poor, who are generally only the righteous students or disciples of the gospel; as Christ himself testifies in Matthew 11:5: The poor have the gospel preached to them; and in 1 Corinthians 1:27-28: What is foolish in the sight of the world, etc. For evil men and the devil persecute the church or congregation of God, and make many poor people, who are then afterwards forsaken, so that no one will take care of them, nor give them anything. In addition to this, there are very few who will do their utmost to preserve the preaching ministry, that is, to order it in such a way that it will remain in proper use for our descendants after we have died. This cannot be done in any other way than by providing the servants of the preaching ministry with sufficient necessities; likewise the schools in all towns and villages, from which all persons capable of both spiritual and secular government are taken, should be staffed with God-fearing and learned men, and they should be provided with food for their hard, unenjoyable and yet extremely useful and necessary work. To do such a thing is exceedingly difficult. For what concerns God's honor and the happiness of our souls, no one wants to promote; indeed, where one can only hinder it, one does so with all one's heart. On the other hand, where error, lies, false worship and idolatry are to be established, people not only contribute from all angles, but are also willing and

devout to do so. As can be seen in the papacy, where so many altars, chapels, monasteries, cathedral churches and bishoprics have been founded and so much interest and rent has been devoted to their maintenance that it is unspeakable; and yet they are full of all ungodliness, indeed, the devil himself reigns and rules there in the flesh. But now, when a city is barely able to feed one or two ministers of the gospel, people complain exceedingly.... Since it so happens that the Christians here are generally poor, abandoned and destitute, a proper bishop should take care of them so that they do not suffer hardship; which St. Paul confesses here (Gal. 2:10) that he has done." (On Gal. 2:10. VIII, 1762-4.)

§ 9.

The congregation must see to it that everything under it is orderly and honest, not only before the Lord but also before men. 1 Cor. 14:33, 40: "For God is not a God of disorder, but of peace, as in all the churches of the saints. Let all things be done honestly and in order." 2 Cor. 8:20, 21: "And take heed lest any man speak evil of us, because of the rich tax that is paid by us: seeing that all things be done honestly, not only before the Lord, but also before men." Col 2:5: "For though I am absent in the flesh, yet am I with you in spirit, rejoicing to see your order and your steadfast faith in Christ."

Note: When the 28th article of the Augsburg Confession states:

"The bishops or pastors may make order, so that things may be done properly in the churches", this is not to say that only the preachers and bishops have the duty to ensure good order in the congregation; rather 1. Rather, it takes into account the existing order at that time, which our orthodox, godly fathers were not willing to overthrow, but

rather to maintain where it could be done without compromising the gospel; 2. it should not forgive the right and duty of the whole congregation. The old theologian Carpzov therefore adds to these words in his excellent introduction to the symbolic books: "It is to be observed, if the Augsburg Confession at this point allows the bishops the right to order ceremonies, that this is done 1. according to the nature of that time, when this was also granted to them by human right, as the paragraph beginning with the words: "But that the bishops otherwise (apart from what they are entitled to by divine right) have power and judicial compulsion" etc., [cf. Art. XXVIII, par. 29; German text]   2. that nothing is thereby taken away from the rights of the whole Church, as the Augsburg Confession not unambiguously indicates. Confession not unambiguously indicates at the same time." (Isagoge, p. 750.) In another place, the same Carpzov adds in explanation of those words of the Augsburg Confession: "All this, however, does not exclude, but rather includes, the consensus (agreement) of the church, so that the bishops always have a church unanimous with them, and that such orders are not made without the agreement of the church or against the will of the same." (p. 745.)

Our Confession expressly confirms this when it says in the Formula of Concord:

"We believe, teach and confess that the church of God in every place and at every time, according to the occasion, has power to change such ceremonies as may be most useful and edifying to the church of God." (Art. 10. of the summary term.)

Thus Luther also writes:

"A bishop, as a bishop, has no power to interpret some statute or ceremony to his church without the consent of the church in clear words or in a tacit manner. Because the church is free and a ruler (woman), and bishops may not rule over the faith of the churches, nor trouble and harass them against their will. For they are only servants and stewards, but not masters of the churches. If, however, the church as one body agrees with the bishop, they can impose whatever they like on each other, if only godliness does not suffer as a result;

they can also leave such things as they like." (Luther's answer to Melanchthon in Augsburg to the questions sent to him about the statutes of man from 1530. XVI, 1207 [StL 16, 1014])

Furthermore, Gerhard writes:

"The constitutive (or constitutional) power is the power of the church to establish regulations and rules for order and honor in external and central matters, and to establish certain customs, and to promote the conformity of the members of the church in external worship, or to abolish them, as the need or the benefit of the church requires. These powers, however, belong to the whole Church, but are not peculiar to the ecclesiastical state, although we readily admit that the first and chief parts of that power belong to the church office." (Loc. de minist. § 193.)

§ 10.

The congregation has the duty to strive for unity in the Spirit in the bond of love and peace with the orthodox church apart from it. Ephes. 4:3: "Be diligent to maintain unity in the Spirit through the bond of peace." 1 Thess. 4:9-10: "But there is no need to write to you about brotherly love, for you yourselves were taught by God to love one another, and this you also do to all the brethren who are in all Macedonia. But we exhort you, brethren, that you may be more perfect." Romans 15:26-27: "For it hath pleased them of Macedonia and Achaia to make a certain contribution for the poor saints which are at Jerusalem. It hath pleased them verily, and their debtors they are; for if the Gentiles have been made partakers of their spiritual things, their duty is also to minister unto them in carnal things.” 2 Cor. 8:19: "But not only this, but he is also ordained by the Gentiles to be a companion of our journey in this

good deed, which is done through us in honor of the Lord, and (in praise of) your good will."

Note: The old Leipzig theologian Hieronymus Kromayer also testifies to this when he writes:

"Not only is the truth to be recognized, but fellowship is also to be cultivated with the confessors of the same, according to the sayings of Ephes. 4:3: Be diligent to maintain unity in the Spirit through the bond of peace. 1 Cor. 12:12: As there is one body, and yet has many members, and all the members of one body, though they are many, are one body: so also is Christ. For by one Spirit we were all baptized into one body, whether we be Jews or Greeks, whether we be bond or free, and have all been made to drink into one Spirit." (Theol. posit.-pol. Lips. 1677. p. 1029.)

Luther therefore declares:

"Where it can otherwise be done without sin and without driving or without annoyance, it is quite fine that the churches also compare themselves in outward things, which are nevertheless free, as they compare themselves in spirit, faith, word, sacrament, etc.. For this is fine and pleasing to everyone. Also because such inequality, being unnecessary, looks very much like a schism, disunity or separation of hearts.... For St. Paul faithfully teaches us everywhere that we should be diligent to teach and live in unity and harmony and guard against disunity or inequality wherever we can, Rom. 12:16. 1 Cor. 1:10." (Kurzes Bekenntniß vom heiligen Sacrament vom 2. 1545. Erlanger Ausgabe XXXII, 423-25. [StL 20, 1790 f.])

On the words: "Nehmet euch der Heiligen Nothdurft an" Dr. Heinrich Müller writes:

"Paul wants us to make ourselves partakers of their need, that is, we should regard and respect it as if it were our own, and as we would gladly have done to ourselves, so should we do to them. It is not enough for us to give them our gift, but before we give them our alms, we should first take their misery to ourselves, so that it may distress and grieve us as if we felt it ourselves; as the Spirit says to the Hebrews (Heb. 13:3): "Remember those who are bound as those who are bound with you. For where there is union, there is also fellowship in suffering, as can be seen in the members of one body. When the

foot hurts, the eyes weep and the hand dries them again." (Apostolic closing chain. p. 48.)

§ 11.

It is also incumbent on the congregation to help in its part so that the church as a whole is built and promoted. Amos 6:6: "You drink wine from the bowls and anoint yourselves with balm, and do not worry about the harm of Joseph." Acts 11:21-23: "And the hand of the Lord was with them, and a great number believed and turned to the Lord. Now these words of theirs came to the ears of the church in Jerusalem, and they sent Barnabam to go as far as Antioch. And when he was come, and saw the grace of God, he was glad, and exhorted them all to cleave unto the Lord with a stedfast heart." Acts 15:1 ff.

Note: Luther testifies to this by reminding us that God instituted holy baptism alongside preaching and established visible Christian congregations through it, so that the church might be continually expanded and preserved to the end. He writes:

"That Christ, in the first part: 'To him that believeth' Christ adds and tells of Baptism, which is the command of the outward ministry in Christendom, as he also summarizes it in Matthew 28:19: 'Teach all the Gentiles and baptize them,' and so on. And show first of all that nevertheless the faith of which this gospel preaches must not remain secret and hidden; as if it were enough that each one, when he hears the gospel, should go and believe for himself alone, and not confess his faith before others; but that it may be manifest, not only where the gospel is preached, but also received and believed, that is, where the church and Christ's kingdom stand in the world, he will bring us together and keep us by this divine sign of baptism. For if it were without this, and we were to be scattered without outward gathering and signs, Christianity could not be spread out,

nor preserved to the end. But now he wants to bind us together through such divine gathering, so that the gospel will go further and further and through our confession others will also be brought to it." (Church Postil on the Gospel on the Day of Ascension. Erl. XII, 211. 212. [StL 11, 982 f.; Lenker 3, 233-234])

Furthermore, the old Marburg theologian Daniel Arcularius draws from the report that the church at Jerusalem sent Barnabas to Antioch, that he might further the work of the Lord there, the doctrine

"of the union of the churches, by virtue of which the one is obliged to help the other, when the need arises, by teaching, reminding, exhorting, comforting, and similar duties of godliness, according to the example of the Jerusalem and Antioch churches." (Commentar. ad Act. (Commentar. ad Act.)

Chapter III.

On the exercise of the rights and duties of a local Lutheran congregation independent of the state.

A.

First section.

On Congregational Meetings.

§ 12.

For the conscientious, wholesome and God-pleasing exercise of its rights and duties, every free local church needs to establish such orderly public assemblies in which it deliberates, decides or carries out those acts that are necessary for its self-government. Matthew 18:17: "If he does not hear them, tell it to the church. If he does not listen to the church, consider him a pagan and a tax collector." 1 Cor. 5:4: "In the name of our Lord Jesus Christ, in your assembly with my Spirit and with the power of our Lord Jesus Christ." Acts 21:17-22:

"So when we came to Jerusalem, the brethren gladly received us. But the next day Paul went with us to Jacobo, and the elders all came there. And when he had greeted them, he told them one by one what God had done among the Gentiles through his ministry. But when they heard this, they rebuked the Lord and said to him, "Brother, you see how many thousands of Jews there are who have become believers and are all zealous about the law, but they have been reported against you for teaching all the Jews who are among the Gentiles to fall away from Moses and saying that they should not circumcise their children or walk in the same way. What is it then? In all things the multitude must come together, for it will come before them that you have come." Acts 6:2: "Then the twelve called the crowd of disciples together and said: It is not expedient for us to omit the word of God, and to serve tables." Acts 15:30: "When they had finished, they came to Antiochiam and gathered the crowd together and delivered the letter." 1 Timothy 5:20: "Punish them that sin before all, that others also may fear."

Note: Luther also declares that, where a congregation is properly constituted, it should assemble not only for the use of the means of grace, whereby no one is denied access, but also for the common deliberation and management of all its other affairs. He therefore not only writes in his book Conciliis und Kirchen:

"Where we cannot have the councils, the parishes and schools, although small, are nevertheless eternal and useful councils" (XVI, 2767. [StL 16, 2261]),

but also gives the following instruction in his book: "Deutsche Messe und Ordnung des Gottesdienstes" from 1526:

"There are three differences between the divine service and the Mass. *)

________

*) Here Luther understands Mass to mean any orderly public gathering for the use of the means of grace with communal prayer, praise and thanksgiving.

First, a Latin one... Secondly, the German Mass and service, of which we are now speaking; which are to be organized for the sake of the simple-minded laity. But these two ways we must go and let them happen in such a way that they are held publicly in the churches before all the people, among whom are many who do not yet believe or are Christians, but the several part stand there and gape that they also see something new; just as if we were holding worship among the Turks or pagans in an open square or field. For this is not yet an orderly and certain assembly in which Christians could be governed according to the gospel, but a public stimulus to faith and Christianity. But the third way, which should have the right kind of evangelical order, would not have to take place so publicly in the square among all kinds of people, but those who earnestly wanted to be Christians and confess the gospel with hand and mouth would have to register themselves by name and gather in a house alone for prayer, reading, baptizing, receiving the sacrament and practicing other Christian works. In this order one could know, punish, correct, expel or banish those who did not behave in a Christian manner according to the rule of Christ Matthew 18:15 ff. Here one could also impose a common alms on Christians, which one would willingly give and distribute among the poor according to the example of St. Paul 2 Cor. 9:1-2, 12. Here one could also have a short, fine way with baptism and the sacrament and focus everything on the Word and prayer and love. Here one should have a good short catechism on faith, the ten commandments and the Lord's Prayer. *)  Recently, if one had the people and persons who earnestly desired to be Christians, the order and manner would soon be made. But I cannot and do not yet wish to organize or establish such a church or assembly.

_________

*) What Luther says here about the short hymns, the baptismal and communion form and a short catechism was put into practice in the Lutheran church a few years later.

For I have not yet people and persons for it; so I do not see many who urge it. But if it comes to pass that I must do it, and if I am urged to do it, that I cannot refrain from it out of good conscience, then I will gladly do my part and help as best I can. However, I will leave it at the two ways I have mentioned and publicly promote such worship among the people, to train the youth and to call and stimulate others to faith, alongside the sermon, until the Christians who take the Word seriously find themselves and stop themselves, so that it does not become a riot if I want to drive it out of my head. For we Germans are a wild, rough, raging people, with whom it is not easy to do anything, except in the greatest need." (X, 270-272. [StL 10, 228 ff.])

Therefore, when in the same year 1526 a Reformation order for the Lutheran congregations in Hesse was drafted at a synod at Homberg in Hesse, according to which, among other things, such congregational assemblies were also ordered to deal with the affairs of the congregation, Luther made no objection to it, except that it was not yet time for such an order. That Homberg Synod had, among other things, ordered the following:

"That in every parish, after the Word of the Lord has been preached in it for a time, a meeting of the faithful should be held every Sunday either immediately after communion or after the meal, in a suitable place, in which all men who are well disposed to the service of Christ and who belong to the number of the saints should participate, in order to settle together with the bishop everything that has to be negotiated in the parish on the basis of the Word of God." (Philip the Magnanimous's Hessian Church Reformation Order. Published by Credner. Giessen, 1852. p. 76.)

What once prevented Luther from implementing this order, namely the mixed state of the congregations, which would then result in dissension, cannot prevent us here from introducing this "right evangelical order", since "factions" has already occurred here as a result of the permitted religious freedom and can be best controlled by this "right evangelical order" in addition to the preaching of the Gospel.

§ 13.

All adult male members of the congregation have the right to actively participate in speaking, deliberating, voting and deciding in such congregational meetings; women and youth are excluded. Matt. 18:17-18: "If he does not hear them, tell it to the church. If he does not listen to the church, consider him a Gentile and a tax collector. Truly I say to you, whatever you bind on earth shall be bound in heaven, and whatever you loose on earth shall be loosed in heaven." Acts 1:15, 23-26: "And in those days Peter stood up among the disciples and said: (Now the multitude of names was about a hundred and twenty).... And they placed two, Joseph called Barsabas, whose surname was Just, and Matthiam, praying and saying: 'Lord, the herald of all hearts, show which of these two you have chosen, that one may receive this ministry and apostleship from which Judas has departed, that he may go to his own place. And they cast lots for them; and the lot fell on Matthiam, and he was numbered with the eleven apostles." Acts 15:5, 12, 13, 22, 23: "Then some of the Pharisees, who had become believers, came up and said: They must be circumcised, and commanded to keep the law of Moses.... Then the whole multitude held their peace, and listened to Paul and Barnabas, who were telling how great signs and wonders God had done through them among the Gentiles. Afterward, when they had fallen silent, James answered and said: Ye men, brethren, hearken unto me .... And it seemed good to the apostles and elders, with the whole church, to choose out of them men, and to send to Antiochiam with Paul and Barnabas, even Judam, whose surname was Barsabas, and Shilan, which men were teachers

among the brethren. And they gave writing in their hand thus: We, the apostles, and elders, and brethren, wish salvation to the brethren of the Gentiles who are in Antioch, and Syria, and Cilicia." 1 Cor. 5:2: "And ye are puffed up, and have not rather borne affliction, that he who did the work might be done by you." 1 Cor. 6, 2: "Do you not know that the saints will judge the world? If then the world is to be judged by you, are you not good enough to judge lesser things?" 1 Cor. 10:15: "As I speak to the wise, you judge what I say." 1 Cor. 12:7: "In each one the gifts of the Spirit are manifested for the common good." 2 Thess. 3:15: "Yet do not regard him as an enemy, but admonish him as a brother." — 1 Cor. 14:34-35: "Let your wives keep silence in the church: for it shall not be permitted unto them to speak; but they shall be subject, as also saith the law. But if they want to learn something, let them ask their husbands at home. It is evil for women to speak among the church." 1 Pet. 5:5: "Likewise, young men, be subject to the elders. All of you be subject to one another, and hold fast to humility. For God resists the proud, but gives grace to the humble."

Note 1: Johann Gerhard writes:

"From Acts 15:22 it is concluded that not only the apostles but also the presbyters were present at this church assembly, indeed that the whole church, with the apostles and presbyters, had a decisive vote...." .... In the Synod of Nicaea an old man, who was a layman and not very experienced in the art of reasoning and worldly wisdom, judged more against the philosophers than all the other bishops." (Confess. cathol. f. 683.)

Daniel Arcularius writes:

"That in public church matters nothing is to be decided without the vote and consent of the congregation, the apostolic synod teaches us by its example

(Acts 15.), in that, although the apostles and elders, to whom the congregation had delegated the judgment, presided over it, they did not act as rulers and did not take the decision of a public matter to themselves in such a way as to exclude the vote of the congregation. At the same time, one sees here the greatest humility in the people, in that it rests in the weighty judgment of the apostles and elders. This punishes the arrogance of the bishops, who presume to decide church matters and decide what they like without consulting the congregation and often against their will." (Comment. in Acta, ad cap. 15, 22.)

When the apostle Paul says that the incestuous man in the church at Corinth is to be banned in the "assembly" of the same, the Arminian theologian Grotius explains this to mean that he should be judged and banned "by the best" of the Christians, but the famous theologian of our church Calov rejects this interpretation, which departs from the words of Scripture, and writes on that passage 1 Cor. 5:2:

"Grotius here unfoundedly understands not all Christians, but the best. For which were to be thus gathered together, and which were to be considered the best, would have been in the dark." (Bibl. illustr. ad 1. c.)

Concerning the meeting which, according to Acts 15, was held to settle a dispute which had broken out in the Antiochian church, the same Calov:therefore remarks:

"It is said that a great dispute arose there, not only among the apostles, but also among the presbyters (i.e. among the pastors). (i.e. among the pastors), even the whole congregation, or the present assembly, until Peter spoke and Paul and Barnabas told of the miracles of God which had happened among the Gentiles, whereupon the whole multitude was silent. Now after Jacobus had also been heard, who gave his judgment and confirmed it with the Scriptures, it seemed good to send not only the apostles but also the presbyters, and not these alone, but also the whole church, certain men to Antioch with a synodal letter drawn up by all those present in the church assembly, which was written in the name, with the knowledge, agreement and approval of all. So it was

not the apostles alone who passed resolutions in the synod, but the apostles and presbyters with the whole congregation. What the apostles alone thought good about the question raised, or about the election of those who were to be appointed to the mission, or about the synodal letter, but also what the presbyters, what the whole congregation, thought good, was regarded as a decision of the synod. It is not Lucas alone who says that they all thought it good, or that it was thus judged and decided by all — the synodal letter itself testifies to this by beginning: "The apostles, presbyters and brethren", and in the narrative it says: 'It was well with us, unanimously assembled', and v. 28: 'It pleases the Holy Spirit and us', namely all those previously mentioned. Therefore neither the presbyters nor the laity are excluded from the synod and from the signatures, which the papists deny.... Bellarmin confines the great controversy that arose in the assembly to the apostles and presbyters without reason; but it is quite incongruous to infer from the words: 'There arose a great controversy', to conclude that it was raised by the apostles and presbyters alone. Why then? Probably because it is called a great one and was led by men who had a great reputation! But Lucas rather says that it was a much-discussed, versatile, far-reaching one. If, therefore, such conclusions were to be drawn, it was from the multitude of the people; which is at least more certain, since it is said afterwards that the whole multitude was silent and listened attentively to Barnabas and Paul. So they spoke beforehand and at the same time gave their judgment.... Finally, that the apostles, who are introduced speaking after the disputation, would have pronounced the final judgment, and they alone, therefore they would be introduced speaking by Lucas alone, is a completely wrong conclusion, because neither the judgments of those were the final decision, nor are the presbyters or others present among the people excluded from the decision and the decisive judgment of the synod, but included, as has been shown. It is therefore not one and the same thing here: to speak and to pass the final judgment....

Only Jacobus uses the word 'decide', and the decision is attributed to him, not to Petro, not to the others. But will the papists therefore say that the other apostles were excluded from the final judgment? This is more correctly inferred from the decision of the synod, as Lucas mentions it, and from the contents of the synodal letter which made the synodal decision. Namely, not the apostles alone, nor the presbyters only with the apostles, but the apostles and presbyters with the whole church or the rest of the multitude." (Bibl. illustr. ad 1. c.)

Note 2: Luther also testifies in his interpretation of 1 Peter that young people who are not yet of age should not yet speak in public church assemblies:

"St. Peter wants to have such an order in Christianity that the young should follow the old, so that all may go along in humility, the young toward the elder. If this were to happen now, there would not be many laws. He wants it straightforwardly so that the younger ones should be governed according to the understanding of the elders, as they know best, that it may be done to the praise of God. But he thinks, St. Peter, that such old people should be learned and intelligent in the Holy Spirit. For if they themselves are fools and understand nothing, they will not govern well; but if they are wise, it is good for them to govern the young. But St. Peter is not yet speaking here of worldly government, but in general that the elders should govern the younger in spirit, whether priests or other old men." (On 1 Pet. 5, 3. IX, 823. [StL 9, 1104])

§ 14.

The outward leadership of the assembly belongs to those who preside over the church in general or who have to administer the office of outward government in particular. Acts 15:6: "But the apostles and the elders came together to consider these things." 1 Timothy 5:17: "The elders who preside are counted worthy of double honor, especially those who labor in the word and in teaching." Romans 12:8: "If anyone

exhorts, let him wait for exhortation. If anyone gives, let him give with simplicity. If anyone rules, let him rule with diligence." 1 Cor. 12:28: "And God has set in the church first apostles, then prophets, then teachers, then workers of miracles, then gifts of healing, helpers, rulers, various kinds of tongues."

Note: All offices of the church are appointed by God with the one office of preaching, hence the preachers are also called overseers in God's Word. Particularly in larger congregations, however, it is necessary and beneficial to establish auxiliary offices for certain special functions of the preaching ministry and to choose men gifted for this purpose in addition to the preacher to administer them. This happened, for example, in Jerusalem with regard to the care of alms (Acts 6), and we can also see from 1 Timothy 5:17 that in apostolic times there were elders who did not labor in the word, like the preachers, but who nevertheless helped in the work of governing the churches. Where there are no such auxiliary offices, the preacher, by virtue of his office, has the duty to take charge of the outward government of the congregations; but where there are ruling elders, this work can also be entrusted to them. Hence Chemnitz writes:

"Because the church office includes many duties which, when the number of believers is very large, cannot be performed by one or a few, so that everything might be done properly, decently and for edification, when the number of people in the church had multiplied, those duties of the preaching office began to be divided into certain levels of church ministers, so that each one would have a certain specific position in which he would serve the congregation by performing certain duties of the preaching office. Thus in the beginning the apostles were responsible for the ministry of the word and the sacraments and also for the distribution and administration of alms. Later, however, as the number of disciples grew, they entrusted this part of the ministry of preaching, which concerned almsgiving, to others, whom they called deacons, that is, ministers. And the cause for which they do this they give,

that they might continue in the ministry of the word and in prayer without restraint, Acts 6:4. And this first origin of the degrees and ordinances of the preaching office in the apostolic church shows what cause, what manner, what purpose and use should be of such degrees and ordinances; namely, that, according to the constitution of the church, the several offices belonging to the preaching office may be provided more conveniently, properly, diligently, and in order, with a certain dignity, for edification.... But all these degrees the apostles comprehend under the name of presbytery and episcopacy... 1 Tim. 5:17. Paul mentions a twofold class of presbyters, some of whom labored in the word and in doctrine, others were in charge of church discipline, which presbyterate Tertullian also mentions.... But this reminder must be added: 1. that it is not a commandment of God which or how many such degrees or orders there should be; 2. That in the times of the apostles there were not in all churches and always the same and as many degrees or orders, which is to be inferred from the letters of Paul written to various churches; 3. That in the times of the apostles there was not such a distribution of those degrees that one and the same person did not often assume and perform all those offices which belong to the preaching ministry, which is known from apostolic history. Such orders were therefore free in the time of the apostles, and consideration was given to order, propriety and edification; except that at that time certain gifts, such as tongues, prophecy, the apostolate and miracles, were given by God to certain special persons. Those degrees, however, of which we have hitherto spoken, were nothing above and apart from the ministry of the word and the sacraments, but the true functions of the preaching ministry itself were divided into those degrees." (Examen Concil. Trid. II, 13. p. 574-78.)

Of the ruling elders J. Gerhard writes:

"In the apostolic and first church there were two kinds of presbyters, which are called seniors in Latin, as is inferred from 1 Tim. 5:17.

For some administered the teaching office, or, as the apostle speaks there, labored in the word and doctrine, who were called bishops, pastors, etc.. Others, however, were only responsible for the censure of morals and the maintenance of church discipline, since the still pagan authorities did not support the teachers in the church in this matter; these were called rulers and overseers, as is inferred from 1 Cor. 12:28, Rom. 12:8. Ambrose writes at the beginning of 1 Tim. 5:17: 'The synagogue, too, and afterwards the church, had seniors, without whose advice nothing was done in the church, and I do not know through what negligence this was done away with, other than through the indolence or rather the pride of the teachers, in that they alone want to count for something. Both kinds were collectively called rulers, 1 Tim. 5:17, and superiors, Acts 15:22, Heb. 13:7, 17, 24. From both at the same time was gathered that holy college which Paul calls the presbytery, 1 Tim. 4:14.... The presbytery corresponds today to the consistory or church council, in which ecclesiastical and political persons preside and are primarily responsible for church discipline." (Loc. th. de minist. § 232.)

Finally, the old Danish-Lutheran bishop Brochmand writes:

"Saravia and Erastus fiercely argue that a presbytery consisting of ecclesiastical and political persons is not divine, but a human invention. We oppose them with the passage from 1 Timothy 5:17: 'The elders who preside are to be held in double honor, especially those who labor in the word and in teaching,' where the apostle expressly teaches that there are elders of two kinds. Some did their service by teaching, but the others were charged with the care of church discipline. This interpretation is confirmed by Paul himself, who in 1 Cor. 12:28 mentions the rulers, that is, those who did not both teach and govern the church. For they are distinguished from the apostles, prophets and teachers. But Saravia and Erastus are very sour about not being able to overturn the proof taken from the Pauline passage 1 Tim. 5:17, or at least to make it suspect." (System.

univers. th. II, fol. 383.)

Chancellor Pfaff reports:

"Also among our own (the Lutherans), who are in Holland, as well as in Hamburg, the elders, who are laymen, exercise the common rights together with the clergy." (Treatise on the Origin of Canon Law. 1722. p. 289.)

§ 15.

The subjects of discussion and decision-making in such assemblies are matters of doctrine (Acts 15), appointments to church offices (Acts 1:15-26, 6:1-6, 2 Corinthians 8:19)."Not only this, but he is also ordained by the church to be a companion of our journey in this good work, which is done through us in honor of the Lord and [in praise of] your good will"), church discipline (Matt. 18:17-20. 1 Cor. 5:1-5. 2 Cor. 2:6-11. 1 Tim. 5:20.), public offense (Acts 21:20-22.But when they heard this, they praised the Lord and said to him, "Brother, you see how many thousands of Jews there are who have believed, and they are all zealous for the law; but they have been reported against you for teaching all the Jews who are among the Gentiles to fall away from Moses, and saying that they should not circumcise their children, nor walk after the same manner. What is it then? In all things the multitude must come together, for it will come before them that you have come"), disputes among the members (1 Cor. 6:1-8), matters of good order and ceremonies (1 Cor. 14:26-40, 16:1-2), etc.

Thus Hesshusius writes:

"Matt. 18. the Lord Christ does not give to the secular government, but to his church the highest judgment and authority in church matters, among which are almost the most important: the election and appointment of preachers, and the judgment on doctrine, and

the removal of unfaithful teachers. For he expressly says that whoever the church will not hear should be regarded as a banished heathen and publican, which is not only to be understood as meaning that the church has the power to banish impenitent sinners, but also that the church has supreme authority in all church matters, censures, church punishments, doctrinal divisions, and the appointment of pastors." (Von Beruf und Enturlaubung der Prediger. Gießen, 1608. p. 50. 51.)

Thus John Gerhard also writes:

"That not only the prelates and pastors, much less the Roman pope alone, have the right to make ecclesiastical laws, we prove: 1. from the handing over of the keys. To whom the ecclesiastical keys are given by Christ, to him also belongs the right to make ecclesiastical laws. The reason is that the one to whom the keys are given also has everything that belongs to the exercise of the power of the keys. But the keys are given to the whole church. 2. from the administration of the church government (politiac ecclesiasticae) in the times of the apostles. The government of the church in the times of the apostles was so administered that, in addition to the apostles, the other believers were also admitted to the common deliberations concerning the government of the church, Acts 1:16, 6:2. Thus not only the bishops, as the successors of the apostles in office, but also the other believers have the right to make ecclesiastical laws. 3. from the nature of the apostolic council. Of which laws, which concerned ceremonies, in the apostol. Council (Acts 15) still have the right to make ecclesiastical laws. However, ceremonial laws were not prescribed by the apostles alone, but by the whole Church in the Apostolic Council. For at Jerusalem not only the apostles, but also the elders, v. 6. came together, by whom are understood not only the pastors, but also other curators of the church. Both came together for the same purpose, to consider this speech, whether circumcision and other Mosaic customs should still maintain their validity in the New Testament. Yes, the

whole multitude of the believers were present v. 12. and on the basis of a common vote of all, the resolution was passed v. 22: And it seemed good to the apostles and the elders together with the whole church; hence the superscription of the synodal resolution is thus worded v. 23: We, the apostles and elders and brethren, wish salvation to the brethren of the Gentiles who are in Antioch and Syria and Cilicia; which is repeated in v. 25: It hath pleased us well to be gathered together with one accord, and v. 28: For it pleaseth the Holy Ghost and us to lay no more burden upon you than these necessary things. 4. from the practice of the first church." (Confess. cathol. fol. 627. a.)

Dannhauer writes:

"The Church is holy in that the ecclesiastical rights (such as the dispensation of the benefits and sacraments of Christ, the government of the keys, the determination of the ceremonies, etc.) are directly communicated to her and adhere to her. For the church is Christ's bride, the key-bearing housewife and distributor of the spoils, Ps. 68:13, to which alone the right of the church, when the apostles die and the bishops become wolves, can be preserved and propagated without interruption, to which the final judgment is to be transferred, Matt. 18:17, which, in the absence of order, in case of need, can teach, baptize, and absolve (as our Chemnitz holds in the Gospel Harmony, chap. 92, p. 1748), through any members of their community other than those who are made kings and priests by Christ, Rev. 1, 6, and can report the wolf when the Gauls approach and, while the dogs sleep (and become wolves), attack the Capitol. Then even the geese have no right to be mute, and it is, when all is on fire, to wait for no other calling than the general one of love." *) (Christeis. I, 69)

_____________

*) Dannhauer alludes here to the following story: When in the year 390 B.C. the Gauls had taken Rome and now also the Capitolium, a temple with a castle on which a crowd of Romans was still staying, had climbed so quietly in the dark night that neither the guards, who had fallen asleep, nor the dogs noticed it, it was the geese consecrated to Juno, which woke the garrison from their sleep with their cries and thus saved the Capitolium. (S. Liv. hist. V, 47.)

§ 16.

Matters of doctrine and conscience are to be settled with unanimity according to God's Word and the confession of the church (Isaiah 8:20: "Yes, according to the law and the testimony. If they will not say this, they shall not have the dawn"); but indifferent matters, that is, those things which are neither commanded nor forbidden in God's Word, are to be decided by a majority vote according to the principles of love and equity and after prior consultation in Christian order. (1 Cor. 16:14: "Let all your affairs be done in love." 1 Cor. 14:40: "Let everything be done honestly and properly." Col 2:5: "For though I am not here in the flesh, yet I am with you in spirit, rejoicing to see your order and your steadfast faith in Christ.") Should it happen that something is decided and established by the church contrary to God's Word, then such a decision and establishment is null and void and must be declared and revoked.

Note 1. That in a Lutheran congregation matters which are decided in God's Word and testified to in the church confessions cannot be decided by a majority vote, but that every member must submit to the Word of God as the supreme judge and to the confession as the testimony of the orthodox church: our church confessions testify to this when it says:

"We confess ourselves first of all to the prophetic and apostolic writings of the Old and New Testaments, as to the pure and pure fountain of Israel, which alone is the only true guide by which all. Teachers and doctrine are to be judged and judged.... What (but) has hitherto been said of the summa of our Christian doctrine" (in the confessions of the church) "is meant only to mean that one has a unanimous, certain, general

form of doctrine, to which our Protestant churches confess themselves in general, from and according to which, because" (not only: insofar as) "it is taken from God's Word, all other writings, insofar as they are to be tried and accepted, are to be judged and regulated." (Formula of Concord, repetition of the summary concept, etc.)

In the preface to the Book of Concord it is therefore finally stated that the symbolic books were written and accepted so that

"the pure doctrine may be recognized and distinguished from the falsified one, and so that the restless, quarrelsome people who do not want to be bound to any certain form of pure doctrine may not have everything free and open before they can arouse angry disputes to their liking and introduce and defend unrighteous errors."

A congregation that wants to be a Christian congregation, and an evangelical Lutheran one at that, cannot therefore first want to decide by vote whether what has already been decided in God's Word and in the church confessions should apply to it, or even reject it by vote. As soon as a congregation no longer wants to recognize the decisive authority of the symbolic books of the Evangelical-Lutheran church, it ceases to be a Lutheran congregation; but if it does not want to submit even to the clear statements of Holy Scripture, as the Word of the eternal God, then it becomes a school of Satan. Revelation 3:9.

Note 2: Concerning the indifferent things Luther writes:

"So in all other outward ordinances of things, which are in themselves free and not contrary to faith or love, one should have the distinction that one keeps them out of love and freedom to will the others with whom one is, that one rhymes and submits to those. But if they insist that one must and should keep it in obedience as necessary for salvation, then one should leave everything and do the opposite to prove that nothing is necessary for a Christian but faith and love alone; *)

__________

*It can be seen from this that middle things are always presented as necessary for salvation when their due observance is derived from due obedience and not from free love alone. Let this be done by a single preacher or by a whole congregation.

the other is left free to love, according to the demands of society. For keeping such things out of love and freedom does no harm; but keeping them out of necessity and obedience is condemnable. This should also be understood in the ceremonies, singing, prayers and all other church ordinances, as long as one does this out of love and freedom; only for the service and will of society, which is there, one should keep it, where it is otherwise a work in itself is not evil. But if one insists that it must be so, one should immediately desist and act against it in order to preserve the freedom of faith." (Church Postil on the Epistle on the 4th Sunday of Advent. Erlanger Ausg. VII, 114 [StL 12, 87; LW 75, 161-162; Lenker 6, 99])

But whether a parishioner has to hold a middle ground for the sake of love and peace can be seen from the fact that the majority voted for it. — Thus J. Gerhard writes:

"We say that the power to make ecclesiastical laws is to be attributed to the whole church, but not to the superiors and pastors alone. We say that this legislative power given to the church is not an unconditional one, by virtue of which it would be permitted to determine arbitrarily what it pleased, but a limited one, namely one bound to the norm of the divine word and to the welfare of the church. Thirdly, we prove that the ecclesiastical laws do not bind consciences per se, and unconditionally and absolutely: 1. from Christian liberty. Christ has freed us from the yoke of ceremonial laws, and absolved us from the servitude of conscience, which arises from human binding. Acts 15:10. 1 Cor. 7:25. Gal. 5:1 Col. 2, 16:20, where the liberty allowed to Christians in the use of middle things, if only the offense is gone, is affirmed. 2. from Christ's command. Christ expressly forbade the apostles to teach anything except what He Himself commanded, Matt. 28:20; just as God did not will in the OT that anything should be added to or subtracted from His commandments, Deut. 4:2, 12, 32; Proverbs 30:2, and expressly testified

that he did not want him to be served by human commandments, Isaiah 29:13, so that he gave no one power to establish new services or to burden consciences with new laws. 3. from the example of the apostles. They did not arrogate to themselves the power to make new laws and by them to rule consciences 1 Cor. 7:35. 2 Cor. 1:24. 4. from the nature of the lawgiver. God alone is Lord of consciences and alone our lawgiver Isaiah 33:22; James 4:12, as the only one who has the power to save and condemn. He alone has the power to enact laws which directly, immediately and unconditionally bind consciences. (5) From the ultimate end which the true Church is to have in this. The true Church does not command to do or not to do things for the sake of her commandment, but only for the sake of order and decency, that order be kept and offense avoided; so long, therefore, as this is not violated, she leaves consciences free, and does not make them a scruple, nor impose a necessity upon them." (Confess. cathol. f. 626. 627.)

If, according to this, the whole Church cannot demand that one submit to its orders in matters of middle ground for the sake of dutiful obedience, then of course a so-called church government or an individual pastor can do this even less.

§ 17.

To ensure that everything is conducted properly and honestly and that love is not violated, the meeting must be announced publicly in advance and a convenient time must be set for all members wherever possible. However, anyone who does not appear at the meeting forfeits their right to vote. For the sake of love and peace and necessary prudence, it is advisable that important resolutions concerning urgent matters only become valid when they have been confirmed at the meeting immediately following.

Note. This is implied in the Formula of Concord when it states:

"Accordingly we believe, teach and confess that the church of God in every place and at every time, according to the occasion, has good right, authority and power to change, diminish and increase these things without levity and annoyance, in an orderly and proper manner, as is always considered most useful, beneficial and best for good order, Christian discipline and discipline, evangelical prosperity, and for the edification of the church. Paul teaches Romans 14:21 and proves it with his example in Acts 16:3, 21:26, 1 Corinthians 9:19, how even the weak in faith can be yielded and yielded to with a clear conscience in such external matters." (Repetition Art. 10.)

In Luther's order of a common box at Leißnig it says:

"Three times a year, namely on the Sunday after the eighth day of the Three Kings, etc., a whole common parish assembly wants to and should come to the town hall at eleven o'clock and stay there at least until two o'clock in the afternoon. First of all, to publicly read out and listen to this fraternal association of ours, to discuss the administration, income and expenditure of our common treasury and other necessities and comforts from the instruction of our ten appointed leaders with the presentation of their commercial and accounting books and otherwise from all our common concerns, and finally to decide by the grace of God so that this fraternal association may be maintained and not diminished according to the opportunity of the common property and supplies. Even if someone from the common parish could not be present on such three specific days, as no one should be absent without noticeable great cause, nothing less, as touched upon above, should be duly proceeded with by the group." (X, 1173. 74. [StL 10, 974])

§ 18.

The essence of the proceedings should be recorded by an appointed scribe at the

end of each meeting, voted on the accuracy of the account, corrected as necessary and read out again at the beginning of the next meeting. Acts 15:23-31.

§ 19.

The preacher begins and closes the meeting with a prayer; in the event of his absence, a prayer is read out by a designated person. Matt. 18:19: "I tell you further: If two of you become one on earth, for whatever reason you ask, it will be done for you by my Father in heaven." Acts 6:4: "But let us continue in prayer and in the ministry of the word."

Note 1: Mart. Chemnitz writes:

"This office has a power conferred by God 2 Cor. 10:4 ff. and 13:3-4.but one that is circumscribed by certain duties and limits, viz: To preach the word of God, to teach the erring, to reprove the sinning, to exhort the fainthearted, to comfort the fearful, to lift up the weak, to resist the gainsayers, to prove and condemn false doctrine, to punish offenses in morals, to administer the sacraments instituted by God, to forgive and retain sins, to be an example to the flock, to pray for the church, both privately and to recite the public prayers of the church," etc. etc. (Exam. Conc. Trid. fol. 573.)

Balduin writes:

"In public prayers the minister is in praying the mouth of the congregation, in whose name he speaks to God, just as in preaching he is the mouth of God, in whose name he speaks to the people." (Tractat, de casibus conscient. p. 247.)

Luther:

"If then the office of the word is conferred on one, all offices are also conferred on him which are exercised by the word in the church, that is, the power to baptize, to bless, to bind and loose, to pray, and to judge and judge." (Epistle on how to elect church ministers, etc., to the council and congregation of the city of Prague. Hall. A. X, 1862 [StL 10, 1592]).

Note 2: The Saxon General Articles state:

"In affiliate charges, whenever the minister conducts the early morning service in one of the parishes and the people in another parish cannot hear the minister’s sermon, the rural schoolmaster should read the Epis­tles and Gospels of the Sunday with the exposition of Dr. Luther and the Chief Parts of the Catechism without the explanation [of Luther], and he should in addition sing some Christian German hymns." (38)

B.

Second section.

On the exercise of the duty of a church to take care that the Word of God may dwell among it richly and prosper.

§ 20.

The exercise of this duty consists above all in the establishment and maintenance of the public ministry in their midst. Titus 1:5: "For this reason I left you in Crete, that you should complete the work I left you to do, and fill the cities with elders from one place to another, as I commanded you." Eph. 4:11, 14: "And he made some apostles, some prophets, some evangelists, some pastors and teachers, so that we would no longer be children, being weighed and swayed by all kinds of winds of teaching, by the deceitfulness and trickery of men, to deceive us."

Note 1: That it is not at the discretion of a congregation to establish the public preaching office among itself or not, is attested by our church confession when it says:

"The church has God's command that it should call preachers and diaconos." (Apology Art. 13, par. 12)

The Wittenberg Articles of Reformation of 1545, to which Luther also subscribed, further state:

"First, let it be said of the preaching office, that it may be known that we confess (as it is also eternal, unchangeable truth) that the preaching office and ministry of the sacraments

<pages 68>

is necessary and that the church is bound to it, and that no people of God, no elect people, are without the voice of the gospel and the sacraments alone in the assembly. On the other hand, this great good deed is to be recognized and thanked for, that God has commanded the church to choose persons for the ministry of preaching and the service of the sacraments, and wants to be powerful through these persons chosen by the church; awaken much among them and enlighten them with special gifts for the improvement of the churches, as Paul commanded Tito to place and ordain priests in the cities." (See Luther's Werke, Hallische Ausg. XVII, 1442. 43 [StL 17, 1150])

Furthermore, Luther writes in the House Postil:

"Yes, many may well lead out, and say: What more may we do to pastors and preachers, since we can read there ourselves? So they certainly go there and don't read it at home either. Or if they do read it at home, it is not so fruitful, nor so powerful, as the powerful word is through public preaching and the mouth of the preacher, whom God has called and ordained to preach and tell you." (On the Gospel of the 8th Sunday, according to Trin. XIII, 1816-17. [StL 13, 2253])

Here Luther writes about Paul's conversion:

"Here it is especially to be noted that although God speaks with Paul from heaven, he does not want to abolish the preaching office, nor make anyone special, but directs him into the city to the preaching chair or pastor; there he shall hear and learn what is to be learned. God wants us to go and hear the gospel from those who preach it; that is where he should be found and nowhere else. So Paul comes to the knowledge of Christ and the Word through Ananiam. He must have received his light from the same little log of sulphur, which was not a finger to Paul and like a candle to the sun. This is especially to be noted here in this history, that one should learn to hold the preaching office in high esteem. For here it is loud and clear that Paul, the great doctor, gets his understanding through the little doctor, the Ananiam." (On the Lection on the Day of St. Paul's Conversion. XIII, 2528-29. [StL 13, 2654])

§ 21.

[Note: this thesis was expanded on at the Synod 1863. See Part EC5.]

The election and call of a preacher is the first part of the dutiful care for the establishment and maintenance of the public preaching ministry [Predigtamts] in the midst of a congregation. For the proper execution of this highly important act, according to God's Word and with a heartfelt common invocation of God, the congregation, where possible, calls in one or more experienced ministers of the church already in office, uses their advice and, if they can be present, entrusts them with the direction of the public election hearings. Tit. 1:5: “For this cause left I thee in Crete, that thou shouldest set in order the things that are wanting, and ordain elders in every city, as I had appointed thee.” Cf. Acts 1:15-26, 6:1-6, 14:23. Every member of the church who is able to vote is allowed to put forward a candidate, Acts 1:23: “And they appointed two, Joseph called Barsabas, who was surnamed Justus, and Matthias.” The eligibility of each of the nominees is then discussed on the basis of 1 Tim. 3:2-7, Tit. 1:6-9, 2 Tim. 2:15, 24-26, and the one who receives all the votes or at least an absolute majority of the votes from those recognized and nominated as eligible is recognized and accepted as the one called by God through the congregation [Gemeinde]. On the basis of this election, a certificate of election is drawn up, read to the congregation, signed in the name of the congregation by the board or persons chosen for this purpose, and sent to the elected person, 1 Cor. 16:3: "whomsoever ye shall approve by your letters, them will I send." In such a document, the elected minister is to be committed by the congregation to the writings of the apostles and prophets of the Old and New Testaments, as to the Word of God,

as well as to the public confessions of the Evangelical-Lutheran Church and to the faithful execution of the holy preaching ministry in all its aspects, Col. 4:17: "And say to Archippus, Take heed to the ministry which thou hast received in the Lord, that thou fulfil it." (Cf. Acts 15:23. 26:22. 20:20, 21, 26, 27. 2 Tim. 1:13, 14, 8; 4:2, 5. 1 Pet. 5:1-4.) In such a deed of vocation, however, the congregation must at the same time promise that it will recognize the called one as its shepherd, teacher and guardian, receive the word of divine preaching from him as God's Word, obey him, love him, be at peace with him, honor him and provide for his support, on the basis of the following Scriptures: Luke 10:16: " He that heareth you heareth me; and he that despiseth you despiseth me; and he that despiseth me despiseth him that sent me." 1 Thess. 5:12: "And we beseech you, brethren, to know them which labour among you, and are over you in the Lord, and admonish you." 2:13: "For this cause also thank we God without ceasing, because, when ye received the word of God which ye heard of us, ye received it not as the word of men, but as it is in truth, the word of God, which effectually worketh also in you that believe." Heb. 13:17: " Obey them that have the rule over you, and submit yourselves: for they watch for your souls, as they that must give account, that they may do it with joy, and not with grief: for that is unprofitable for you." 1 Thess. 5:13: "And to esteem them very highly in love for their work's sake. And be at peace among yourselves." 1 Timothy 5:17: "The elders who are good leaders are worthy of double honor, especially those who labor in the word and in teaching." Luke 10:7: " And in the same house

remain, eating and drinking such things as they give: for the labourer is worthy of his hire. Go not from house to house." 1 Cor. 9:13-14: "Know ye not that they that sacrifice eat of the sacrifice, and they that keep the altar eat of the altar? So also the Lord has commanded that those who preach the gospel should feed on the gospel." Gal 6:6: "But he who is taught in the word shares all good things with him who teaches him." If the chosen one accepts the call, the congregation presents him, where possible, to orthodox church ministers already in office (Acts 6:6: "They set them before the apostles and prayed, and laid their hands on them"); so that, if this has not yet happened, they may "try" or examine him (1 Tim. 3:10: "And let these be tried first; then let them serve, if they are blameless") and, by public ordination according to apostolic order, declare, commit and confirm him as one duly called (1 Tim. 4:14: "Neglect not the gift that is in thee, which was given thee by prophecy, with the laying on of the hands of the elders"), or else publicly and solemnly induct them into his ministry, Acts 13:2-3: "As they ministered to the Lord, and fasted, the Holy Ghost said, Separate me Barnabas and Saul for the work whereunto I have called them. 3 And when they had fasted and prayed, and laid their hands on them, they sent them away."

Note 1: That the Christian congregations have always elected their preachers with the assistance of ministers already in office is attested by our Confession, when it says in the Smalcald Articles [Treatise13-15:

“13] VII. Again the Council of Nice determined that bishops should be elected by their own churches, in the presence of some neighboring bishop or of several. 14] The same was observed [for a long time, not only in the

East, but] also in the West and in the Latin churches, as Cyprian and Augustine testify. For Cyprian says in his fourth letter to Cornelius: Accordingly, as regards the divine observance and apostolic practice, you must diligently keep and practice what is also observed among us and in almost all the provinces, that for celebrating ordination properly, whatsoever bishops of the same province live nearest should come together with the people for whom a pastor is being appointed, and the bishop should be chosen in the presence of the people, who most fully know the life of each one, which we also have seen done among us at the ordination of our colleague Sabinus, that by the election of the entire brotherhood, and by the judgment of the bishops who had assembled in their presence, the episcopate was conferred and hands laid on him. 15] Cyprian calls this custom a divine tradition and an apostolic observance, and affirms that it is observed in almost all the provinces." (First Appendix.)

Incidentally, as important as it is that the electing congregation should call in those already in office, this is not absolutely necessary for the validity of the election. Luther writes about this:

"No bishop should appoint someone without the election, will and appointment of the congregation, but should confirm the elected and appointed person by the congregation. If he does not do so, he is nevertheless confirmed by the calling of the congregation." (Reason and Cause from Scripture that a Christian Assembly or Congregation has the Right and Power to Judge all Doctrine and to Appoint, Install and Dismiss Teachers. X, 1804.[StLX, 1546])

It is self-evident that in electing congregations, which already have one or more pastors, these must necessarily be among those called, because the whole congregation only calls if they are also called, indeed, because it is precisely because of their pastoral office that they are particularly concerned to provide the congregation with true ministers of the Word. Luther therefore writes to a preacher:

"Accordingly, your office requires that you look up so that the congregation or church does not intrude on you against your will. For that would be just as tyrannical against the pastor as if a pastor wanted to intrude against the will of the congregation and church." (XXI, 448. [?])

Note 2: Since the right to vote belongs to the whole congregation, the election is naturally to be carried out by those who represent the whole congregation. In a congregation independent of the state, however, the most natural representatives of the congregation are all its voting members, who therefore have to carry out this highly important work. Admittedly, this did not happen in most German-Lutheran parishes; the main reason for this originally seems to have been that the parishes did not want to drive the parish estates, which were partly donated by the state, partly by individuals, especially nobles, who therefore claimed the supreme right to call on these parish estates. Therefore, when the Swabian peasants in Luther's time stirred up a revolt and demanded, among other things, the right to vote in twelve articles, Luther wrote:

"A whole congregation should have the power to elect and dismiss a pastor. This article is right, if only it were carried out in a Christian manner (without the chapters, noted in the margin, serving no purpose). Now if the goods of the parish come from the superior and not from the congregation, the congregation may not give the same goods to the one it chooses. For that would be robbed and taken; but if it wants to have a parish priest, it must first humbly ask for one from the superior. If the superiors will not, then let them choose one of their own and nourish him from their own goods, and let the superiors have their goods, or obtain them from them by right." (XVI, 84 [StLXVI, 65])

But Luther complains in many places in his letters that the congregations did not want this. And because the congregations usually did not want to have the independent right of election under this condition, they had to be content with having only the so-called Votum negativum, that is, the right to refuse to accept a proposed preacher, if they could justify this refusal as a just one. Nevertheless, in our German-Lutheran Church the doctrine has continued to be maintained that the right of election is a congregational right and that therefore the election is not valid without the consent of the congregation; and in a not insignificant number of congregations they even retained

the right of election. Fröschel, a former deacon in Wittenberg in Luther's time, testifies of this city as late as 1565:

"Bugenhagen was elected pastor and accepted by the laudable university and by the lords of the council and the whole congregation, as is still the case." (see Unschuld. Nachrr. Jahrgang 1731. p. 695.)

Dannhauer testifies to this of the Strasbourg church. He writes:

"The best vocation is that which flows from the coming together of all three estates, namely the political, the ecclesiastical, and the domestic.... This is in use here in Strasbourg, but not elsewhere, or at least in a few places." (Theol. cas. p. 230. 231.)

Chancellor Pfaff reports of Rostock:

"When a preacher leaves there, some candidates for the preaching office present their gifts of grace and abilities to the people in public sermons. Afterwards, the people gather in the town hall, and in a chamber there are boards posted on the same, and on each board is the name of one of the aforementioned candidates; and when the people pass through this chamber, each one makes a mark on the board on which is written the name of the person to whom he gives his vote. Whoever subsequently has the most votes is elected preacher. All this is done without confusion or noise." (Treatise on the Origin of Canon Law. 1722. p. 302.)

Lassenius also testifies to the same thing when he writes

"And in this way, as you know, the evangelical preachers are still mentioned to this day, but with the difference that in some places it is done by all and everyone in the congregation, in others only by the most distinguished who are in the congregation and those to whom others have given their right, and by the authorities alone, and in some places by the preachers alone, after the congregations in such and such a place have compared themselves with one another, but in such a way that the one called recognizes the whole congregation as his callers." (Eighty-two Short Consolations. St. Louis, Mo., 1861. pp. 331-332. [Der Lutheranerbook review by Sihler])

Löscher reports the following in his so-called "Innocent News": After from the beginning

the Lutheran congregations in Holland, namely those at Amsterdam, had had free universal suffrage, about the year 1683 the Consistory determined that the poor should no longer cast their votes in the election of preachers, preacher-elders, and ministers to the poor! The congregations complained about this. They finally turned to the synod, but even this did not help the congregations to gain their rights! The Lutheran consistory, on the other hand, turned to the ruling Reformed mayors of Amsterdam and issued the decree that anyone who did not pay 20 Dutch guilders a year should not vote with the others. As deeply aggrieved as the congregations felt by this shameful encroachment on their rights, they could do nothing for the time being but present the story of this flagrant injustice to their co-religionists in a public publication, which was done in 1683. In 1689, the congregation in Amsterdam turned to the Lutheran theological faculties in Greifswald, Giessen and Tübingen and asked for their advice and instruction. They all agreed with the theological concerns expressed by the congregation, but warned them not to cause a schism for the sake of this injustice. When the mayors of Amsterdam were informed of this, they rebuked the congregation for having obtained such theological responses, and reduced the 20 guilders required for the right to vote to ten guilders, but declared that otherwise their decision must stand; whereupon in 1691 the congregation published a new pamphlet under the title: "Authentische Belege vom Gewissenszwang", in which they, the congregation, poured out their complaint "especially because of the poor" and expressed their concern that it would become an Arminian Caesaropapism (i.e. an authoritative papal coercion by government), since their consistory itself had finally left religious matters to the secular authorities of another religion. At the same time, the congregation communicates the theological concerns it has raised in its letter. (See Unschuldige Nachrichten 1742. sect. 776-777.)

Note 3: Chemnitz explains in his usual thoroughness how the election of preachers must be carried out according to God's Word in the following words:

"Here the question arises as to who must be those through whose voice this election and calling must take place, so that it can be regarded as divine, that is, that God Himself chooses, calls and sends the laborers into his harvest through these means. Now there are certain and clear examples of this in Scripture. Acts 1:15 ff. Peter, since another was to take Judas' place, presents the matter not only to the apostles, but also to the other "disciples"; for that was the name of the believers at that time, whose number, as many as were gathered together, amounted to 120. And then Peter presents from the Scriptures who and how they should be chosen, and prayers are also associated with this. Although the lot was also cast (because the calling was not to be a completely indirect one, but an apostolic one), the lot was no longer used later. As Acts 6:2 ff. deacons were to be elected and appointed, the apostles did not want to arrogate to themselves the right of election alone, but called the congregation together. They do not, however, entirely dispense with the care of the appointment and do not leave it to the blind and disorderly caprice of the people or the crowd, but take the government of the election or appointment into their own hands; they present the doctrine and rule as to whom and how to elect; then the chosen ones are brought before the apostles, so that it may be confirmed by their judgment whether the election has been carried out lawfully; these, however, confirm the election by laying their hands in prayer to God. According to Acts 14:23, Paul and Barnabas appoint presbyters in individual churches to which they have preached the gospel; but they do not take the right and authority to elect and appoint for themselves alone; but Luke uses the word cheirotonesantes, *) which 2 Cor. 8:19. which is used of the election by the votes of the congregation; the same is taken, namely, from the custom of the Greeks to cast their votes by

____________

*) The word actually means as much as stretching out the hand.

stretching out their hands, and denotes one who by casting a vote elects or indicates his assent. Paul and Barnabas therefore did not impose the presbyters on the church against its will without seeking its consent. And when, according to Acts 15:22, men had to be chosen who were to be sent with commissions to the Antiochian church, Luke says: 'And it seemed good to the apostles and elders together with the whole church to choose men from among them with Paul and Barnabas. It is necessary, however, to note this in apostolic history, that sometimes the ministers and the rest of the church together presented and elected those whom they considered worthy, as Acts 1:23. Sometimes the church had the presentation and election, but the confirmation of the election was subject to the judgment of the apostles, Acts 6:5-6. Often, however, the apostles, who were better able to judge these matters, presented to the congregations those whom they considered qualified for the office, and when the vote and consent of the congregation were added, the appointment was valid. Thus Paul sends Timotheum, Titum, Sylvanum etc. to the churches. Thus presbyters are presented in Acts 14:23, in whose appointment the congregation agrees by vote. Occasionally some have also offered their services to the church themselves, 1 Tim. 3:1: "This is certainly true, if anyone desires the office of bishop, he desires a precious work". In the time of the apostles, however, a legitimate appointment always required the consent of the congregation and the judgment and confirmation of the presbytery. Thus Titus was appointed in Crete to preside over and govern the election of presbyters, so that it might be carried out in the proper manner and that he might confirm and ratify the election by ordination. For Paul uses the same word in Titus 1:5, where he speaks of the appointment of presbyters, as in Acts 14:23, where mention is made both of the vote (by the people) and of the appointment of presbyters." (Examen Concil. Trid. II, loc. 13. p. 586. ff.)

Gerhard writes about this:

"Although
