it hardly seems possible to prescribe a certain rule down to the smallest detail..., nevertheless, if we want to speak of a general model, the examination, ordination and induction belongs to the presbytery [or elders]; the appointment, presentation and confirmation to the Christian authorities; the consent, vote and approval or, depending on the circumstances, the demand to the people. The general principle, therefore, that pastors are elected with the consent of the congregation, and that none are imposed upon them against their will, has express testimony in Scripture, and has been proved by the constant practice of the early church; but the form of election according to its individual parts is different. .... In some churches the people, renouncing their right, leave their part to their authorities and ministers of like confession, and rest without hesitation in their will and election; in some they transfer their right to certain more distinguished persons, who execute everything in the name of the people. In neither of these ways does the right to call preachers cease to belong to the whole church, for the right is surrendered to the fidelity of others, not given up if someone voluntarily renounces his right for a time, nor can this result in a limitation or abrogation of the right." (Loc. de min. § 86. 106.)

Note 4: Concerning the commitment of preachers to the Symbolical Books of the church, it should be noted that this is one of the chief bulwarks of the churches, that preachers cannot make themselves lords over their faith (2 Cor. 1:24:

"Not that we are lords over your faith, but we are helpers of your joy, for you are in the faith"). All false teachers say that they want to teach according to the Holy Scriptures; therefore, if preachers do not want to be committed to the public Confessions of the orthodox church, the congregations have no guarantee that their preachers will not present papist, Calvinist, chiliastic, Methodist, rationalist, etc. doctrines to them, without their being able to teach them according to the Scriptures. If they could do

so, they would be exposed to ever new disputations and disputes even about the articles of the general Christian faith, which would be forever cut off by the commitment to the symbols of the church. Therefore, as dear as a Lutheran congregation is to the pure doctrine of the divine Word, its faith, its Christian freedom, its peace and tranquility, it should be very careful not to accept any preacher who would not commit himself to our holy Concordia. From the beginning, therefore, our Church, following the example of the ancient orthodox Church,  *)  has not accepted as her minister anyone who did not first make a sacred promise to teach according to her Confessions and not to deviate from them either in the doctrines contained therein or in the manner of speech observed therein. The first person in the Lutheran Church to rebel against the connection to the church Confessions was the restless mind and false teacher Andreas Osiander in Königsberg, who declared it to be a tyranny of conscience. He wanted the freedom to teach what he liked, but the church should be his servant. However, Melanchthon replied to him in a speech in 1553 as follows: "Osiander prides himself on having retained freedom and not having tolerated these bonds. In the great licentiousness and anarchy of this time, many take pleasure in this shouting, who take unlimited liberty to invent opinions and, like (the philosopher of doubt) Pyrrho, to cast doubt on everything that has been correctly handed down. But the pious and the wise see not without great pain where this furious rebuke is headed, namely, that the young and the righteous should not even be reminded of modesty. For wild, impudent men, puffed up with self-admiration, cannot be held in check either by such promises or by other restraints. — But first I will speak of the originators of our habit and the intention behind it. This vow was not only recently devised by us, but was introduced by this college about 20 years ago,

__________

*) Which already in apostolic times did not even exchange or accept as a member anyone who did not profess and commit himself to the so-called Apostles’ Creed.

namely by Luther, Jonas and the pastor of this congregation, Dr. Pomeranus. Osiander does great dishonor to these sincere men when he sows the suspicion that they wanted to set up a tyranny, since it is now clear that they had the most honorable cause for their plan. At that time, too, many enthusiastic people roamed about, who soon afterward spread new follies, Anabaptists, Servet, Campanus, Schwenkfeld, etc. And there is no lack of such pests at any time. Therefore, as much as human diligence could prevent it, this faculty wanted to remind good talents of modesty and show them the barriers that could not easily be broken; it also wanted, as much as it could, to restrain the restless minds. This was also the custom of the old church, in which no tyrants ruled and the sources of doctrine were still pure. Signatures were required in godly synods. In the Nicene Synod, not only the bishops but also Emperor Constantine signed the decrees of the same synod with their own hand. Also, no one was admitted to the ministry of the Gospel without prior examination and explicit confession, in which those called to teach declared that they were devoted to the unadulterated teaching of the Gospel and promised not to throw it away." (Corp. Reform. Vol. XII, p. 7.)

Of the oath to the symbols, J. Gerhard also writes:

"The purpose is to promote God's honor and to preserve the peace of the church by drawing to the light those who secretly harbor erroneous opinions and wait for opportunities to spread them in the church. In this way the little foxes are drawn out of their hiding-places, who are out to devastate the vineyard of the Lord, and a bridle is put on the swarming spirits, so that they refrain from stirring up discord, spreading error and confusing the Church for fear of the penalties imposed on perjury, and thus the unity of faith and the Church is diligently preserved." (Loc. de magistr. § 210.)

In another place he writes about this:

"But even by such an oath the symbolic books of Holy Scripture are not put

on an equal footing, nor are cords thrown around the consciences of men, nor is a greater progress in heavenly doctrine prevented, but only a confession is demanded, care is taken for the peace of the church, and the way is closed to secret machinations." (Disputat. theol. p. 963.)

Note 5: Our Confession testifies that ordination is only a confirmation of a previous call, when it says:

"For formerly the people elected pastors and bishops. Then came a bishop, either of that church or a neighboring one, who confirmed the one elected by the laying on of hands; and ordination was nothing else than such a ratification." (Treatise on the Power and Primacy of the Pope. § 70)

Luther also says this. He writes:

"It depends on whether the church and the bishop are one, and whether the church wants to hear the bishop and the bishop wants to teach the church. This is what happened. The laying on of hands, which blesses, confirms and testifies to this, as a notary and witnesses testify to a secular matter, and as the priest who blesses the bride and groom confirms or testifies to their marriage, that they have previously taken each other and publicly confessed; whether the priest is an angel or a devil: because the office is done, the bride is blessed." (Schrift: Exempel einen rechten christlichen Bischof zu weihen, vom J. 1542. XVII, 156 [StL17, 114])

Therefore Balduin writes:

"The office of the Word has a certain commandment and institution, John 20, but of the ordination to the office one reads nothing of the kind; therefore it has always been reckoned among the indifferent things [adiaphora] in the orthodox church." (Comment. in epp. Pauli, ad 1 Tim. 4, 14. q. 3.)

Thus Aegidius Hunnius also judges of the case when one cannot have ordination thus:

"If a body of believers, lacking any pastor, agree to choose a person competent to teach, and in this case of necessity call and choose him to be a preacher, while the cooperation of neither a presbyter nor a bishop is to be had, from whom he could receive ordination, then a minister appointed and chosen by the voice of the church is undoubtedly a true pastor of the church, who has a

legitimate call and can perform all the duties of his office in a proper manner." (Exam. P. I. assert. 6.)

That, however, a congregation should not fail to present its chosen one for ordination, except in cases of extreme necessity, Chemnitz testifies when he writes:

"This also is beyond doubt, that the calling to the ministry of the gospel for the sake of those who run, and are not sent, Jer. 23:21, should have some public testimony and act of testimony of the church; wherefore the apostles manifested and as it were sealed the calling of those who were lawfully chosen to the ministry of the word and sacraments by a public testimony and act of testimony of the church. For the Holy Spirit also wanted Paul, who was directly called, to be proclaimed and presented as the apostle to the Gentiles by a public testimony of the Church. But in this public confirmation, testimony or proclamation, since it was a public act, the apostles used the outward ceremony of the laying on of hands, which was customary among that people at that time, both because of the public designation of the called person and because of the prayers that were made by the whole church over the called person.... Fasting was also added to prayer, Acts 13:3, and this earnest prayer at the ordination of ministers is not in vain, because it is based on divine command and promise. But this is what Paul says: 'The gift that is in you by the laying on of hands.'" (Exam. Conc. Trid. II. loc. 13.)

Finally, what makes ordination necessary and useful, naturally also applies to the induction of one who has already been ordained.

§ 22.

In order that the word of God may be rightly heard in a congregation, it is also necessary to introduce and, although not legal, zealously observe Sunday and customary feast days, as well as certain weekly services, especially Advent and Lent services,

annual days of repentance, harvest, thanksgiving and other holy days (Gal. 4:10-11: "Ye keep days, and months, and feasts, and seasons. I fear for you, lest perhaps I have labored among you in vain." Col 2:16-17: "Let no man therefore make you conscience of meat, or of drink, or of certain solemnities, or of new moons, or of sabbaths, which are the shadow of things to come; but the body itself is in Christ." Luke 11:28: "Blessed are those who hear the word of God and keep it." Acts 2:46: "And they were daily, and always with one accord in the temple, breaking bread to and fro in houses." Heb. 10:24-25: "And let us consider one another with charity and good works, not forsaking the assembling of ourselves together, as the manner of some is; but exhorting one another, and so much the more, as ye see the day approaching." (Acts 2:11: "We hear them speak with our tongues the great works of God") and the holding of public church examinations with the youth. (2 Tim. 3:15: "And because you have known the Scriptures from childhood, they are able to instruct you for salvation through faith in Christ Jesus.")

Note 1: Our Augsburg Confession testifies that the outward observance of Sunday and other appointed days is not to be observed as a divine law with the words:

"The Christian congregation is commanded to keep such order for the sake of love and peace, and to be obedient to the bishops and pastors in these cases, and to keep them in such a way that one does not annoy the other, so that there is no disorder or desolation in the church. But so that consciences are not burdened that they are considered to be such things as are necessary for salvation, and that they are considered to be committing sin if they break them without causing offense to others; just as no one says that a woman

who goes out with her head bare without causing offense to others is committing sin. So is the ordinance of Sunday, of Easter, of Pentecost, and of other such celebrations and practices; for those who think that the ordinance of Sunday is established as necessary for the Sabbath are greatly mistaken, for the Scriptures have abolished the Sabbath. Scripture has abolished the Sabbath, and teaches that all the ceremonies of the old law may be abolished after the opening of the gospel; and yet, because it was necessary to ordain a certain day, that the people might know when they should come together, the Christian Church has ordained Sunday for this purpose, and has been all the more pleased and willing to make this change, that the people might have an example of Christian liberty, that it might be known that neither the keeping of the Sabbath nor of any other day is necessary," etc. etc. (Art. 28, pars. 55-60. Cf. Art. 15.)

Note 2: Balthasar Meisner writes about the necessity and salutary nature of celebrating feasts and the like:

"There is evidently no more convenient way in which the less instructed can be taught and admonished about the principal things of faith and religion, about the most noble benefits which God has shown in the New Testament, and about the necessary gratitude for them, than when, as Basil writes, those things which could not be learned every day are received together in communal celebration. For here, according to Jerome's testimony, faith in Christ grows from the mutual sight and conversation, love becomes more ardent and expresses itself more, and joy arises and increases in the hearts of the faithful. People today are not so fervently zealous in religion that they gather daily in crowds; for some are busy looking for their daily food, others are occupied with their professional work, most are drawn away by indolence, security, carelessness, even disgust; so that it is necessary that certain times should be sanctified or set apart, in which no other work or business should be done than to come together for a holy assembly, for holy works of worship, and for public service. ... After the Lord's Day we also count other feasts,

which for the sake of history and for the sake of order and propriety are celebrated every year and maintained without interruption of Christian freedom, and of which it is believed that they can also be celebrated further for the great benefit of the people; so that on them, namely, everything that is described by our Savior and Redeemer above other remarkable things in the Holy Scriptures can be explained more precisely, executed more completely and, with proof of use, laid before the eyes of the people and presented.... It is true that we are obliged to devote our whole lifetime to the contemplation and ever new consideration of these divine works; but I do not want to say how morose and indolent we usually prove to be in this, only that it is absolutely necessary that in solemn assembly a more solemn remembrance and an annual living visualization of the aforementioned good deeds should also take place, if they are not to gradually all disappear from our hearts and finally be completely lost." (Colleg. adiaphorist. disp. H. § 29.30.42.49.)

Note 3: Concerning the church examinations, the Apology of the Augsburg Confession states:

"With us the canons are held that the pastors and church ministers publicly instruct children and youth in the Word of God. Confession: "With us the canons are held that the pastors and church ministers instruct the children and youth in the Word of God publicly and at home." (Art. 15.)

Furthermore, Luther writes in the preface to the Small Catechism:

"Therefore, for God's sake, I beseech you all, my dear sirs and brethren, who are pastors or preachers, to accept your office with all your hearts, to have mercy on your people, whom you are commanded to teach, and to help us to bring the Catechism to the people, especially to the young people, and who are not better able to take these tables and form for themselves and teach it to the people from word to word,... so that they can recite it and learn it by heart. But those who do not want to learn that it is said to them how they deny Christ and are not Christians, should not be admitted to the sacrament, should not raise a child from baptism, should not use any part of Christian freedom, but should be badly sent home to the pope and his officials, as well as to the devil himself. To this end, parents and landlords should deny them food and drink. ... For although no one should or can be forced

<pages 86>

to believe, the flock should nevertheless be kept and driven so that they know what is right and wrong with those with whom they live....",

§ 23.

In order that the word of God may be rightly heard in a congregation, it is also necessary that the congregation should have its newborn children baptized without delay by its preacher, as the steward of God's mysteries (1 Cor. 4:1: "Therefore let every man esteem us to be the ministers and stewards of God's mysteries." Mark 10:13 ff. "And they brought little children to him, that he might touch them" etc.); that they might let him prepare their youth for the full enjoyment of the means of grace and confirm them publicly in his time (Matt. 21:15-16.: "But when the chief priests and scribes saw the miracles which he did, and the children crying in the temple, and saying, Hosanna to the Son of David! they were indignant, and said unto him, Hearest thou also what these say? Jesus said to them: Yes; have you never read? Out of the mouths of babes and sucklings hast thou prepared praise?"); that they might often desire and receive from him the consolation of absolution and the sacrament of the body and blood of Jesus Christ (John 20:23: "Whose soever sins ye remit, they are remitted unto them; and whose soever sins ye retain, they are retained."). 2 Cor 2:10: "But to whom you forgive anything, I also forgive. For I also, if I forgive anyone anything, forgive it for your sake in Christ's stead." 1 Cor. 11:20, 26: "So when you come together, the Lord's Supper is not held there .... For as often as you eat of this bread and drink of this cup,

you shall proclaim the Lord's death until he comes"), and that after a previous interrogation and confession (heb. 13:17: "They watch over your souls as those who are to give an account of them." Cf. 1 Cor. 4:1. Matt. 7:6.: "You shall not give the sanctuary to the dogs, nor cast your pearls before swine, lest they trample them under their feet and turn and tear you apart"); that she may have him publicly consecrate her betrothed for marriage with God's word and prayer (1 Cor. 7:39: "But if her husband falls asleep, she is free to marry whom she wishes; only that it be done in the Lord." 1 Timothy 4:3-5: "And forbidding to marry, and to abstain from meats, which God hath created to be taken with thanksgiving of them which believe, and of them which know the truth. For all God's creatures are good, and nothing that is received with thanksgiving is detestable. For it is sanctified by the word of God and prayer"); that in sickness, death and other afflictions and trials they may also be instructed and comforted by him from God's word (James 5:14-15: "If anyone is sick, let him call to him the elders of the church, and let them pray over him, anointing him with oil in the name of the Lord. And the prayer of faith will help the sick person, and the Lord will raise him up; and if he has committed sins, they will be forgiven him"), and finally, that they should bury their dead in a Christian manner (Acts 8:2: "Now men who feared God sent for Stephen, and made a great lamentation over him").

Note 1: In a letter to Piscator, Luther speaks out against the delay in baptizing children:

"In the fourth place, one must, as much as possible, be opposed to the delay in baptism, so that this habit may not be made a rule

to abstain from baptism constantly. Thus in Italy, in many towns, a large proportion of the people remain without baptism as long as they live, because they are not baptized in childhood. When they have grown up, they despise everything and die as those who do not believe in God." (XXI, 1339. [StL 21, 3033 f.])

Gerhard writes of this:

"Since baptism is the ordinary means of rebirth, John 3:5, and the holy sacrament of reception, therefore the laudable custom is observed in our churches that the parents bring the little ones to baptism immediately on the first, or second, or third day after birth, or even immediately without delay after they are born, to have them baptized, namely when danger to life is feared for the sake of weakness. But those who have been negligent in this matter and postpone baptism for the sake of splendid preparation, sink themselves into the most severe temptations if the children die without baptism." (Loc. de bapt. § 246.)

Note 2. Concerning Confirmation, Chemnitz writes:

"Our own have often shown that the use of confirmation, after the abolition of useless, superstitious, and Scripturally conflicting ordinances, may be performed godly and for the edification of the church in harmony with Scripture in the following manner, that those, who have been baptized in infancy (for such is now the state of the church), when they have reached the years of discernment, be diligently instructed in a definite and simple catechism of the doctrine of the church, and when it is seen that they have fairly grasped the rudiments, be thereafter presented to the bishop and the church; and that the boy who was baptized in early childhood would first be briefly and simply reminded of his baptism, how, why and for what final purpose he was baptized, what the whole Trinity had conferred and sealed upon him in that baptism, namely the covenant of peace and grace; how the renunciation of the devil, the confession of faith and the vow of obedience had taken place. Secondly, that the boy himself should make his own and public profession of this doctrine and faith before the Church. Thirdly, that when asked about the noblest essentials of the Christian

religion, he would answer each one, or, if he did not understand something, would be better instructed. Fourthly, that he would be admonished, and by this his confession would show that he was far from all pagan, heretical, fanatical, and godless opinions. Fifth, that an earnest exhortation from God's Word be added, that he may persevere in the baptismal covenant and in that doctrine and faith, increase and gradually be strengthened. Sixthly, that a public prayer be made for those boys, that God, through his Holy Spirit, would be worthy to govern, preserve and strengthen them in this confession. In which prayer the laying on of hands could be used without superstition. Nor would such prayer be in vain, for it is based on the promises concerning the gift of constancy and the grace of strengthening. Such a use of confirmation would be of great benefit for the edification of youth and the whole Church; it would also be consistent both with Scripture and with the purer antiquity. For that in the apostolic laying on of hands there was an interrogation of doctrine and a confession of faith, is evidently attested by Acts 19. Also of the exhortation to constancy and of confirmation by the word in the doctrine and faith once received, there are examples of the apostolic church, Acts 14, 15, and 18. And that public prayer was used, is attested by Acts 8." (Exam. Conc. Trid. II. de confirm. fol. 258. 259.)

Note 3: The fact that in a true Lutheran congregation those who wish to partake of Holy Communion must first register and undergo a Christian interrogation is attested to by the Augsburg Confession. Confession, when it says:

"This custom is kept with us, not to administer the sacrament to those who have not first been interrogated and absolved."' (Art. 25.)

Furthermore, the Apology states:

"With us, however, the people need the Holy Sacrament willingly, without being urged, all Sundays, who are first interrogated as to whether they are instructed in Christian doctrine, know or understand anything in the Lord's Prayer, in faith, in the ten commandments." (Art. 15. [par. 42 [German text], Triglot Concordia, p. 326])

But Luther adds the warning: the

pastor

"should not torture the ignorant with long trials if they want to go to Holy Communion, but should not leave them completely untried or uninterrogated." (Letter to Balth. Thüringen, pastor in Coburg. XXI, 1348.)

Luther writes about this in detail in his "Weise, christliche Messe zu halten" etc. from the year 1523:

"In this one should have the same manner and order as is observed at baptism, namely that first of all the bishop or pastor should be informed who those are who wish to receive the sacrament, and they themselves should ask him to administer the holy sacrament to them, so that he may know their names and what kind of life they lead. After that, even if they ask for it, he should not admit them, for they have given an answer to their faith, and in particular have given an account of this question: Whether they understand what the sacrament is, what it is for and what it gives, and what they want to use it for, namely, whether they can say the words of the sacrament by heart; and to show that they go to the Lord's table for this reason, that they, afflicted by sin with a weighed down conscience or fear of death or some other temptation of the flesh, the world or the devil, hunger and thirst for the word of grace and salvation, to be received from the Lord Himself through the ministry of the minister, so that they may be comforted and strengthened; just as Christ gave and instituted such things out of unspeakable love in this Lord's Supper with these words: Take and eat, etc. But I deem it sufficient that he who desires the sacrament should be asked and examined in this way once a year; indeed, he should be so sensible that he should be asked only once in his life, or not at all. For with this order we want to prevent the worthy and the unworthy from coming to the Lord's table at the same time, as we have seen so far under the papacy, where nothing else was sought except to receive the sacrament alone. But there has been neither talk nor thought of faith, consolation and the right use and benefit of the sacrament; indeed, they have also concealed the words of the sacrament, namely the bread of life, with great diligence, indeed they have dealt with it with the utmost absurdity, that you who received the sacrament were doing a work that was good because of your own worthiness,

not that you received and strengthened faith through Christ's goodness. But we wish to exclude and separate from the communion of this sacrament those who do not know how to respond to the above-mentioned pieces, as those who lack the wedding garment. Afterwards, if the pastor or bishop sees that they understand all this, he should also take care to see whether they prove their faith and understanding with their life and conduct (for Satan also understands all this and can speak of it); that is, if he sees a fornicator, adulterer, drunkard, gambler, usurer, adulterer, or otherwise notorious for other public vices, he should certainly exclude him from communion, for he proves by sinful display that he has changed and reformed his life. But for others who occasionally fall and return, and are sorry that they have fallen, not only should the sacrament not be denied, but it should be known that it is instituted precisely for their sake, that they may be refreshed and strengthened thereby. For we are all lacking in many ways, James 3:2, Galatians 6:2; for I speak here of the despisers, who sin brazenly without fear, and yet boast nothing less than great things of the gospel." (X, 2764-66. [StL 10, 2247 f.; AE 53, 32 ff.])

Note 4: It is true that the Augsburg Confession expressly says Confession expressly states

"that confession is not commanded by Scripture, but instituted by the Church." (Art. 25.)

Hence we are told that private confession was not introduced in all Lutheran congregations, which were nevertheless recognized as orthodox. Thus, for example, the Wittenberg theological faculty wrote in 1619:

"Although we do not deny that such private confession is not used in all orthodox churches, since nevertheless there is also forgiveness of sins and worthy use of Holy Communion; therefore the confessional has not been arranged for either of these ends." (Consil. Witeberg. II, 139.)

Furthermore, Balduin writes:

"If in those churches where private confession is in use, it can be omitted by someone under certain circumstances, rather in those places where it is not in use and yet the doctrine of the Lord's Supper is kept pure (as happens in some places in Upper Germany),

it is omitted and yet Holy Communion is received salutarily. The customs, which are intermediate things, can take nothing away from the essence of the sacrament." (Tract. de eas. consc. p. 467. 468.)

Finally, the Leipzig theologian Hieronymus Kromayer writes:

"Although this (private confession) has no divine commandment and has been abolished by many orthodox churches, e.g. the Strasbourg, *) Swedish and others, as a middle thing, it nevertheless has examples in Holy Scripture and its use is not to be despised.... The fact that in our churches no one is admitted to Holy Communion unless he has confessed his sins is due to the church's institutions. However, they do not condemn other churches which do not have this private confession." (Th. posit.-pol. p. 584. 585.)

However, in the Augsburg Confession the confession is made:

"Confession is thus taught that one should receive absolution in private in the churches and not drop it" (Art. 11.);

and in the Apology it is added by way of explanation:

"We also retain confession for the sake of absolution, which is God's word, by which the power of the keys absolves us from sin. Therefore it would be against God to remove absolution from the church, etc. Those who despise absolution do not know what forgiveness of sin is or what the power of the keys is." (Art. 12.)

How highly Luther therefore held private confession, he indicates in the words:

"If a thousand and a thousand worlds were mine, I would rather lose it all, for I would let the least bit of this confession come from the church. Yes, I would rather have the papacy's tyranny of fasting, celebrations, dresses, places, plates, caps, and whatever I could wear without dishonoring the faith, than that confession should be taken from the Christians. For it is the first, most necessary and most useful school for Christians, in which they learn to understand and practise God's

__________

* Dannhauer writes: "In the articles of the Augsb. Confession, private absolution is established, and yet Christian freedom is also permitted. Thus in the church of St. Nicolai in Strasbourg, for the sake of Marbach, to whom Luther himself belonged, private absolution was retained and is still retained today." (Th. casual. p- 99).

Word and their faith; which they do not do so powerfully in public lectures and sermons." (Warning to the Frankfurt congregation of 1533. See Luther's Volksbibliothek IV, p. 61.)

Therefore, although private confession does not belong to the nature of a Lutheran congregation, the lack of it is no small shortcoming.

Note 5. Concerning the diligent use of Holy Communion, Luther writes in the preface to his Small Catechism:

"We are not to compel anyone to believe or to partake of the sacrament, nor to agree on any law, time, or place, but to preach in such a way that they force themselves without our law and compel us pastors to administer the sacrament: Whoever does not seek or desire the sacrament at least once or four" (that is, about four times, "once four") "of the year, it is to be feared that he despises the sacrament and is not a Christian, just as he is not a Christian who does not believe or hear the gospel; for Christ did not say: Leave these things alone or despise them. But: Do these things as often as you drink them, etc."

Note 6: J. Gerhard proves that the ecclesiastical consecration of engaged couples is rightly maintained for the following reasons:

"1. for the sake of the example which God himself has given us, Gen. 1:27, 2:22. 2. For the sake of the apostolic commandment, both for the sake of the general commandment that everything in the church should be done properly and honestly, 1 Cor. 14:28, that whatever we do in word or deed we should do in the name of the Lord Jesus, giving thanks to God and the Father through him, Col. 3:17.that all God's creatures should be sanctified through the word of God and prayer, 1 Tim. 4:5; that we should think on all things that are chaste and profitable, Phil. 4:8; as also for the sake of the special commandment that marriage should be in the Lord, 1 Cor. 7:39, and that marriage should be honorable to us, Heb. 13:4. 3. for the sake of instructing the new spouses, that they may be reminded of the dignity of the state into which they are entering, and of the duties which one party owes to the other according to the standard of the Word. 4. for their benefit, that in this way

their marriage may be confirmed in the sight of God, the angels and the Church, and that they themselves may be consecrated to God by prayer and may hope for divine blessing in their marriage. 5. for the sake of the ancient institution of this ecclesiastical consecration. For the history of the Church of all times shows that it has always been customary to sanctify marriage by word and prayer. The marriage from which the future members of the Church are hoped for is rightly entered into with the Church present as a witness. 6. for the sake of the consent of the Gentiles themselves. The pagans have recognized to some extent from the light of nature that marriage is not a merely human, but a divine foundation, therefore they held that it was to be consecrated with sacred ordinances." (Loc. de conjug. § 462.)

Note 7: Concerning the duty of the preacher to visit the sick and dying, the pious Scriver writes:

"Another part of the pious preparation for the blessed end, which takes place in the sickbed, is that one should at times allow his pastors to come to him and make use of their ministry in a salutary way. It cannot be denied that the most comforting and salutary things that a Christian needs to know in times of distress sometimes escape him either because of his simplicity or because of the present anxiety and confusion of mind; therefore it is highly necessary and useful to have the ministers of the Word and of the Church at hand, so that they, as faithful caretakers of souls, may teach, instruct, remind, strengthen and comfort the sick and dying. Preachers are called by the holy apostles God's helpers or co-workers in his spiritual field and garden work, some of whom plant, others water; but when is watering more necessary than at the time of greatest heat, when the plants want to wither and wither, so that at least the heart's vault may be preserved and maintained?... The sacred ministry of preaching, as is known and has been explained elsewhere, also has its glorious promises from our God that he will give prosperity for its planting and watering.... I do not want to call unhappy those who, in the absence of a preacher, in the presence of godly Christians, under their sighs and prayers, or even without courage and when they are alone, press away; but one knows that the means ordained by God

must not be despised if one can get hold of them." (Treasure of Souls V, 6. col. 953. 955.)

Note 8. The old Rostock theologian David Chyträus writes about Christian funerals:

"From the beginning, the Church of God has always used honest and Christian ceremonies at funerals, and has decreed that, when the corpses are buried, respectful assemblies of God-fearing people are held, which are not vain and frivolous spectacles, as the pagans have done in the past, nor do they only show outward signs of mourning and love for the deceased; but that pious, God-fearing Christians reminded themselves and others of these highly important, necessary, serious things at the burial of the dead: 1. How frail, short and miserable our and all people's temporal life is.... 2. of the great cause of death.... 3. of the redemption from sin and death through our one Savior Jesus Christum.... 4. of the burial and resurrection of Christ, who lay in the grave the whole Sabbath, and rose again the third day, that he might raise our dead and buried bodies out of death and dust, out of the earth into the Sabbath of eternal life, joy and glory.... 5. of the last judgment, wherein after this bodily death and resurrection every man shall give account of his whole life. 6. that the Christians, with godly songs of the resurrection and eternal life and other Christian ceremonies, show that they make a distinction between godly men and unreasonable cattle. 7. that those who follow with us to the burial, in consideration of the foregoing causes, with Christian earnest prayer, faithfully commit to our Lord God all the afflictions of all Christendom and each one of his own whole life. For God wants us to confess that this temporal life is also God's gift, and that we should pray for the preservation and prolongation of this temporal life, so that we may learn rightly and thoroughly the doctrine of God and our Lord Jesus Christ, which knowledge must be begun in this life, and turn to others according to our calling.... For the sake of these many highly important causes, the burial of the dead in this church should also

<pages 96>

be held quite honestly with godly, Christian and devout hymns, lectionaries, funeral sermons and other useful ceremonies. Those who have fallen asleep in true confession and invocation of our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ and have previously received the holy absolution and sacrament of the body and blood of Christ, should be carried to the funeral with an honest procession, in which the deceased's friends, neighbors, church servants and students accompany them, and confirmed to the earth in the churchyard or cemetery." (See Christian Kortholten theologische Tractätlein. Kiel, 1704. pp. 286-290.)

§ 24.

In order for the Word of God to be properly practiced in a congregation, it is also necessary that the congregation, where possible, establish an evangelical-Lutheran children's school, appointing and replacing for this purpose an orthodox, godly and skillful teacher in Christian order (Matthew 18:10: "See to it that you do not despise one of these little ones."). Ephesians 6:4: "And you fathers, do not provoke your children to anger, but bring them up in discipline and admonition to the Lord." Gen 18:19: "For I know that he will command his children and his household after him to keep the ways of the Lord and to do what is right and good, so that the Lord will bring upon Abraham what he has promised him." Deut. 6:6-7: "These words which I command you today you shall take to heart. And you shall sharpen them for your children and speak of them when you sit in your house." 2 Timothy 3:15: "Because you have known the Scriptures from childhood, they are able to instruct you for salvation through faith in Christ Jesus." (Rom. 2:20: "A teacher of the simple"), committing this teacher to the Word of God of the Old and New Testaments and the confessions of the Lutheran Church,

and finally submitting him to the supervision of the public preaching ministry. (1 John 2:13: "I write to you fathers, because you know him who is from the beginning. I write to you young men, because you have overcome the evil one. I write to you children, because you know the Father." Heb. 13:17: "Obey your teachers and follow them, for they watch over your souls as those who are to give an account of them." Acts 20:28: "Take heed therefore unto yourselves, and to all the flock, among whom the Holy Ghost hath made you overseers, to feed the church of God, which he hath purchased with his own blood.")

Note 1: Luther gave two powerful testimonies about the duty of Christian congregations to establish Christian children's schools, among other things, in two special writings, which can be found in the fourth volume of "Luther's Volksbibliothek": 1) An die Rathsherrn aller Städte deutschen Landes, dass sie christliche Schulen aufrichten und halten sollen. From the year 1524. 2) Sermon that children should be kept at school. In the first of these, he writes:

"If he (the devil) is to be harmed, if he bites, it must be through the young people who grow up in the knowledge of God and spread God's Word and teach others .... Therefore I beg you all, my dear sirs and friends, for God's sake and for the sake of the poor youth, that you will not hold this matter in such low esteem as many do who do not see what the prince of the world thinks.... Dear Sirs, must so much be spent yearly on arbors, roads, bridges, dams, and such innumerable things, that a city may have peace and comfort in time; why should not so much be spent on the poor, poor youth, that a skillful man or two may be kept as schoolmasters?... The third (reason) is probably the highest, namely God's commandment, who through Moses so often drives and demands that parents should teach their children; as the 78th Psalm also says: How has he so highly commanded our fathers to make known to the children and to teach

<pages 98>

children's children! And this is also shown by the fourth commandment of God, where he so highly commands the obedience of parents to their children, that disobedient children are to be put to death by judgment, Deut. 21:21. And why do we old people live differently, except that we wait, teach and bring up the young people? It is not possible that the foolish people should teach and keep themselves; therefore God has commanded us, who are old and experienced, what is good for them, and will exact a heavy reckoning from us for them. Therefore Moses also commands (Deut. 32:7), saying: "Ask your father, and he will tell you; the elders will show you. Although it is a sin and a shame that we have come to this, that we should first of all provoke and be provoked to draw our children and young people, and consider their best interests, yet nature itself should drive us to do the same, and the examples of the Gentiles also show us manifold. There is no unreasonable animal that does not wait for its young and teach them what they deserve, except the ostrich, of which God says (Job 39:17) that it is as hard on its young as if they were not its own, and leaves its eggs on the ground. And what is the use of our having and doing anything else, and of being vain saints, if we leave under way that for which we live most of the time, namely, the care of the young? I also consider that among the outward sins the world is not so heavily burdened before God and deserves such terrible punishment as the one we commit against our children by not drawing them.... Yes, you say, all this is said to the parents; what business is it of the rulers and authorities?" (Here in America, where the congregations stand in the place of the Lutheran authorities in this regard, someone might say: What business is it of the congregations? Luther therefore continues:) "Is rightly spoken; yes, but what if the parents do not do this? who should do it? Should it therefore be left undone, and the children neglected? Where should the authorities and the council excuse themselves that they should not do this? There are many reasons why parents do not do this. In the first place, some are not so pious and honest that they would do it even if they could; but like ostriches they harden themselves against their young, and leave it at that,

that they have thrown away their eggs and begotten children; they do no more. Well, these children shall nevertheless live among us and with us in the common city. How, then, will reason and especially Christian love suffer them to grow up uneducated, and to be poison and dross to other children, so that in the end a whole city is ruined? as happened to Sodom and Gomorrah and Gaba and several other cities. On the other hand, the majority of parents are unfortunately unskilled and do not know how to raise and teach children. For they themselves have learned nothing without taking care of the belly; and they especially include people who should teach and raise children well and correctly. Thirdly, although the parents would be skillful and would like to do it themselves, they have neither time nor space to do so because of other business and household duties, so that necessity forces them to keep common tutors for the children. Unless each man wanted to keep one for himself. But this would be too difficult for the common man, and many a fine boy would be neglected for the sake of poverty. Moreover, many parents die, leaving orphans behind them; and how they are cared for by guardians, if experience were too little for us, should show us that God calls Himself the Father of the fatherless (Ps. 68:6), as of those who are otherwise abandoned by everyone else. There are also some who have no children; they do not care for them. That is why it is "due to the council and the authorities" (in America the church) "to take the greatest care and diligence for the young people." (Luther's Volksbibl. IV, 71-78. [StL 10, 458 ff.; AE 45, 447 ff.])

Note 2: Luther indicates that the office of school teacher, insofar as it is driven by God's Word, is also an ecclesiastical office and that therefore a school teacher must also be committed to God's Word and the church's confession when he writes in his treatise Councils and Churches:

"Would you perhaps like to say here: What do you ultimately want to make of the conciliar churches if you want to restrict them so precisely? In this way a pastor, even a pedagogus (a schoolmaster) — will the parents be silent — would have more power over his pupils than a concilium over the church?

I answer: Do you also think that a pastor or schoolmaster are such minor offices that they cannot be compared to the conciliar? If there were no pastors and bishops, where would a concilium be gathered? If there were no schools, where would one take pastors? I am talking about such schoolmasters who not only teach children and youth the arts, but also draw them to Christian doctrine and faithfully educate them.... A council, as a great judge, must make old great peels pious or kill them (spiritually), but cannot beget others. A parish priest and schoolmaster have to deal with small young peasants and always produce new people as bishops and councils where necessary. A concilium cuts down the big branches of the trees or even cuts out the bad trees. But a pastor and schoolmaster plant and grow young trees and spice bushes in the garden. Oh, they have a precious office and work and are the noblest jewels of the churches; they preserve the churches. Therefore, all lords should do their best to preserve pastors and schools.... The ceremonies should even be left from the councils at home in the parishes, even in the schools, so that the schoolmaster would be magister ceremoniarum (master of ceremonies) next to the parish priest. For the others all learn it from the pupils, without any essays or effort. So what, when and how the pupils sing or pray in church, the crowd learns it afterwards, and what they sing over the corpse or at the grave, the others learn it too; when they kneel down and fold their hands, the schoolmaster knocks with the stick while singing: Et homo factus est (and has become man), the crowd does the same afterwards; when they take off their little hats or bow their knees, as often as the name of Jesus Christ is mentioned, and what they practise of the same Christian discipline and gifts more, the crowd does the same afterwards without preaching, as if moved by living examples." (XVI, 2763-70. [StL 16, 2258-2263])

Note 3: That the schoolmasters are to be subject to the supervision of the preaching office, Luther testifies in the "Ordnung eines gemeinen Kastens", which he made to the congregation of Leißnig, in which it says, among other things:

"Our pastors, preachers and the ten rectors should take care of the school office and the government of the

youth with careless, faithful attention, and on all Sundays, for this reason, keep a careful watch and advice and handle it with increased seriousness." (X, 1168. [StL 10, 970])

§ 25.

Finally, for the Word of God to be rightly heard in a church, the church must not tolerate division through conventicles, that is, teaching or prayer meetings led by unappointed persons outside the public preaching ministry ordered by God. (1 Cor. 11:18: "When you come together in the church, I hear that there are divisions among you." James 3:1: "Brethren, do not suffer anyone to be a teacher, knowing that we will receive all the more judgment." 1 Cor. 12:29: "Are they all apostles? are they all prophets? are they all teachers?" 1 Cor. 14:28: "But if he is not an interpreter, let him keep silent among the church; but speak to him yourself and God." Acts 6:4: "But let us continue in prayer and in the ministry of the word." Romans 10:15: "But how shall they preach if they are not sent?")

Note: Hence it is stated in our basic church confession:

"It is taught by the church government that no one should publicly teach or preach or administer the sacrament in the church without a proper calling." (Augsburg Conf. Article 14.)

Thus Luther writes:

"But that the apostles also first went into other people's houses and preached, they had a command, and were called and sent to preach in all places, as Christ said in Mark 16:15: Go into all the world and preach to every creature. But after this, no one has such a common apostolic command, but every bishop or pastor has his particular parish, which St. Peter 1 Pet. 5:3. also calls the clergy, that is, part, that each one is commanded his part of the people, as St. Paul to Titus

also writes; in which no other or stranger without his knowledge and will is to submit to teach his parishioners, neither secretly nor publicly, and no one is to listen to him in body or soul, but to announce and report to his pastor or authority. And this should be firmly adhered to, so that no preacher, however pious or righteous he may be, should preach or teach secretly to a papist or heretical pastor's people without the knowledge and will of that pastor. For he is not commanded to do so. But that which is not commanded should be left alone; we have enough to do if we want to carry out what is commanded. Nor does it help them to pretend: All Christians are priests. It is true that all Christians are priests, but they are not all pastors. For in addition to being a Christian and a priest, he must also have an office and a commanded parish. Profession and order make pastors and preachers. Just as a citizen or layman may well be learned, but is not therefore a doctor to read publicly in the schools, or to submit to such an office, if he is called to it. This is what I have had to report about the sneak preachers and preachers of false preaching, of whom there are now too many, to warn all pastors and authorities to watch out for them diligently, to admonish their people to do so, and to command them to beware of such runners and boys and to avoid them as certain messengers of the devil. Unless they bring good tidings and testimony of their profession and command from God for such work in such a parish. Otherwise they should not be admitted nor heard, even if they wanted to teach the pure gospel, yes, even if they were angels and vain Gabriel from heaven, for God wants nothing done by his own choice or devotion, but everything by command and profession, especially the ministry of preaching, as St. Peter says in 2 Pet. Peter says 2 Pet. 1:21. That is why Christ would not allow the devils to speak, even though they proclaimed him Son of God and spoke the truth, Luke 4, 34. 35. Mark 1:24-25. For he would not allow such examples of preaching without a profession." (Interpretation of the 82nd Psalm of 1530. V, 1060-62. [StL 5, 721 f.])

In his letter on the sneak preachers and the corner preachers, Luther further writes:

"And first of all, they are thus well and easily seized, if one asks them about their

vocation, who they have called sneak or come and preach in the corner like this, they may give no answer, nor indicate their command. And I say truly, if such sneakers had no other evil in them, and were vain saints, yet these few things (that they come sneaking without profession and unasked) may convince them forcibly that they are the devil's messengers and teachers. For the Holy Spirit does not slither, but publicly pleads from heaven. Serpents slither, but doves fly; therefore such slithering is the right course of the devil; it never fails. I have heard it said that as the slithering ones can be found among the laborers in the harvest, and preach in the fields among the laborers, so also among the charcoal burners and individuals in the forests, and everywhere sowing their seed and blowing out poison, people turn away from their parish churches. Behold the real devil's kick and grasp, how he shuns the light and mouses in the darkness. Who is so crude as not to realize that these are real messengers of the devil? If they were of God and righteous, they would first of all find their way to the pastor and deal with him, declare their profession, and tell him what they believed and whether he would allow them to preach in public. If the pastor would not allow them to do so, they would be excused to God and would want to beat the dust off their feet, etc. After all, the pastor has the preaching chair, baptism, the sacrament inside and all pastoral care is his command. But now they want to secretly chew out the pastor with all his orders; they are real thieves and murderers of souls, blasphemers and enemies of Christ and his church. There is truly no other advice here than that both offices, secular and spiritual, should do their utmost. The spiritual must truly instruct the people always and with diligence, teach these above-mentioned pieces that they allow no sneak and certainly recognize them as messengers of the devil, and learn to ask them: Where do you come from? Who sent you? Who commanded you to preach to me? Where do you have seals and letters that you are sent by men? Where are your miraculous signs that God has sent you? Why do you not go to our pastor? Why do you sneak up to me so secretly and creep into the corners?

Why do you not appear in public? Are you a child of light, why do you shy away from the light? With such questions, I am afraid, one should easily answer them, for they cannot prove their profession. And if we could bring the people into such an understanding of the profession, then such sneaks could well be controlled. Again, that they should also always be instructed and admonished to report such sneaks to the pastor; which they are also obliged to do if they want to be Christians and be saved. For if they do not do so, they are helping the devil's messenger and sneak to steal from the pastor (yes, God himself) his preaching ministry, baptism, sacrament and pastoral care, as well as secretly stealing the parishioners and thus devastating and destroying the parish (as God has ordained). If they heard such an admonition and knew that it had to do with the profession, some pious hearts would probably denounce such preachers and assassins to the pastor. For, as I said, with the profession, where one insists on it, one can well frighten the devil. A pastor can boast that he holds the office of preaching, baptism, sacrament and pastoral care publicly and rightly, and that he has been commanded to do so, and that one should seek and wait for such things from him; but the strange sneaks and plagiarists cannot boast of this, and must confess that they come forth as strangers and take up and fall into a strange office. This cannot be the Holy Spirit, but must be the wicked devil. The secular ministry must also take note of this, and, like the spiritual ministry, must insist on the profession and ask the sneak or his host: Where do you come from? Who sent you? etc., as above. And also ask the innkeeper: Who called you to harbor this sneak, to listen to his sermon? How do you know that he has orders to teach you, and you to learn from him? Why did you not tell the pastor or us? Why dost thou leave thy church, since thou art baptized, taught, reported, and there thou belonged by God's order, and crawl into the corner? Why do you establish a new church secretly and without authority? Who gave thee power to divide this parish, and to make riots among us? Who commanded you to despise, condemn and condemn your pastor before he has been questioned or sued? Whence hast thou become such a judge over thy

pastor, yea, even judge thyself? For such an iniquity, and much more, doth every one commit, who hangeth himself to the slanderers, and shall be justly reproved for it. .... Let the prophets be and remain in command; let them wait for the doctrine, and teach one another, and always help one another faithfully, that it may be done honestly and properly, saith St. Paul. Paul 1 Cor. 14:40. But how can it be honorable and orderly if each one interferes with the office of another, which he has not been commanded to do, and every layman wants to stand up in the church and preach?... This is why St. Paul often uses the word "church" in this chapter, so that he makes a certain distinction between the prophets and the people: the prophets speak, the church listens. For thus he says in 1 Cor. 14:4: But he who prophesies improves the church. And again v. 12: Seek to correct the church, so that you may have full satisfaction. Now who are these who are to correct the church? Are they not the prophets and (as he calls them) those who speak with tongues, that is, read or sing the text as the church listens, and the prophets who are to interpret the text for the improvement of the church? This is clear enough, that he here commands the congregation to hear and correct, and not to teach or preach. Then he makes an even clearer distinction and calls the congregation laity, saying vv. 16, 17: "If you bless in the Spirit, how can he who stands in the place of the laity say Amen, since he does not know what you are saying? You may give fine thanks, but the other person is not improved by it. There is a difference between the preacher and the layman. But what need is there to go on talking about it? The text is there, and also gives the reason that no other office is to be taken, for thus says St. Paul, D. 29: Let the prophets speak two or three, and let the others judge. This is no different from what is said about the prophets, that one or two should speak and the others should judge. What does 'others' mean here? Does it mean the mob? By no means. Rather, it means the other prophets or diviners who are to help preach in the church and improve the congregation; they are to judge and help ensure that the preaching is right. And if it happens that one of the prophets <p>or preachers

preaches the best, let the first one be reproved and say: Yes, you are right, I did not understand it so well; just as it happens over the table or in other matters that one gives justice to the other (even in secular matters), so also one should give way to the other much more in this matter. From this you can see how finely and diligently the sneaks have regarded St. Paul's word, with which they think they are proving themselves to be seaters in all churches, that is, they attack, judge and blaspheme all the preachers of all Christendom and appoint themselves and make themselves judges over other people's pulpits. These are called real thieves and murderers, who usurp other people's offices with iniquity and violence; against this St. Peter teaches in 1 Pet. 4:15: "Let no one suffer as an evildoer or as one who usurps another's office." (Letter to Eberhard von der Tannen from the Schleichers and Angle Preachers of 1531. XX, 2074-2086. [StL 20, 1667-1675])

In another place Luther writes:

"Nor does it follow that every citizen wants to set up a parish in his house, for no one is allowed to do so; for there is a great difference between a common and a public assembly and between a household; for what a citizen does in his house is called acting secretly." (XXI, 341 [StL 21, 1741 f.]). An die Neunmänner oder Klosterherren zu Hervord vom J. 1532.)

Provost Johann Porst in Berlin, the well-known publisher of a widely distributed Berlin hymnal, once set up edification meetings in his parish in which laymen were also allowed to teach. However, as highly as he held this institution, in a sermon he preached in 1705 to justify such meetings, he finally declared in this sermon himself, instructed by various experiences:

"Because once in my absence, when I cannot be present for the sake of official business, a dispute and argument occurred, I will stop such things where I cannot be present; also, with divine help, I will carefully prevent anything that can give someone an impetus or an opportunity to do so. And because what a simple-minded and unstudied person says, what he learns from a saying for his edification, is immediately proclaimed to be a sermon, I will from now on always transform it into the form of an examination; nor will

I allow anyone to speak except when asked by me." (Theologia homilet. in exemplis or special sermons etc. Halle 1735. p. 788.)

C.

Third section.

On the exercise of the duty of a church to take care of the purity of doctrine and life and to discipline its members in both respects.

§ 26.

All members of the church are to strive to grow and become rich in all teaching and knowledge (2 Pet. 3:18, 1 Cor. 1:5), so that they do not remain children and allow themselves to be weighed and swayed by all kinds of winds of teaching (Eph. 4:14, Heb. 5:12), but test and judge the teaching preached to them according to God's Word. Acts 17:11: "They were the noblest of those in Thessalonica, who received the word very willingly and searched the Scriptures daily to see whether it was so." Matthew 7:15, 16: "Beware of false prophets, who come to you in sheep's clothing, but inwardly they are ravening wolves. By their fruits you will recognize them." 1 John 4:1: "Beloved, do not believe every spirit, but test the spirits to see whether they are from God." 1 Cor. 10:15: "As I speak to the wise, you judge what I say."

Luther writes about this:

"The word and teaching of men have established and decreed that the doctrine of judgment should be left only to the bishops and scholars and councils: what they decide, all the world should consider right and an article of faith; as their daily boasting about the pope's spiritual right sufficiently proves. For one hears almost nothing from them but such fame that they have the authority and right to judge what is Christian or heretical, and the common Christian man should wait for their judgment and abide by it.

Behold, this glory, so that they have driven in all the world and their highest refuge and defiance is how insolently and foolishly they storm against God's law and word! For Christ equals the antithesis, taking away from the bishops, scholars and council men both the right and power to judge the doctrine, and gives it to everyone and to all Christians in general, as he says, John 10:4: My sheep know my voice. Item v. 5: My sheep do not follow strangers, but flee from them, for they do not know the voice of strangers. Item v. 8: As many as come are thieves and murderers, but the sheep do not hear them. Here you see clearly whose right it is to judge doctrine. Bishops, popes, scholars and everyone has the power to teach, but the sheep are to judge whether they teach Christ's voice or the voice of strangers. Dear, what may the water-bubbles say again, who are scratching: Concilia, Concilia! Oh, one must listen to the scholars, the bishops, the crowd, one must look at the old usage and custom! Do you think that God's word should give way to your old custom, habit, bishops? Never ever! Therefore we let bishops and concilia conclude and set what they will, but where we have God's word before us, let it stand with us and not with them, whether it be right or wrong, and let them yield to us and obey our word... Again Christ says, Matthew 7:15: "Beware of false prophets, who come to you in sheep's clothing, but inwardly they are ravening wolves. Here Christ does not pass judgment on the prophets and teachers, but on the disciples or sheep. For how could one beware of the false prophets here if one were not to take their teaching into consideration, judge and pass judgment? The third saying is St. Paul's, 1 Thess. 5:21: Test everything; keep what is good. See, here he does not want any doctrine or sentence to be held, unless it is tested and recognized as good by the church that hears it. For this testing does not concern the teachers, but the teachers must first say what is to be tested; so also here the judgment is taken from the teachers and given to the students among the Christians; that it is quite a different thing among the Christians than with the world.

In the world the masters command what they will, and the subjects receive it. But among you, says Christ, it shall not be so; but among Christians each is judge of the other, and in turn subject to the other. Although the spiritual tyrants have made a worldly authority out of Christendom... In sum, what need is there to produce more sayings? All the warning that St. Paul gives, Rom. 16:13, 18. 1 Cor. 10:14. Gal. 3:4-5. Col. 2:8. and everywhere, again, all the sayings of the prophets, when they teach to avoid the doctrines of men, do nothing else but take from the teachers the right and power to judge all teachers, and lay it on the hearers with earnest commandment, at the loss of souls; so that they not only have the right and power to judge all that is preached, but are guilty of judging by the disgrace of divine majesty." (Reason and cause from Scripture that a Christian assembly or congregation has the right and power to judge all doctrine, etc.). From the year 1523. X, 1797-1800 [StL 10, 1538 ff.]).

Elsewhere Luther writes:

"The seventh and last office is judgment and knowledge of all doctrine. Indeed, it is not for a bad reason that the priestly larvae and colored Christians have usurped this office. In fact, they intended that if they left this office among the congregation, they would not retain any of the above-mentioned (other) offices. For if the listeners were deprived of the right to pass judgment on doctrine, what might or may not a doctor or teacher dare to do, if it were possible that he were already worse than the devil? Again, if the judgment is granted and offered to the audience, what might or may a teacher venture to do if he were already more than an angel from heaven? For if this were permitted, not only would Paul punish Peter, but the angels from heaven would also be condemned. If the popes and concilia had spoken and spoken with much greater fear and terror of the priesthood, of the preaching office, of the other offices than baptizing, blessing, binding, praying, and judging doctrine, they should have feared the

judgment and condemnation of the hearers; indeed, nothing would ever have come of the papacy if this judgment had reigned.... Here is the saying of Christ, Matt. 23:2-3: On Moses' seat they have sat down, the scribes and Pharisees. Whatever therefore they tell you to observe, observe and do; but do not do according to their deeds. With these and other such sayings of the Gospel and all Scripture (by which we are admonished not to believe false teachers), what else does Christ teach us, but that each one should discern for himself his own salvation and blessedness, that he may know and be sure what to believe and whom to follow? That he also be a freely authorized judge of all those who would teach him, and be taught inwardly by God alone, John 6:45; for it will not condemn you or save you if another man's teaching is false or righteous, but your faith alone; let one man teach and preach what he will, and you must see to your highest harm or profit what you believe." (Letter to the Council etc. of Prague, dated 1523. X, 1853-55 [StL 10, 1585-1587])

Joh. Gerhard writes:

"Bellarmin objects: Since the people are uneducated, they cannot judge the doctrine of their pastor otherwise than by comparison with the doctrine of their ministerial ancestors and the ordinary pastors. Answer: That this is wrong is shown by the example of the Bereans, who searched the Scriptures daily to see whether what was taught by Paul and Barnabas was true, Acts 17:11. They do not regard the teaching of the ordinary pastors as the rule by which to judge, but the Scriptures, to which they are recommended by the Holy Spirit. That ignorance of the people, of which Bellarmin speaks, has its origin in the papacy in the prohibition to read the Holy Scriptures, for which the authors of the same will one day give a heavy account.... We do not make shepherds of the sheep, but call them sheep and let them remain sheep; meanwhile we do not want them to be unreasonable sheep, who could not and would not distinguish between shepherds and wolves." (Loc. th. de min. eccl. § 88.)

Finally, Luther writes of the

so-called charcoal-burner's faith:

"So also now the Papists say that they believe what the Church believes; and as it is said of the Poles, that they should say: I believe what my king believes. Why not? What better faith could there be that would have less trouble and anxiety than this! So it is said that a doctor asked a charcoal-burner in Prague on the bridge, out of pity for a poor layman, "Dear man, what do you believe? The charcoal burner answered: That the Church believes. The Doctor: What does the Church believe? The charcoal burner: That I believe. After that, when the doctor was to die, he was so severely challenged in his faith by the devil that he could neither stay nor rest until he said: I believe that the charcoal burner believes. Just as it is said of the great Thoma Aquino that at the end of his life he could not remain before the devil until he said: I believe what is written in this book; and he had the Bible in his arms. But God grant us not much such faith. For where they have not believed otherwise than thus, both doctor and charcoal-burner have believed themselves into the abyss of hell... And how could a people be more delicately kept in error than by such speech? For when they become certain that they may not worry about whether they are rightly or wrongly taught, they sleep and snore away safely, and henceforth do not ask for any teaching or preaching." (Warning to the people of Frankfurt am Main from the year 1533. XVII, 2442. 2443. [StL 17, 2013])

§ 27.

The church also has to establish the office of such elders or overseers among themselves, who do not work in the word and in teaching (1 Tim. 5:17: "The elders who preside are to be held in double honor, especially those who work in the word and in teaching"), but in government and in the administration of discipline and order in the church are to assist the one who has the office of the word (Rom. 12:8: "If anyone governs, let him be careful."). 1 Cor. 12:28: "God has

set in the church, first apostles, second prophets, third teachers, then workers of miracles, then gifts of healing, helpers, rulers"). The requirements for eligibility are to be determined according to Acts 6:3: "Look among you for seven men of good report, full of the Holy Spirit and wisdom, whom we may appoint for this need." 1 Timothy 3:8-12: "Let the ministers be honorable, not double-minded, not drunkards of wine, not dishonest in their dealings, having the mystery of the faith in a pure conscience. And let them be tried first; then let them serve if they are blameless. Let their wives be honorable, not blasphemers, sober, faithful in all things. Let every servant be the husband of one wife, and let them rule their children well, and their own houses." *)

Note: See the testimonies of Gerhard and Brochmand in the note to § 14. In 1523, Luther drafted the "Ordnung eines gemeinen Kastens" (order of a common box) for the community of Leißnig, according to which the entire community government was to be handled. It states:

"The administration of the common chest should be ordered and carried out in this way: Namely, that every year, on the Sunday after the eighth of the Three Kings' Days, at about 11 o'clock, a common parish assembly should and will appear at the town hall here, where, by the grace of God, in the true Christian faith, ten foremen or leaders of the common caste are to be elected from the whole crowd without distinction, the most suitable, namely, two honorable men, two of the

_________

*) It is true that these apostolic prescriptions are not at first given for the ruling elders or overseers, but for the deacons, i.e. servants or almoners; but if the holy apostles already made these requirements for those who were merely concerned with the administration of alms in the congregation, they are undoubtedly only to be applied to an even higher degree to church leaders.

governing council, three from the common citizens in the city, and three from the peasants in the country; which ten thus chosen shall willingly take upon themselves the burden of this administration and guardianship for the sake of God and the common good, and shall, in good Christian conscience, regardless of favor, envy, benefit, fear or any unseemly cause, be dutiful and bound to act to the best of their ability, to act faithfully and safely in the contents of this present our association, the administration, income and expenditure.... All Sundays in the year from 11 o'clock to 2 o'clock at vesper time, the ten rectors shall be together in our common parish hall or in the town hall and shall diligently cultivate and be present there; all of them shall take counsel and act, so that the honor of God and the love of the neighboring Christians may be maintained in proper exercise and sent to improvement, and such their counsel shall be kept in sincere and faithful secrecy and shall not be revealed in a disorderly manner. Even if some of them are not always opposed (present), prevented by honest causes, the majority shall nevertheless have the power to act and proceed.... To call, appoint and dismiss a schoolmaster for the young boys, the ten appointed heads shall have power and command in the name of our common parish congregation, according to the advice and good opinion of our chosen pastor and a preacher and other divine scripture scholars, so that a pious, blameless, well-educated man for Christian, honest and honorable discipline and instruction of the youth, as a highly necessary office, is placed in charge.... Our pastors, preachers and the ten rectors are to take care of the schooling and government of the youth in an unflagging, faithful manner and on all Sundays, for this reason, they are to give careful consideration and advice and handle it with increased seriousness.... The ten rectors shall each take great care of the discipline and government of these German schools and young girls, so that Christian discipline, honor and virtue may be steadfastly maintained." (X, 1161. et seq. [StL 10, 964 ff.; LW 45, 182-189])

Another example of such overseers can be found in the biography

of Daniel Greser, superintendent at Dresden, who died in 1591. He writes:

"Because I was a pastor at Giessen, I helped to establish an order of banishment at Ziegenheim and how a church council should be organized, etc.. By order of Landgrave Philip of Hesse, I established the church council according to this order and had the entire Christian congregation elect it by vote, and so eight people, old, honest, godly and brave men, were chosen to take on this office according to their conscience with seriousness and diligence, which they then promised to do faithfully. I therefore agreed with the church council that we would meet every four weeks in the parish on a certain day, which would be called the day of prayer; and on this day of prayer I sang the litany in the church before the altar myself, so that the choir and the congregation responded to me in unison everywhere; as the people were very diligent and devout; and after the service in the church had been spent everywhere, the senators of the church council went home with me to the parish. The (public?) sins, infirmities and wicked errors that had been committed were reported by each according to his conscience. But those who were reported were sent to the jailer so that they had to confess, and then they were warned by the church council to desist from their wicked lives, with a warning that if they did not mend their ways, they would be renounced and publicized to the entire Christian community. And through this admonition such discipline and fear has been brought into the people that the erring ones have reformed and, praise and thanks be to God, have never needed public renunciation or banishment." (See: J. Jacobi's Versäumte Buße, p. 153.)

Although the establishment of such a parish council for the sake of the adopted consistorial constitution occurred only rarely in the later older Lutheran parishes, it was not entirely absent. C. M. Pfaff writes:

"Here and there the collegial rights and the government of the churches were handed over to lay elders.... This is found in Belgium, also in our Belgian and in the Hamburg church, where the lay elders exercise the collegial rights with the preachers."

(De originibus juris eccles. Tubing. 1719. 4. p. 183. 188.)

Daniel Schneider, Superintendent of Erbach, writes: "In the Lutheran congregations, especially in the Empire, *) in Hesse and the surrounding areas, there is also a kind of church seniors or elders, who often remain in such an office for their entire lifetime, once they have been called to it, and who are responsible for helping to ensure good discipline, and also to report what goes against it to the appropriate place for improvement." (See: Allgemeines biblisches Lexikon. Frankfurt 1730, under the title: "Eltister.")

Schneider shares here an order of elders as it was introduced in the reign of the Lutheran Count Friedrich Ernst zu Solms und Tecklenburg. Remarkable is the 11th and 12th paragraph. They read as follows:

"If, contrary to expectations, the pastors and school attendants in one place or another do not perform their duties as they should, the seniors should also take good care of their duties in churches and schools and, in particular, report it immediately to the consistory if the pastors are found to be tardy and negligent in visiting the sick, just as they should also observe the school regulations and see to it that parents do not willfully keep their children from school. — Similarly, if the seniors know of anything objectionable and annoying against those who wish to attend Holy Communion, they should report it to the parish priest." (op. cit.)

§ 28.
