The congregation must see to it that only pure church and school books recognized by the orthodox church are introduced and tolerated among them (1 Thess. 5:21: "But test everything, and keep what is good." 2 Tim. 1:13: "Hold fast the example of the wholesome words which you have heard from me") and the confessional ceremonies are not to be abandoned (Gal. 2:4-5."Since some false brethren have intruded and crept in beside

___________

*) The "Reich" here refers to the Upper Rhine, Bavarian, Swabian and Franconian districts.

us in order to destroy our freedom which we have in Christ Jesus, that they might take us captive; we did not give way to them for one hour, that we might be subject to them, so that the truth of the gospel might be established among you").

Note 1: Concerning the church hymnals and the songs to be included in them, Luther writes:

"David calls his psalms Israel's psalms, and does not want them to be his own or to have the glory of them alone, but Israel should confirm them and judge and recognize them as his own. For it is because of this that the people of God or God's people accept a word or song and recognize it as right, because the Spirit of God must be in such a people, who wants and should be honored in his people. This is how we Christians speak of our psalmists. St. Ambrose has made many beautiful Hymnos Ecclesiae, which are called church hymns because the Church has accepted them and uses them as if she had made them and they were her songs. Therefore one does not say: Thus sings Ambrose, Gregorius, Prudentius, Sedulius; but: Thus sings the Christian Church. For it is only the chants of the Church that Ambrose, Sedulius, etc. sing with the Church, and the Church with them, and when they die, the Church remains, singing her songs forever." (Interpretation of the last words of David. 2 Sam. 23, 1. Ill, 2796. [StL 3, 1889])

Further writes Luther in the preface to the improved edition of his hymns of 1529: "Because I see that the daily additions are printed without any distinction, as everyone likes it, I do not want to take any measure of the fact that the first of our hymns are also being falsified the longer they are printed; I am worried that this little book will be so long, as has always happened to good books, that they have been so overwhelmed and devastated by the addition of unskillful minds that the good has been lost underneath and only the useless has remained in use.... In order that this may now, as much as we like, be spoiled, I have again reviewed the booklet anew and put our songs together one after the other with expressed names, which I previously avoided for the sake of fame, but must now do out of necessity, so that foreign, unfit

songs would not be sold under our name; after that, the others are put after it, which we consider the best and most useful. Please and admonish all those who love the pure word not to improve or increase our booklet without our knowledge and will; but if it is improved without our knowledge, that it may be known that it was not our booklet published in Wittenberg." (Erlangen edition LVI, 298. 299. [StL 10, 1424])

Luther prefaced another edition of his hymns from 1545 with the warning:

Many false masters now write songs.

Look to yourself and learn to judge them rightly;

Where God builds his church and his word,

The devil wants to be there with deceit and murder.

(Ib. p. 306. [StL 10, 1430])

Note 2: When the preachers Adolph Held and Joh. Kisler in Stabe had published a new explanation of the catechism and introduced it in their congregations, the theological faculty of Wittenberg (in the year 1636) issued an objection to it, in which it said, among other things

"It is indeed praiseworthy that every teacher and preacher, according to the gifts which God has bestowed and given him, should serve God's church not only with teaching and preaching, but also with public writings, and thus not bury his pound in the ground; But another is to be heard in common doctrinal and controversial writings, and another is to compose new symbolic books and introduce them into the church, either completely abolishing those received by the whole church, or changing them in some respects, or adding to them badly (that is, only) and introducing them alongside them. Here we say, as if none were due. Since Peucerus and his followers of Luther's Catechism stormed out of the Saxon churches and introduced a different one, it was certainly not approved of him.... What did the publication of the Formula of Concord cause but that every doctor or pastor of his liking spoke unequally of several important articles of faith and urged such in his particular church or school and presented them to the common man? If it should now arise again that each one should speak, write, and introduce catechisms of his liking into the

particular churches, confusion and division in speech and doctrine could easily arise again. Although Dr. Hutterus compiled a theological compendium or catechism, he would not have been allowed to introduce such a catechism into some schools or churches in these countries, even though he took everything from the Formula Concordiä, if he had not been ordered by public authority to write such a booklet, and if he had not subsequently been subjected to the censorship of the other doctors and theologians and approved by them. Indeed, even in the irreligious churches it is strictly and precisely observed and taken into account that not everyone is permitted to write such compendia for the youth or generally symbolic books and to insert them into one or another particular church as he pleases. How much more do we in the orthodox church want to be concerned that our symbolic books are properly preserved in their essence, that they are neither directly nor indirectly destroyed, which can easily happen if everyone introduces new catechisms or children's teachings to his liking, in which the phrases and common ways of speaking, the method, the descriptions, the classifications, etc. are changed. etc.; for it can easily happen that a changed and strange and hitherto uncommon way of speaking makes the matter itself ambiguous and doubtful. Therefore, just as one should not change the matter itself, so one should not change the accepted and approved ways of speaking in the church without great and important cause, and indeed not without public authority, according to the admonition of the Apostle Paul 2 Tim. 1:13: "Hold fast to the example of wholesome words. Now if this is to be observed in words, how much more will it be necessary in the whole treatment of things or doctrines. We therefore completely reject the idea that the two pastors in question were not authorized to write and publicly introduce such doctrines." (Consilia theol. Witebergens. I, 786. 787.)

Note 3. For a correct judgment of what confessional ceremonies are to be held, the Formula of Concord states:

"We also believe, teach, and confess that at the time of confession, when the enemies of God's word desire to suppress the pure

doctrine of the holy Gospel, the whole church of God, yea, every Christian man, but especially the ministers of the Word, as the rulers of the church of God, are obliged, by virtue of the Word of God, to confess the doctrine, and what belongs to the whole religion, freely and publicly, not only in word, but also in deed, and that in this case they should not give way to the adversaries in such matters, nor suffer them to be imposed on them by the enemies by force or deceit for the purpose of defaming the right worship of God and planting and confirming idolatry, as is written in Gal. 5: Stand therefore in the liberty wherewith Christ hath made us free, and be not entangled again in the yoke of bondage. Gal. 2: Since some false brethren had entered in and crept in beside us to destroy our liberty which we have in Christ Jesus, that they might take us captive, we did not give them one hour to be subject to us, that the truth of the gospel might remain with us. And in the same place Paul speaks of circumcision, which at that time was a free middle thing, 1 Cor. 7, and was otherwise used by Paul in spiritual freedom, Act. 16. But since the false apostles required and abused circumcision to confirm their false teaching, as if the works of the law were necessary for righteousness and salvation, Paul says that he did not want to give way for one hour, so that the truth of the gospel might be established. So Paul relents and yields to the weak in food and time or days, Rom. 14; but he will not yield to the false apostles, who would lay such things on their consciences as things necessary, even in such things that are free to themselves; Col. 2[:16]: Let no one make you consciences about food, drink, or certain feast days. And since Peter and Barnabas gave way in such a case, Paul punished them publicly, as those who did not walk correctly according to the truth of the gospel, Gal. 2[:11]. For here it is no longer a matter of the outward means, which by their nature and essence are and remain free for themselves, and therefore may suffer no commandment or prohibition

to use or refrain from using them. But it is first of all a matter of the high article of our Christian faith, as the apostle testifies: That the truth of the gospel may stand, which is obscured and perverted by such compulsion or commandment, because such things are then either publicly required for the confirmation of false doctrine, superstition and idolatry and for the suppression of pure doctrine and Christian freedom, or else are abused and thus taken up by the adversaries. The same applies to the article of Christian freedom, which the Holy Spirit, through the mouth of the holy apostle, has so earnestly commanded his churches to preserve, as we have now heard. For as soon as this is weakened and the commandments of men are imposed on the church as necessary by force, as if their omission were wrong and sinful, the way is already prepared for idolatry, by which the commandments of men are not only held equal to the commandments of God, but are also set above them. Thus also by such yielding and comparing in outward things, since one is not previously united in Christian doctrine, the idolaters are strengthened in their idolatry, while the orthodox are grieved, annoyed and weakened in their faith, both of which every Christian is obliged to avoid for the salvation and blessedness of his soul, as it is written: Woe to the world because of anger. Item: If anyone offends the least of those who believe in me, it would be better for him if a millstone were hung around his neck and he were drowned in the deepest part of the sea. But especially consider what Christ says: "Whoever confesses me for man, him I will also confess for my heavenly Father, Matthew 10." (Solid Decl., Art. 10.)

Balthasar Meisner therefore writes:

"Although a Church not exposed to the persecutions of heretics and tyrants may freely use the true middle things according to her convenience and usefulness, or abstain from them, and make changes in them as she thinks good, for the edification of the weak; but if the enemies of the truth, in fighting against the ceremonies, aim at the heart and life of heavenly doctrine and evangelical truth,

and therefore of Christian liberty, nothing must be either instituted or abolished in their favor, but one must fight for the customary ceremonies, if only they are godly and useful, as for house and home." (Colleg. adiaphorist. Disp. I, § 52.)

§ 29.

The congregation is to receive as members only those who are baptized (Ephesians 5:2-26: "Christ loved the church, and gave himself for it, that he might sanctify it, having cleansed it by the washing of water in the word." 1 Cor. 12:3: "For by one Spirit we were all baptized into one body, whether Jews or Greeks, whether bond or free; and were all made to drink into one Spirit"); which 2.If they are adults, they profess the belief that the Holy Scriptures of the Old and New Testaments are the Word of God and that the doctrine contained in the confessions of the Evangelical Lutheran Church, especially in Luther's Small Catechism and in the unamended Augsburg Confession, is the pure Christian doctrine (Gal. 2:4: "Since some false brethren had entered in with them and crept in alongside them."). 2 Cor. 6:14-15, 17: "Do not pull on the strange yoke with the unbelievers. For what enjoyment has righteousness with unrighteousness? what fellowship has light with darkness? How does Christ agree with Belial? or what part does the believer have with the unbeliever? Therefore come out from among them, and separate yourselves, says the Lord, and touch no unclean thing, and I will receive you." 2 John 10:11: "If anyone comes to you and does not bring this teaching, do not take him home or greet him. For whoever greets him makes himself partaker of his evil works."

Ephes. 4:3-6: "And be diligent to keep the unity of the Spirit in the bond of peace. One body and one Spirit, just as you were called to one hope in your calling. One Lord, one faith, one baptism, one God and Father (our) of all, who is over you all, and through you all, and in you all"); and who finally 3. lead a Christian, unpleasant life (1 Cor. 5:9-13: "I have written to you in the letter that you should have nothing to do with fornicators. I do not mean the harlots of this world, or the covetous, or the robbers, or the idolaters; otherwise you would have to leave the world. But now I have written to you that you should have nothing to do with them; for if anyone who lets himself be called a brother is a fornicator, or a covetous man, or an idolater, or a blasphemer, or a drunkard, or a robber, you should not eat with him. For what concern is it of those outside that I should judge them? Do you not judge those who are within? But God will judge those who are outside. Put out from among yourselves those who are evil." 1 Cor. 10:21: "You cannot drink the cup of the Lord and the cup of devils at the same time; you cannot partake of the Lord's table and the devil's table at the same time." Matthew 7:6: "Do not give the holy things to the dogs, and do not throw your pearls before swine, lest they trample them under their feet and turn and tear you apart.")

Note: Compare Luther's testimony to this in the note to paragraph 12. The fact that the listeners also have to commit themselves to the Augsburg Confession and Luther's catechisms is attested by the Formula of Concord, when it states:

"To the same Christian and in God's Word well-founded

Augsburg Confession we again herewith confess from the bottom of our hearts, remain with the same simple, bright and loud mind, as the words entail, and consider the said Confession to be a purely Christian symbol by which true Christians of this time should be found next to God's Word; just as Christian symbols and confessions have been made in the Church of God in the past about a number of great disputes that have occurred, to which the pure teachers and listeners have professed with heart and mouth at that time." (Repetition, Preface.)

Further:

"And then, sixthly, because these highly important matters also concern the common man and laymen, who for the sake of their salvation must nevertheless distinguish as Christians between pure and false doctrine, we also unanimously confess the small and large catechism of Dr. Luther. Luther's Small and Large catechisms, as written by him and incorporated into his tone, because they have been unanimously approved, accepted and publicly used in churches, schools and homes by all churches related to the Augsburg Confession, and because in them the Christian doctrine from God's Word has been understood in the most correct and simple way for the simple-minded laity and explained in the same way." (Sol. Decl., “Comprehensive Summary,” par. 6 [German text].)

§ 30.

The church is to investigate the matter of those of its members who are reported to it as those who have disobeyed God's Word in doctrine or life and have already been punished in vain according to Matthew 18:15, 16, both in private and before witnesses, or whose false doctrine or sin is known and evident to everyone, in a public assembly according to God's Word in Christian order with the use of witnesses (2 Cor. 13:1: "When I come to you the third time, all things shall be established by the mouth of two or three witnesses") and to

reprove, admonish and punish those found guilty of their error or sin (2 Cor. 2: 6: "But it is enough that he has been punished by many in this way". 2 Thess. 3:14, 15: "But if anyone does not obey our word, rebuke him by letter and have nothing to do with him, so that he may be ashamed. Yet do not regard him as an enemy, but admonish him as a brother." 1 Timothy 5:20: "Punish them that sin before all, that others also may fear." Gal 2:14: "But when I saw that they were not walking according to the truth of the gospel, I said to Peter in public, 'If you, who are a Jew, live a Gentile life and not a Jewish one, why do you force the Gentiles to live a Jewish life?) But those who do not hear them, the congregation, and fall into fundamental error or into open error: Those who do not hear them, the church, and persist in fundamental error or in obvious mortal sin without repentance and stiff-necked, must, after a unanimous decision, be publicly expelled from the church or banned by the minister of the Word (Matthew 18:17-20: "If he does not hear them, tell the church. If he does not listen to the church, consider him a Gentile and a tax collector. Verily I say unto you, Whatsoever ye shall bind on earth shall be bound in heaven: and whatsoever ye shall loose on earth shall be loosed in heaven. Furthermore, I say to you: Where two of you become one on earth, whatever they may ask, it shall be done for them by my Father in heaven. For where two or three are gathered together in my name, there am I in the midst of them." 1 Cor. 5:1-5: "There is a common cry that there is fornication among you, and such fornication that even the Gentiles do not know how to say that a man has his father's wife. And ye are puffed up, and have not rather borne sorrow, that he that did the work

might be done by you. I indeed, who am not present in body, but am present in spirit, have already judged as present him who has done these things: In the name of our Lord Jesus Christ, in your assembly with my spirit, and with the power of our Lord Jesus Christ, to deliver him to Satan for the destruction of the flesh, that the spirit may be saved in the day of our Lord Jesus Christ." Rom. 16:17: "But I exhort you, brethren, that ye look out for them which cause divisions and offences contrary to the doctrine which ye have learned, and depart from them." Titus 3:10-11: "Avoid a heretical man when he is once and again reproved, and know that such a one is perverse and sins as he who reproves himself." Compare also 1 Cor. 5:9-13 and 2 Tim. 2:17-21). The church is no longer to recognize such banished persons as brethren, but to regard them as pagans and tax collectors, and therefore to deny them the rights of brethren, and to suspend all brotherly fellowship with them; but to deal with those who are unwilling to accept such a ban with Christian discipline (1 Cor. 5:1-2.); on the other hand, to publicly absolve and readmit those on whom the ban has achieved its purpose and who therefore return in repentance (2 Cor. 2:6-11: "But it is enough that he has been punished by many, so that you should now forgive and comfort him all the more, so that he may not sink into too much sorrow. Therefore I exhort you to show your love for him. For this is why I have written to you, that I may know whether you are righteous to be obedient in all things. But to whom you forgive anything, I also forgive. For I also, if I forgive anyone anything,

for your sake I forgive it in Christ's stead, so that we may not be overtaken by Satan. For we are not unaware of what he has in mind").

Note 1: The pious theologian J. Quistorp the Younger, professor at Rostock (died 1669), testifies to how much depends on the congregation for the proper exercise of church discipline when he writes:

"But as long as the congregation of saints does not offer us a hand, but this burden lies solely on the shoulders of the despised preachers, no improvement of the times can be expected." (See Pia desideria, German from 1665.)

Note 2: On those on whom church discipline is to be practiced, compare Luther's testimony in § 7. Note 2: To the question:

"Who is subject to church discipline?" Conrad Dannhauer answers:

"1. who allows himself to be called a brother (1 Cor. 5:11); 2. who is of sound mind; 3. who is a member of the visible church; 4. who is still alive; 5. who is an impenitent sinner. But man himself, who has sinned without it extending to his offspring (Ezek. 18:4). The brother, not the brotherhood, not a whole congregation of brothers; as far as the last point of church discipline is concerned, namely the ban. For this would not only be throwing a community out of fellowship, which is impossible, but also sowing the wheat with the tares, since there is no visible particular church in which the invisible one is not hidden. But 1. every brother, the highest as well as the lowest, for it is said: Whose sins ye retain, they are retained, John 20:23, and: If any man be called a brother, 1 Cor. 5:11. To those who only attack the doves with church discipline, but dare not touch the eagles, fits what J. Valentin Andreä wrote in his book on the church discipline. Valentin Andreä wrote in his Apologus, p. 146, therefore also includes the bishop and superior of the church, also the patriarch, also the pope, also the king and anyone else who is the highest in a Christian republic. 2. a brother who is of sound mind; but a madman or one possessed in body is not an object of this (binding) key, because he does

not do what he does of his own accord, but driven by his black guest. 3. A brother who is a member of the visible church, even if he is already excluded from the invisible, is a Christian from the root of the baptismal covenant, even if not from the true faith in essence. On the other hand, this key does not apply to someone who has become a member of a foreign community, e.g. a declared apostate and enemy, a convicted heretic, an incurable sinner in the Holy Spirit, such a sinner, if he has gone out from us both in mind and place, is no longer a brother, and precisely because he has gone out from us and is in enemy territory, is no longer able to be ecclesiastically banned by us, but is to be shunned, Tit. 3:10, not to be tolerated, and to be regarded as an enemy and treated as such.... "Therefore," says Jerome to Tit. 3:10, "a heretic is one who has condemned himself, because a fornicator, an adulterer, a murderer and other vices are expelled from the church by the priests, but the heretics themselves pass judgment on themselves by departing from the church of their own free will,' which departure seems to be the condemnation of their own conscience. It is different with a schismatic who is "spenstlich" (as Luther says), who is still within the boundaries of the church. I have spoken of the heretic after he has gone out from us, for before he goes out he is to be admonished (Tit. 3:11) and punished. 4. a brother who is still alive. Both keys extend equally far; just as absolution does not actually take place with a deceased person, neither does the ban. Nor are those who have died in sin to be condemned thoughtlessly, since we cannot know what divine grace has wrought in them in their last struggle and breath. It was enough for Petro to have said of Judas: He went to his place. 5. A brother who is an impenitent sinner, walking behind, stiff-necked. I say, a sinner. As there is no law given to the innocent and righteous, so there is no discipline. In fact, Christ

and his apostles were also subject to curses, but not so in law. I say, an impenitent, that is, one convicted of those sins of which mention has just been made, 1 Cor. 5:11. Here belong those who are infamous through shameful things or have no honest name, who want to know nothing of repentance, procurers, public harlots, comedians, murderous duelists, who make a trade out of such sins. To these must be added the irreconcilable, who are inflamed with unquenchable flames of anger and hatred (Matt. 5:23-24.), as well as those who do not want to be punished, who, as Avianus says, 'let no humble word escape their throat, begin to quarrel with the priest in confession as if they had sat down with him on the beer bench. Nor do they come to confession because they are hungry and thirsty for holy absolution and the body and blood of the Lord Christ, but only want to try the pastor, whether he also wants to repel them from confession, as they are wont to call it hostile, so that afterwards they will make an article of complaint to the authorities; which is to be noted for their ungodly mind, because otherwise they do not bother about the confessional, but now in the disagreement that has arisen they do. Then I think it right that they should not be absolved until they come to terms with their pastor." (Liber conscientiae. 1, 1127-38.)

Note 3: That the ban is to be carried out by the minister of the Word, but that it is only to be carried out after a previous fruitless, gradual, fraternal punishment, as well as a public, proper investigation and a judicial sentence. Our confession testifies to this when it says:

"But this would be the right way if one kept the order according to the Gospel, Matthew 18, where Christ says: If your brother sins against you, go and punish him between you and him alone. There you have a fine and precious teaching to govern the tongue well, which is well to be remembered against grievous abuse. Now follow this, so that you do not so soon carry out your neighbor elsewhere and talk after him, but secretly admonish him to do better; likewise, if another brings to your ears something that this or that

person has done, teach him to go and punish him himself where he has seen it; where he has not, that he shut up. You may also learn this from daily house rules. For this is what the master does in the house: if he sees that the servant is not doing what he should, he speaks to him himself. But if he were so foolish as to leave the servant at home and go out into the streets to complain about him to his neighbors, he would certainly have to hear: You fool, what's it to us? Why don't you tell him yourself? Behold, that would be a brotherly thing to do, that the evil be rebuked and your neighbor be honored. As Christ also says there: If he hears you, you have won your brother. You have done a great and excellent work, for do you think it is a small thing to win a brother? Let all the monks and holy orders come flocking together to see if they can take credit for having won a brother. Christ further teaches: "But if he will not hear you, take one or two more to you, so that every matter may stand on the mouth of two or three witnesses; so that you may deal with the one whom it concerns, and not with him behind his knowledge. But if this does not help, then bear it publicly for the community, whether for secular or spiritual judgment. For here you do not stand alone, but have with you those witnesses by whom you can convict the guilty party, on whom the judge can base his judgment and sentence and punish; thus you can properly and rightly come to the point of rebuking or correcting the wicked." (Large Catechism on the 8th Commandment.)

Furthermore, our Confession states:

"It is certain that all pastors should have the common jurisdiction to banish those who lie in public vices, and that the bishops, as tyrants, have drawn them to themselves and shamefully abused them for their enjoyment. For the officials have used it with unpleasant willfulness and, either out of avarice or other willfulness, have plagued the people and "banned them without any prior legal recognition" (i.e., according to the Latin text, without any judicial order). But what kind of tyranny is this, that an official in a city should have the power to plague and coerce people with the ban etc. according to his will alone and without

legal recognition? Now they have used such coercion in all kinds of things, and not only have they not punished the right vices with it, since the ban should follow, but also in other minor matters, where people have not fasted or celebrated properly; without that they have sometimes punished adultery, and then also often reviled and defamed innocent people. For since such an accusation is very important and serious, no one should ever be condemned without legal and proper judgment in the case." (Smalc. Art. Second Appendix.)

Dannhauer writes beautifully about the nature of the preceding fraternal punishment as follows:

"Every Christian, as the bishop of his neighbor, is bound to punish him fraternally, Leviticus 19:17, Matthew 18:15, but it requires: 1. truth, that one is first of all certain of the matter. 2. prudence, to take the right time. One often punishes his neighbor out of season, and would be wise to keep silent. Whoever wants to pull a sprout out of someone's eye must also handle the matter delicately. The censure of one's neighbor must be handled just as delicately and carefully. 3. Kindness: "The righteous will smite me kindly," is written in Psalms 141:5. 4. Sincerity, that no unseemly affections or dishonorable mockery appear in such work. 5. epieikeia and fairness, as which does not make a small mistake so high and, as they say, does not make a mountain out of a molehill. In doubtful cases, out of Christian love, it always believes the good rather than the evil, considers a person innocent rather than guilty. Excuse, says St. Bernard, your neighbor's intention and opinion, you cannot excuse the work, saying that it was done out of ignorance, that he was hasty, that it happened to him by accident, that he was not otherwise so wicked." (Katechismus-Milch ll, 352.)

Ludwig Hartmann's remark also belongs here:

"The witnesses who are used for the second degree of admonition must be sufficiently skillful to win over the brother and at least not be detestable to the one to be punished;

for if one takes either quarrelsome persons or those who are detestable to the one to be punished, or even those who are not secretive and whom he cannot stand, nothing will be accomplished; therefore such may be used,...before whom the one who has erred is not ashamed to confess his sins and who, through their reputation, can persuade him to confess and amend in an appropriate manner." (Pastorale ev p. 861.)

Calov testifies and defends against the Arminians that no one is to be considered and treated as a reprobate until the church or congregation has passed judgment on him, when he writes:

"In order to cast doubt on the authority of the congregation in ecclesiastical censure and discipline, they (the Arminians) agitate, among other things, the passage Matt. 18:17, 'Consider him a Gentile and a publican', claiming that nothing is attributed to the church there. For, says their apology, it is not the church that is commanded to regard a stiff-necked and impenitent sinner as a Gentile and a publican, but the one who has had to deal with him. But they make the mistake of taking together what is too different from one another. It is true that everyone in the church is required to consider such a person a pagan and a publican, but after the church's prior censure, to which it proceeds according to the other stages of admonition by virtue of the order. It is therefore wrong that nothing is attributed to the Church here. For we have here the express provision: If he does not hear those (two or three whom he has taken to himself), tell it to the church; if he does not hear the church, consider him a heathen or a publican according to the judgment of the church and according to that (for the last judgment according to the other stages of admonition is here referred to the church). Otherwise, if every private man could, at his own discretion, consider a brother a heathen or a publican without the judgment of the congregation, innumerable divisions would arise, and the last and most important censure, if the other stages of admonition were of no avail, would be left to the judgment of every private man, which is no less unrighteous than it is contrary to this ordinance of Christ." (Considerat. Armin. Cap. 24. § 11. p. 586.)

Ludwig Hartmann

writes:

"The lawful process to be observed before and during the ban is shown by Christ Matt. 18. Namely, 1. the brother is to be admonished and punished for his wrong or offense by the Christian brother privately and alone, without arbitrator or witness. If he hears him or accepts the secret punishment, the matter is settled and no further trial is necessary. And this is to be regarded as winning according to v. 15. 2. if he does not accept this punishment, which is done in private and secretly and in accordance with the gentleness of Christ, he is to be punished by calling two or three others as witnesses, v. 16. 3. if he also despises these, the matter is to be brought to the congregation, or to those who represent and preside over the congregation and through whom everything that concerns them is duly and publicly made known to the congregation. Those to whom the government of the church is committed are especially called elders 1 Tim. 5:17; Acts 15:2, 4, 6, 22; likewise governors, guardians of church discipline 1 Cor. 12:28; such in the churches of Germany and neighboring kingdoms are the consistories, which consist of both ecclesiastical and political men. 4 Finally, if he also despises the church or the leaders of the church (does not heed their admonitions), he is to be excluded and considered an outcast, Matt. 18:17." (Pastorale ev. p. 845.)

Note 4: Christian Kortholt testifies to the fact that there are cases in which the levels of admonition cannot be observed when he writes:

"Above all, a distinction must be made between hidden and manifest sins. But we do not call hidden sins those which are known to no one at all, for God alone judges them Rom. 2:16; but those which are known to a few and are not connected with public reproach; but those which are manifest, those which are publicly known and therefore connected with the reproach of many. Now as to hidden sins, the minister of the church, no less than every sincere Christian, must observe the rule of the Savior, Matt. 18:15 ff. But manifest sins are to be punished publicly. St. Augustine says: 'That which is committed

before all is to be punished before all. And this is the precept of the apostle himself, who thus addresses his Timothy: 'Let them that sin' (that is, with public reproach) 'punish them before all, that others also may fear. 1 Tim. 5, 20." (Pastor fidelis. Hamburgi 1696. pp. 92. 96. 97.) Two things, however, should be noted here. First, that under certain circumstances love requires that even obvious sins should not immediately be punished publicly. Of this Ludwig Hartmann says:

"Even sins that are public and known to all are not to be punished publicly the first time. For all punishments are to be carried out in such a way that those punished are led to a true acknowledgment of their sins and are moved in their hearts. Therefore, everything that serves the salvation and change of mind of the neighbor is to be tried first. If, according to the nature of the person, you immediately bring the offense out into the open, you will harden more than you will mend by this severity and obvious harshness and soften the ulcer, and 'out of shame he will begin to defend his sin, and whom you want to make better, you make worse,' as Augustine rightly reminds us." (Pastorale ev. p. 856.)

On the other hand, the stages of admonition are not to be understood as if the sinner may only be admonished three different times. Hartmann says of this:

"All stages of exhortation are to be repeated, if necessity requires it, and to work on the person to be converted until he improves or his obstinacy becomes apparent through contempt of all reminders. For Christ shows in Matthew 18 the order and stages of admonition, not how many times it should be given." (op. cit. p. 862.)

Kortholt reminds us of the same when he writes:

"But that this rule of Christ concerning the punishment of hidden sins is not so much to be understood of two exhortations to be given only in a superficial and indifferent manner, but rather of a twofold order and stage, at each of which one is to dwell for a time before proceeding further: this he himself shows shortly afterwards in v. 22, when he answers Peter's question as to how often the sinning brother is to be forgiven: Not seven times, but seventy times seven times." (Pastor fid. p. 94. 95.)

Note 5: Concerning the proclamation of the banishment of indelicate consequences, Balduin remarks:

"The pastor indicates to the congregation from the pulpit that this person, repeatedly admonished, remains obstinate in an obvious offense (which is to be mentioned by name). Since this was not without great annoyance to the congregation and not without the most obvious danger of divine wrath, it was decided in the public meeting of those who had been deputized on this matter" (that is here, in the congregational assembly), "it was decided that such a person should be expelled from the fellowship of the church as a rotten member, rejected from the use of Holy Communion, from the patronage, from weddings and from all the company of honorable people, until he recognizes his offense and makes serious repentance. The congregation is therefore admonished to completely distance itself from such a person as a rotten member, not to admit him to any company, but to regard him as a cut-off member.... At the same time, the wretched person should be prayed for with compassion, that God may lead him back to the knowledge of his sin and work a serious repentance in him so that his spirit may be saved. For this severity is not applied to the destruction of the soul, but rather the salvation of the soul is sought, as Paul writes of the blood transgressor 1 Cor. 5:5. And for this reason the banned person, although excluded from the use of holy actions and from association with the saints, is not to be completely rejected from hearing the Word.... As far as the association of the banished person with other pious persons is concerned, the prohibition of the same may not extend further than Christian love permits. Therefore we must refrain from familiar association with the banned, lest we appear to despise the discipline of the church or to participate in the sins of others; hence we are forbidden to have anything to do with them (1 Cor. 5:9; 2 Thess. 3:15); however, we should not abandon our benevolence toward them, but rather sympathize with their misery, exhort and comfort them, and pray for their conversion, and thus show ourselves eager for their salvation in everything. Nor does the excommunication annul civil association,

contracts and commerce with the excommunicated person; just as in the papacy the oath of allegiance and the obedience of children is dissolved when the authorities or the father have been excommunicated; but because the ban only affects the criminal, but not his friends and relatives, therefore the subjects owe obedience to the authorities, the children to the parents, the wife to the husband, even if he is under ban, and can make use of their service as often as necessity requires. For the ban does not cause a separation of those whom God and nature have joined, but only a separation from a particular church in ecclesiastical matters, until true repentance takes place." (Tractat. de casibus consc. p. 1129-31.)

Concerning the latter point, Luther writes:

"I fear in our part that our pastors will be too bold and grasp at the bodily things, at the goods, like the pope; if he excommunicated one and put him under ban, and he did not turn to it, he said: Ey, we must also forbid him the market, etc., so that he does not buy or sell. That's the devil if you want to go too far. Then someone asked whether an exile would also like to go to church and listen to the sermon? Answer: Yes, he should not be forbidden to do so, for in sermons they learn where they are lacking." (XXII, 975. [StL 22, 627])

Note 6. Chemnitz writes about the readmission of the banished when they repent:

"When they" (the Christians in apostolic times) "realized from the fact itself that such banished ones were divinely grieved, recognized the greatness of sin, feared God's wrath, and earnestly asked for forgiveness and reconciliation with God and the church, so that they might be received back into the fellowship of the church: then the church, seeing that it had accomplished the purpose sought by discipline, used such leniency that he who was already godly sorrowful would not be plunged into despair or hardened or hardened by too great severity, that he might not, as Paul says, sink into too great sorrow or be overtaken by Satan, 2 Cor. 2:7... Therefore, because the Corinthian church had applied the severity of discipline

<pages 136>

and saw that by the working of the Holy Spirit that which is sought by discipline had been wrought in him, that he recognized the greatness of his sin, was grieved by the knowledge and feeling of God's wrath, and with fervent faith humbly and earnestly sought God's mercy, and asked that he might be absolved from sin by the ministry of the Word: Therefore the church thought that the fallen man should be received back, that forgiveness should be announced to him from the gospel, and that he should be absolved from sins through the keys of the kingdom of heaven. But because the crime committed was exceedingly great and they had previously been sharply rebuked by the apostle for proceeding too lightly against that fallen man, they reported the matter to the apostle and asked him what was to be done in such a case. And since the situation was as we have explained, Paul approved the Corinthians' advice and judgment and replied: 'It is enough that he is thus punished by many,' that is, by the whole church; therefore, just as before, when he was safe and unrepentant, you accused him, punished him, and bound him by retaining his sins, so now that he has been brought to a change of heart, you should comfort him all the more to the contrary and give or forgive him his sin, so that he may not sink into too great sorrow.... And to confirm the judgment of the Corinthians, Paul adds these words: "Whomsoever ye forgive, him will I also forgive;" and, citing his example concerning the reception of the fallen, he adds: "For I also, if I forgive any man, forgive him for your sakes, in Christ's stead;" because he promised in Matthew 18: 'Where two or three are gathered together in my name, there am I in the midst of them: therefore whatsoever ye shall loose on earth shall be loosed in heaven. Paul therefore exhorts them that, just as that bloodsucker had previously been banished by public judgment of the church and handed over to Satan, that is, declared by the common voice of the church that he was not a member of Christ but of Satan, so now again the re-admission and reconciliation of him was confirmed by the public authority

of the church and its common voice. For this is what Paul's words are meant to express: 'Therefore I exhort you that you "prove" and confirm the love with which you are to embrace the penitent "in him" by your public vote. For the Greek word kyrun means to approve, confirm, validate and hold by common vote and with public authority." (Examen. Loc. de indulgentiis, p. 75-78.)

The Apology testifies in the following words that no punishment and nothing shameful at all is to be imposed on the penitent returnees:

"The fathers did not want to accept those who were found in public vices without a punishment, and this had many reasons. For it serves as an example that public vices should be punished, as the gloss in the decree also says; so it was also unwise that those who had fallen into open vices should soon be admitted to the sacrament without trial. All these ceremonies have now long since ceased, and there is no need to reinstate them, for they do nothing at all to reconcile us to God. Nor was it the opinion of the Fathers in any way that men should thereby obtain forgiveness of sins, although such outward ceremonies easily lead the inexperienced to believe that they help somewhat towards salvation. Whoever teaches or holds this, teaches and holds in an entirely Jewish and pagan way." (Art. 12.)

Note 7: An important rule in judging a complaint brought to the congregation is what Dannhauer writes:

"One witness is not a witness" (Num. 35:30). (Tract. de cas, consc. I, 1123.)

This also includes what Luther wrote to Amsdorf in 1542:

"We hear no one from our congregation when the other part is absent." (Letters of Schütz, in translation. Ill, 98.)

Note 8: That one who has fallen publicly, if he is penitent, should not be banished, but should be publicly reconciled with the congregation, Luther testifies in the following words:

"It is not secret that a citizen's son here, the old S., has fallen into a public murder, as you all know, and has

nevertheless gone to the sacrament and led a bride to church; of this I have no favor. Now it is said that the matter has been cleared up with friends and the authorities; I do not know that; the council and friends who are concerned must give letters and seals about it. As long as this is not done, he shall not stand before me for baptism, choir and sacrament until he shows the letters and seals of the council that the matter is settled before a council. Let us do so, because it is a public act, so the reconciliation must also be public; otherwise it is no good. If he brings word from the council and asks for forgiveness of sins, he should kneel publicly before the altar, and the priest should say that he is absolved, for the whole church is offended.... Moses commanded that if a dead body was found between two cities, the city to which the body was closest had to accept it, wash itself, protest to heaven and earth, and testify that it was innocent. Let there be no shame or jest here; therefore do not blame me, who am commanded to govern the church, in this. If guilt is evident, then reconciliation should also be evident." (Luther's Works. Hall. A. XXII, 961. 962. [StL 22I, 616])

Once, when a member of the congregation was about to take Holy Communion, another, who had been offended by him, spat before him publicly in the church, and said, for all to hear, "Thou wretch art not worthy that the earth should bear thee, go thou to receive the devil." A preacher requested advice about this case from a member of the Hamburg ministry, who, with the approval of the latter, replied on Oct. 13, 1614, as follows:

"It is justly to be regarded as a terrible blasphemy and public gross annoyance of the Church of Christ, with which the holy and righteous God has been violently angered, yes, justly and most justly. Num. 15:30. Lev. 24:16, 23.... Besides this, this man has made himself such an exceedingly great blasphemer that he has granted his offender (according to his terrible words) not salvation, but the devil and damnation, and precisely in the most blessed means of life. What could be more abominable before the dear God!

Certainly the greatest torture of the Lord Jesus Christ in his suffering and death did not hurt him as much as this abominable blasphemy did. That the Lord (pastor) used and still uses seriousness in this is to be highly praised. He certainly could not have done less without violating his conscience, nor could he have allowed such a blasphemer to enter the sacrament of the Most Holy Sacrament and conjugal trust without public repentance from the congregation and reconciliation with his adversary; and truly this public sin is not to be reckoned among the common public sins, but among the grossest, as a special abomination and high blasphemy against God, therefore it deserves the severity of the secular authorities in addition to the harsh discipline of the church. Even if public repentance were not already in use, in my and others' opinion, this confession would have to be publicly atoned for and condemned to the congregation, both because of the terrible abomination contained in the abominable words and because it (the confession) was made publicly in front of the whole congregation. As long as he refuses it, he testifies that he is not serious about his repentance; so how could he be knowingly admitted? I find nothing against this, how such a delinquent could be dealt with more gently; especially (unless), since one would have considerable cause to do so, he would, next to reconciliation with his adversary, like to make a heartfelt deprecation in the name of the whole congregation to the most noble estates and members of the church, and the Lord (the pastor) would then publicly announce the same to the whole church; which I would not easily accept. However, this shall be my advice, the Lord (the pastor) and the others, who oppose the Lord in this matter and think to carry the blasphemer, submit to both parts of the venerable Synodi's concerns and let them like what is decided and pronounced there.

If the opposite part of the Lord wants to refuse this, then the wicked one heaps his sin upon himself with the severe wrath of God and the others share in it with him." (Dedekennus' Thesaurus II, 462. 463.)

When this blasphemer was not to be married in church before his repentance, he sought and received a wedding from a neighboring pastor, which is why the blasphemer is called a "rotten member" in a second concern of November 29, 1614, who "banishes himself and excommunicates and expels himself from his congregation." (Ibid. p. 465.) However, Nikolaus Rebhahn, General Superintendent of Eisenach (died 1626), writes about the process of public repentance in his book Don der Kirchenbuße:

"Nevertheless, not all public sins belong here under this church discipline, but only those which also have the other characteristics in themselves, that they are gross, terrible, annoying sins... which are done out of willfulness and malice and in which one has persisted for a considerable time, or, if it has not lasted long, nevertheless, through a few such acts many pious Christians' hearts and in them the Holy Spirit have been grieved. Spirit has been grieved, an obvious offense has been caused, which others take offense at, and are also deceived and seduced by it." (Cited in Hartmann's Pastorale ev. p. 925.) Hartmann also notes: "In public and notorious crimes, the truly repentant sinner is to be accepted, but not suspended for long. For here, too, Paul's saying that he should not sink into too great sorrow holds good, 2 Cor. 2:5, and so it is not advisable to postpone absolution for a time either as a punishment or as a trial for the penitent, since the advantages which this delay seems to have may bring greater disadvantages. Nor is there any reason for it in Scripture; but to suspend fallen men who are ready to repent for a long time without necessity is a kind of torture of conscience; Nathan, at least, did not suspend David, who was ready to repent, for a long time, but immediately announced to him the forgiveness of

his sins." (Ibid. p. 864.)

Note 9 Those who separate themselves from an orthodox church for the sake of sin or error are not to be put under ban, since they are no longer members, but are outside and have already put themselves under ban; however, the latter is also to be expressly declared publicly and such a person who is banished from himself is to be treated by the members of the church as one who is under ban. (Compare 1 John 2:19: "They went out from us, but they were not of us. For if they had been of us, they would have remained with us; but that they might be made manifest that they are not all of us." 2 Timothy 4:10: "Demas has forsaken me and loved this world.") Luther therefore writes:

"We must not banish our usurers, drunkards, revellers, fornicators, blasphemers and mockers; they banish themselves, indeed, they are ready to do so up to their ears; they despise the Word of God, do not come to church, do not listen to sermons, do not go to the sacrament. Well then, if they do not want to be Christians, let them be pagans; after all, who asks much about it? .... So the pastor should not absolve them, give them no sacraments, let them neither come nor stand at any baptism, nor at any honest wedding, nor at any funeral; let them behave like the pagans among us, which they do gladly. And when they want to die, no priest or chaplain shall come to them; and when they have died, the executioner shall drag them out into the drudgery pit to the city; no student or chaplain shall come to them; because they want to be heathens, we also want to keep them as heathens." (Table Talk. XXII, 974. 5. [StL 22, 626 f.])

§ 31.

Although the congregation should not presume to dismiss its preachers and others who administer an ecclesiastical office in it arbitrarily, if they fall into errors that are dangerous to the soul and, after they have been found guilty in a proper examination (1 Tim. 5:19. "Against one of the elders receive no accusation except two or three witnesses"), they will not be reproved either by the congregation or by the righteous

ministers of the Word who have been brought in, or they are revealed as stiff-necked, impenitent sinners, or they have committed a case by which they lose the good testimony of those who are outside and blaspheme the enemies of the Lord: the church in Christian order (which may include temporary suspension) must remove them from their office as those whom God Himself has put away. (Matthew 7:15: "Beware of false prophets, who come to you in sheep's clothing, but inwardly they are ravening wolves." Hos 4:6: "You reject God's word, therefore I will also reject you, so that you will not be my priest." John 10:5: "They do not follow a stranger, but flee from him, for they do not know the voice of strangers." 1 Tim. 3:7: "He must also have a good testimony from those who are outside, lest he fall into reproach and a snare from the blasphemer.")

Note 1: Luther testified in a letter to Valentin Hausmann in 1532 that it was not at the discretion of the congregations to remove a preacher from office, when the people of Zwickau had arbitrarily dismissed one of their preachers, particularly at Mühlpfort's instigation. He wrote:

"You yourselves can well consider that, where a manor-owner has studied all his life, consumed his father's estate and suffered all misfortune, there should be a pastor in Zwickau, as they have let themselves be heard: that they should be masters and the pastor a servant, who would sit on the shack every day; if Mühlpfort wanted to, he would stay, if not, he would have to leave — no, my dear sir, you should not take it there, or you should not keep a pastor. We will not do it, nor suffer it, unless they confess that they do not want to be Christians. We should and will suffer it from pagans, but Christ himself will not suffer it from Christians. If those in Zwickau or you yourselves, my dear sirs and friends,

do not want to feed your brother, you may well leave that alone. Christ is somewhat richer than the world, even if he is poor. It is said: Esurientes implevit (he fills the hungry with goods); let us leave it at that." (XXI, 357. [StL 21, 1645 f.])

When another congregation wanted to remove a preacher, to whom they themselves gave testimony that he was irreproachable in doctrine and life, but who punished vices severely, Luther wrote to them in 1543:

"So now you also have the misgiving: because there is no other cause and fault than that you have cast a grudge on the pastor without his merit, yes, for the sake of his great merit and faithful preaching, that it is not to be done, nor is it possible, for the sake of your grief and unjust understanding, to do violence and injustice to such a well-witnessed pastor and to throw dirt on him. I cannot blame the visitators for not doing this, nor for burdening their consciences with such injustice for the sake of the devil and going to the devil with you (where they were authorized to do so by you). Look to yourselves, dear sirs and friends, look to yourselves: if the devil brings you down, he will not leave it at that, but will fall further.... You are not masters of the pastors and preaching office, have not established them, but only God's Son; nor have you given anything to it, and much less right to it, neither the devil in the kingdom of heaven, nor should you master it, nor teach it, nor rebuke it. For it is God's punishment, not man's; he will have it unchastened, but commanded; wait for your office, and let God be pleased with his rule before he teaches you. You are not one who can suffer a stranger to deprive him of his servant or to drive him away when he cannot spare him. Indeed, there is no shepherd boy so lowly as to suffer a crooked word from a foreign master; only God's yours should and must be everyone's servant and suffer everything from everyone, whereas nothing from him, not even God's own word, can or will be suffered. Let this admonition be well understood, which I mean faithfully. For it is God's admonition. But if you will not listen, nor mend your ways, we must let you go, and yet see how we can resist the devil, at least so far that we do not burden our conscience with your sins,

nor become the devil's will in them. We must not put you under ban; you are doing yourselves in, for we would gladly and much rather have you out. And even if you could get another pastor (which is still a long way off), you still cannot become Christians, nor be partakers of some Christian grace and life; no one will accept it against the will and command of the visitators. And who would want to be among such denied Christians, who would have such evil cries that they have driven out their pastor by force and injustice, and yet want to be called Christians and bear such a name with shame? This would make you a good name in all the world and make you a good example. Finally, I counsel you in Christ to be on friendly terms with your pastor and live kindly with him; let him punish, teach and comfort you as God has commanded him and as his conscience dictates; as it is written to the Hebrews in v. 17: 'Obey your teachers and follow them, for they watch over your souls as those who are to give an account of them, so that they may do this with joy and not with groaning, for this is not good for you. For what you are about to do is a wicked example, that any magistrate, judge or councilman should want to expel a pastor at his own whim, when he has no right, justification or reason to do so; God will not and cannot suffer it. God grant that you may not find out." (Serious Scripture, that a righteous pastor should not be deprived of his office because he severely punishes public vices. X, 1898-1902 [StL 10, 1624-1629])

Joh. Gerhard writes:

"If someone has not been deposed by a lawful judicial process, nor for lawful and sufficient causes, as for example because of his zeal in punishing the vices of the people, because of private hatred, because of minor oversights, etc., then the one who is put in his place is not to be considered a true, lawfully appointed church minister." (Loc. de minist. § 175.)

Note 2: Our theologians testify in many ways that it is contrary to God's Word if a congregation makes a contract in advance with the person to be called that he should only administer the office for one or more years and then be dismissed. Thus Jerome Kromayer writes:

"The ministry of preaching

cannot be conferred by the one who calls in the manner of a contract for a certain number of years or with the reservation of the freedom to dismiss the freely called again; because the one who calls is nowhere granted or permitted by God the power to make such a contract; therefore neither the one who calls nor the one to be called can consider such a vocation and mission to be divine." (Theol. posit. 11, 530.)

Ludnzig Hartmann also writes:

"This also includes the controversial question of whether someone can pledge his service or ministry to the church for a certain number of years. We say no: 1. because such an appointment boldly prescribes a time limit to God, who appoints, after the expiry of which he wishes to take leave of the church, however it may behave; just as it is not the business of a legate to prescribe to his master how long he should represent him. 2. because carnal counsels are involved, which should be far removed here; for such a one thinks that if the matter does not turn out according to his heart's desire, if there are still treasures to be gathered or many adversities to be endured, then he will easily wind his way out of these labyrinths. 3. for the sake of many disadvantages: for if the fidelity of a pastor were very agreeable to the church, it would suddenly be deprived of it; also because, as is well known, the church's goods are greatly diminished by this frequent change. If it be further asked, whether it is lawful to call a minister of the word on the certain condition of how long, so that, if the patron will no longer hear and tolerate the pastor, he must go away and wander to another place, I answer, we are God's ministers, and this office is God's, to which we are called by God, though through men; this holy work must therefore be treated in a holy manner, but not according to human caprice. Men can hire a shepherd and a cowherd for a time, and if their service is no longer pleasing, they can dismiss them at a certain time, but not always if they wish: but to act in this way with a shepherd of souls is not in any man's power. Nor can the minister of the Word himself accept the sacred office in such a way, if he does not

want to become a hireling. Certainly those who would be so called would not perform the ministry diligently and faithfully, but would become flatterers and say what pleases people, or they would have to be prepared hourly that the ministry would be recited to them." (Pastorale evangelicum, p. 104.)

Note 3: That the congregation should not seek to remove its preacher immediately for mere weaknesses in doctrine or life, but should admonish him in humility and kindness, is testified in the following. Thus it says in our confession:

"It is also easy for riots to form when the people want to master and expose the bishops' or preachers' conduct and life in the quickest possible way, or when they soon tire of the preachers, for example because of a minor ailment; much great evil follows. Soon, because of the same bitterness, people look for other teachers and other preachers. Again, perfection and unity are preserved, that is, the church remains undivided and whole, if the strong tolerate and bear the weak, if the people also have patience with their preachers, if the bishops and preachers in turn know how to make amends to the people for all kinds of weaknesses and infirmities." (Apology, Art. 4.)

Further down it says:

"If people are to be or remain in unity with each other, whether in the church or in secular government, they must not weigh all their infirmities against each other on the gold scale, they must let each other go over with the water almost as much as possible and always keep good, as much as possible fraternal patience with each other." (op. cit.)

This includes what Luther writes in 1 Cor. 15:10:

"A pious Christian does not do so, but, even if he hears something wrong being preached, he proceeds with humility and admonishes the preacher in a friendly and brotherly way, does not defy and rebuke in the same way." (VIII, 1193.)

Furthermore, Gerhard writes:

"It is ungodly, if one knows that someone's opinion is godly and healthy, to make error out of uncomfortably spoken words." (Loc. th. de bonis opp. § 38.)

Note 4 On the causes of the deposition of a preacher, J. Gerhard et al. writes the following:

"If a minister either falls into heresy or

gives offense by serious crimes, and causes the name of God to be blasphemed, then the church has power to depose him. For there are three kinds of ministers of the church: 1. some are impious (i.e. deceitful), who deal deceitfully and falsify the word of God, 2 Cor. 4:2, Ephes. 4:14, who obscure the light of heavenly doctrine with heretical mists and falsify the purity of it; these the church not only can, but must also remove from herself as a curse, Gal. 1:8, that is, as a detestable thing stained by offense; such were those of whom the apostle wishes them to be cut off, Gal. 5:12, and whom John forbids to open their houses and ears, 2 John 10; 2. some are onerous, whose crimes are both grave and manifest, who are also to be deprived of their office, lest for their sake the name of God be blasphemed, and lest they furthermore give offense to the hearers. 3. But others are morose (i.e. morose), subject to lesser infirmities, who are to be tolerated." (Loc. de minist. § 174.)

Note 5: On the proper process for the removal of a preacher, Gerhard writes:

"In the removal of ministers, if it is to be a lawful one, both a careful investigation of the matter and a diligent observation of a lawful process are required; 1 Tim. 5:19: 'Against an elder take up no case except with two and three witnesses'. For it is not to be allowed to the rashness of the people or the arbitrariness of the rulers to remove a minister from his position and drive him into misery without a lawful investigation and examination of the matter. This investigation of the matter belongs to the whole Church." (Loc. de minist. § 175.)

Similarly, Chemnitz writes:

"Even in our congregations many do not understand this matter properly. For just as someone who hires a servant has the power to dismiss him when he wishes, so some think that they also have the power to dismiss a preacher, even if they have no just cause. There are also some who think that no preacher can move from one place to another with a clear conscience. But just as God alone reserves to himself the right to call, even if

the appointment is made by means, so it is also God's business to dismiss or remove someone from the ministry. Therefore, as long as God tolerates his servant, who teaches rightly and lives blamelessly, in office, the church has no power to remove the foreign servant. But if he no longer edifies the church, neither by teaching nor by life, but destroys it, then God himself removes him, Hos. 4, 6. 1 Sam. 2, 30.... And then not only can the church, but it should also remove such a person from office. For God, as He calls, so He also deposes by means, but as the calling is according to the instruction of the Lord of the harvest, so also the church, if one is to be removed from office, must be able to prove with certainty that this is the judgment and that this is the will of God. And as the appointment, so also the dismissal concerns the whole church in a certain order." (Loc. de eccl. p. 331.)

Note 6: It is all the more necessary that the congregation, if it does not have several preachers of its own, should consult orthodox ministers of the church for the proper exercise of the deposition process, since otherwise it would incur the reproach of wanting to be both party and judge. Therefore, when the people of Zwickau had deposed one of their preachers without the consent of their pastor, Luther wrote to the latter:

"But because your council, driven by the evil spirit, is now offending the preacher at St. Catherine's, neither suing him before any judge nor denouncing him for some wrongdoing, but doing so on its own authority and outrage as frenzied people and real church robbers (not of bodily goods, but of the office and honor of the Holy Spirit) and being both party and judge in the same matter, it will not suffer in any way that you should remain silent or consent to it.." (Admonition to a pastor that he should not remain silent about the unjust dismissal of a preacher, dated 1531. X, 1892 [StL 10, 1620])

It is self-evident here that, first, if it becomes evident to the congregation that their preacher is presenting fundamental errors or is falling into sin and disgrace, they have the right to suspend him immediately, and secondly, that, if they are deserted by righteous preachers, they can also carry out the deposition alone; as Luther writes:

"Perhaps they

<p. 149>

will disgrace you before the simple-minded mob and otherwise unintelligent, as they have not yet been disgraced by the church" (i.e. in the sense of the papists). i.e. in the sense of the papists, by the bishops) "for wolves and false teachers" (that is, declared so by a judicial judgment), "but are held to be true Christians. Yes, indeed, this is wise and well said: if the sheep were not to flee from the wolves until the wolves were called to flee by their Christian concilium and public judgment, then the sheepfold would soon be empty and the shepherd would find neither milk, cheese, butter, wool, meat, nor a hoof in one day; that would be called the sheep herded! What then has Christ our Lord done when he calls and commands us to beware of wolves without waiting for the wolves' concilium? Not only the whole flock of sheep, but also each sheep for itself alone, has the right and power to flee from the wolves wherever it can, just as John 10:5 says: "My sheep flee from strangers." (Example of the consecration of a true Christian bishop, 1542. XVII, 140. [StL 17, 102 f.])

Note 7: Finally, it should be noted that the inability to administer the office does not entitle the congregation to dismiss a preacher, but it does entitle them to remove him from office. Thus Paul Tarnov writes:

"The causes of dismissal can be traced back to two sources; one is the lack of ability to serve the church with usefulness, the other of will or an unpleasant neglect of the works of the ministry. By lack of ability we understand, on the one hand, falsifications of sound doctrine, especially in fundamental articles, or errors that conflict with the foundation of the faith; on the other hand, the deprivation of the instruments to teach it to the hearers usefully and with fruit, which may be the use of the powers of the soul, as of the intellect, the power of judgment, the memory, or the body, as of the tongue and other members necessary for the administration of the ministry, hindered by illness or accident. By will we understand both an excessive negligence in the performance of the parts of the office conferred, as well as shamefulness of life and morals, whether the same be annoying in the church alone, or condemnable in civil life." (Thesaurus consil. by Dedekennus, II, 917.)

§ 32.

Finally, the congregation should also see to it that neither it as a congregation nor any individual member enters into ecclesiastical union with unbelievers or unbelievers and thus makes himself guilty of religious, ecclesiastical, and religious mania. 2 Cor. 6:14-18: "Do not pull on the yoke of others with unbelievers. For what enjoyment has righteousness with unrighteousness? what fellowship has light with darkness? How does Christ agree with Belial? or what part does the believer have with the unbeliever? How is the temple of God like the idols? But ye are the temple of the living God: as God saith, I will dwell in them, and walk in them, and will be their God, and they shall be my people. Wherefore come out from among them, and be ye separate, saith the LORD, and touch no unclean thing: and I will receive you, and will be your Father, and ye shall be my sons and daughters, saith the LORD Almighty." 2 John 10:11: "If anyone comes to you and does not bring this teaching, do not take him home or greet him. For whoever greets him makes himself partaker of his evil works." Cf. Revelation 14:9-11.

Note: This includes communal worship with people of other faiths, patronage of irreligious churches, leaving church buildings to irreligious people, participation in religious associations to achieve ecclesiastical purposes, etc. Our Confession testifies to this. Thus it says in the Apology of the Augsb. Confession:

"But one should not accept or listen to false teachers, for they are no longer in Christ's place, but are against Christ." (Art. 8.)

Further in the Smalc. Articles:

"Since this is the case, all Christians should be most careful not to partake of

such ungodly teaching, blasphemy and unrighteous rage, but should depart from the pope and his associates or followers, as from the kingdom of Antichrist, and curse it, as Christ has commanded: Beware of the false prophet. And Paul commands that false preachers should be avoided and cursed as an abomination. And in 2 Corinthians 6 he says: "Do not pull on the yoke of a stranger with unbelievers, for what fellowship has light with darkness? It is hard to separate oneself from so many countries and people and to lead a special doctrine; but here is God's command that everyone should beware and not be of one mind with those who lead unrighteous doctrine or who intend to maintain it with fierceness." (Appendix 1.)

Finally, the Formula of Concord states:

"As also among the right free adiaphora or middle things are not to be reckoned such ceremonies, which have the appearance or, thereby to avoid persecution, want to give the appearance, as if our religion were not far from the papist one, or were not highly opposed to it, or, or, if such ceremonies were meant, required or accepted, as if both repugnant religions were thereby and thereby compared and made one corpus, or again an admission to the papacy and a departure from the pure doctrine of the Gospel and true religion should take place or be made leisurely. For in this case what Paul writes in 2 Cor. 6 should and must apply: "Do not be yoked together with unbelievers; what fellowship does light have with darkness? Wherefore come out from among them, and be ye separate, saith the Lord." (Thor. Decl., X, pars. 5, 6 [German text])

Thus Luther also writes:

"And in sum, that I come from this piece, it is frightful to me to hear that in one and the same church or at one and the same altar both parts should fetch and receive one and the same sacrament, and one part should believe that it receives vain bread and wine, but the other part should believe that it receives the true body and blood of Christ. And I often doubt whether it is to be believed that a preacher or pastor could be so obdurate and malicious and remain silent about this, and let both parts go along, each in their own delusion that they are receiving one sacrament, each according to their faith, etc. But if there is one who has a heart harder than stone,

steel and diamond, he must certainly be an apostle of wrath. Therefore, anyone who has such preachers, or who confesses to them, be warned against them as against the devil himself in the flesh." (Warning to the people of Frankfurt, XVII, 2446. [StL 17, 2016])

Furthermore, Luther writes in his interpretation of the 110th Psalm:

"There are now some clever people who have begun to patch things up, who want to advise and settle the quarrel, who say that one should give way and yield on both sides. We let them do what they can and try what they can, but if they make the devil pious and one with Christ, they are the first. But I think it is the same with such mending (as Jesus Sirach says in 22 v. 7) as if one wanted to mend broken pieces. And though there have been many shoemakers who have undertaken it, they have also worked in vain and lost both wire and stitch." (V, 1420. [StL 5, 977])

Elsewhere Luther writes:

"But may a good-hearted man (as it is called) say: what harm is there in keeping God's Word and leaving all or some of these (erroneous) pieces, even if they were tolerable, in addition? I answer, they may be called good-hearted people, but they are false-hearted and deceptive-hearted people; for you hear that it is not possible to teach something else besides God's Word, to serve another besides God, to light another besides the light which God has set in darkness. It is certainly falsehood and error, even if it were some of it; for the church should not and cannot teach lies or error, not even in some of it; if it teaches a lie, it is completely false. Life may well be sinful and unrighteous, indeed it is unfortunately all too unrighteous; but the doctrine must be absolutely right and certainly without sin. Therefore nothing must be preached in the church but only the certain, pure and only Word of God. Where this is lacking, it is no longer the church, but the devil's school." (Against Hans Wurst of 1541. XVII, 1684. 1686. [StL 17, 1343 f.])

Thus Balduin also writes:

"It is the duty of a pious and steadfast confessor to guard the support of sound doctrine, of which he is convinced in his conscience, in such a way that it is in no way defiled, diminished or lost altogether; and this can happen precisely through careless dealings with those who

are opposed to the true religion. Therefore, as much as it is incumbent upon us, we are to abstain from their worship, so that we may not, on the one hand, cause ourselves any trouble, and on the other hand, not appear to hold together with the adversaries to the annoyance of the weak. Some people sin against this apostolic exhortation in two ways. First of all, some join in those unholy services of their own accord, without anyone commanding them to do so, either because they are Epicureans, who do not care about any religion and do not care which community they join; or because they seek a little profit, which they see that they could not obtain if they did not keep company with others in religion. But these sin with great difficulty, since there is but one faith, of which every one ought to be sure in his heart; but whatever is done with a wavering conscience is sin, Rom. 14, and in matters of religion shameful gain is to be entirely fled from. Such limp on both sides; and if they are not complete Epicureans, yet they are the least lukewarm in religion, awaiting a severe judgment from the Lord. Secondly, there are those who feign the command of the authorities. But in two cases it can happen that one can be present at the worship of unbelievers or heretics without violating his conscience: 1. to look at it once, if one comes to look at it only so that he can bear better and more certain witness to the ungodly nature of the unbelievers or heretics. Thus travelers visit the synagogues of the Jews, are present at the papist mass, watch the processions of the same, in order to be eyewitnesses themselves of what they would hardly have believed if they had told it to others. But great caution is necessary here, lest it should appear that we hold with the adversaries; therefore we must abstain from the gestures of the adversaries, not fall down on our knees, not beat our breasts, as the papists do when mass is held; but then our disagreement is rather to be openly recognized by some sign, although we should not express our attitude against that worship of the adversaries by laughter or by any other

public sign of contempt. Where it is not permitted to show one's disagreement publicly, it is better to stay away from such services, lest we either trouble our conscience or put ourselves in danger with the adversaries.
