Hypocritical dissimulation has no place here, but is rather offensive to God and man; for no one can serve two masters; either he will hate one and love the other, or he will hold to one and despise the other, Matt. 6:24. Thus Paul entered the idolatrous temple at Athens, but he did not approve of idolatry, but therefore took occasion to teach the idolaters better, Acts 17:22-23. But it is necessary that he who, without danger to his conscience, wishes to be present at the worship of the heretics, if only to see it once, should above all be quite sure that what he sees and hears there is contrary to true worship.... 2. Furthermore, it is permitted to be present at the worship practices of the heretics if a secular office requires it of us, 2 Kings 5:18." (De cas. consc. p. 196 — 98.)

Concerning patronage, the so-called General Articles, which are found in Prince Augustus' Church Order, state

"Because it also happens that some persons, who in the time of the papists still adhered to superstition in many things, when asked for paternity, are aborted at holy baptism; thereby embittering them all the more against the pure teaching of the Holy Gospel; so that, on the other hand, when they are permitted, by the operation of God the Holy Spirit, not only themselves, but also other more deceived persons are converted; therefore the ordinance of godparenthood is not a divine command, but is ordained by men for good and substantial reasons: the pastors and ministers of the church should act reasonably and cautiously in such a case, and should not soon prevent anyone who is not a public blasphemer of God and his word, even if he could not be found in one or more articles in due time, from being baptized, but should remember Christ's

saying when he said: He that is with us is not against us, to be content at the first time with this, that such persons by their presence recognize our holy baptism as Christian and right; so, on the other hand, to advise none of our religion's kinsmen that they should stand at a papist baptism, and hereby confirm their papist abomination, which they do at holy baptism." (Churf. August Kirchenordnung von 1580. p. 308. 309.)

Dannhauer writes:

"Syncretism is subject to ecclesiastical censure, whether it exists in communion with the heresy (which the Council of Laodicea condemns to exclusion in the 32nd and 33rd canons). and 33rd Canon: That one should not pray together with heretics and schismatics); or in civil and matrimonial, by virtue of which a Christian unites himself with a Jewish, Turkish, pagan woman." (Tractat. de cas. consc. I, 1126.)

Concerning the permission for unbelievers to hold their services in Lutheran church buildings, the Lutheran theological faculty at Wittenberg gave the following judgment in the 1st of 1650:

"For our part, we believe that neither you, nor the preacher in Buch, can permit with the will of authority and conscience that in the Buch church, in which until now the pure Lutheran doctrine has always been taught unhindered without any mixture or alternation, at the same time another repugnant, erroneous doctrine, which is condemned in God's Word, even in our symbolic books, and which overturns the foundation of faith, is publicly preached, practiced and propagated to the descendants. For 1. such a thing is straightforwardly contrary to the word of God, for it is not hidden what the apostle 2 John 10-11. judges of it. But if a false teacher is not to be received into one's house, much less will one be able to grant him the church, which is God's house and much holier than any other dwelling place. Nor should we greet him, but abstain from his fellowship as much as possible, so that we may not partake of his evil works, for how can we allow ourselves to be bound together with him in one church, at one pulpit and altar? ... According to this, 2. such acceptance of false teachers is also entirely contrary to the honor of God, the sanctification of his name,

the propagation of his word. For through false teaching God's honor is violated, his name is profaned, his b. Word is falsified and perverted. . Should such things be permitted in the house that is sanctified to the name of God!  3. Nor can any Christian who directly or indirectly cooperates, abets, consents, approves, or even, as much as can be done by Christian, permissible means, does not resist and prevent, free his conscience; indeed, he cannot rightly pray an Our Father, but must pray against himself and by his own prayer accuse himself daily to God. For how can a man pray that God's name may be sanctified, his word and kingdom spread, and all riots and offences averted, if he allows that by which God's name is profaned, etc.? Fourthly 4., it will also be very annoying to manly, especially simple-minded people..... It is also 5. a very dangerous and harmful work, for this would not only strengthen and harden the enemies of divine truth in their error, but would also give them cause to continue their habit, if not publicly, then secretly, and even to take over entire churches. Experience proves that the Calvinists, once they have been taken over, afterwards devour like cancer and even drive the host out of the house, as their own practice in Holland, the Palatinate, Hungary, Transylvania, Anhalt, Bremen, formerly in Danzig in various churches and other places shows. Nor has it ever been heard that the Calvinists were permitted to do such things, unless they had slipped in unnoticed under the pretext of the Augsburg Confession or were otherwise ecclesia pressa (a church deprived of its freedom), since it could not be done in any way. Finally, it should also be said that it should only be regarded as temporary (ad interim). But now it is very doubtful whether it will be discontinued and abolished so soon afterward; and because it is not permissible in itself, but rather annoying and contrary to the word of God, it cannot be seen as a favor to any man, even if it were to last as long as it wanted to. The apostle Paul would not give way to his adversaries for an hour, and that in the midst of things that are in Christian liberty, Gal. 2:4." (Consil. Witeberg. I, 493-95.)

D.

Fourth section.

On the exercise of a congregation's duty to take care of its members also in regard to earthly matters.

§ 33.

First, the congregation should see to it as far as possible that the preacher has food, clothing and lodging for himself (including a room for study and undisturbed intercourse with those under his charge) and his own, Matt. 10:9-10: "You shall not have gold, silver, or brass in your belts, nor a bag for traveling, nor two coats, nor shoes, nor a staff. For a laborer is worthy of his food"; furthermore, that the preacher has the means to show himself hospitable, 1 Tim. 3:2: "But a bishop should be blameless, a woman's husband, sober, temperate, sedate, hospitable, teachable," cf. Tit. 1:8; furthermore, that the preacher can feed on the gospel alone, 1 Cor. 9:14: "So also the Lord has commanded that those who preach the gospel should nourish themselves on the gospel"; finally, that the preacher may stop to read, cultivate fellowship, and not involve himself in the business of food, 1 Tim. 4:13: "Stop to read." 2 Tim. 2:3-4: "Suffer thyself to be a good soldier for Jesus Christ. No man of war entangles himself in the affairs of food, that he may please him who received him." Sir 38:26-27: "How can he wait for teaching who must plow, and who loves to drive the oxen with the scourge, and to do such work, and knows nothing but to talk of oxen? He must think how to plow, and must feed the cows late and early." To the same extent, this also applies to the teachers of the simple-minded.

Note: Sebastian Schwan has made a collection of testimonies from Luther's and Runge's writings on this subject, in which it says, among other things:

"It is not good to talk, preach or write much about this. But what should one do? Should this doctrine of the maintenance and remuneration of preachers be completely concealed and buried? That would be to despise the truth and the preaching office; for such a doctrine belongs to the third commandment of God; and since there should be no maintenance in the ministry and office of the preachers, much would be deterred from it, and thus the church of God would be noticeably harmed. But if one should say something about it and report something about the salary of the preachers in churches and schools, one stirs up a lazy crowd and finds many godless people who hate, envy and resent the preacher because they would rather rob and take away with Heliodoro, Zuliano and Phoca than with Constantino, who made many foundations, ... to use something for God's glory. But both the Old and New Testaments testify that the Lord God wants his servants who perform the divine service to be honest and well provided for.... The priests of the Old Testament had a good livelihood and an ample income. This is also repeated in the New Testament. For our Arch Shepherd and Bishop of our souls, the Lord Jesus Christ, Himself says in Matt. 10[:10]: A laborer is worthy of his meat. About which words Martin Luther thus writes: ‘Here it is well to note that the ministers of the Word are due food and clothing by divine command; and at the present time those who want to be Christians, and yet give nothing at all to the church ministers, or steal and take from them what is given to them by others, like our peasants, nobles and burghers, etc., sin with difficulty. These same journeymen should give an account to him who says: A laborer is worth his wages; but these deprive him of his wages who is worth his wages and who deserves them’. Secondly, note also that the Lord Christ does not only want the servants of the Word to be given food and drink, but also wages and salaries, so that he can provide them with other necessities. For he must also have clothes and whatever else he needs. In the epistles

of St. Paul, this is repeated many times and urged that those who work in the Word of God should be well kept, honestly paid and nourished, and also given charity. Although many unnamed ... recognize it as no work what faithful preachers do with reading, praying, writing, preaching, visiting the sick, accompanying prisoners; in doing so they often either have to forfeit their lives or are in extreme danger of their lives at times. Indeed, so strongly is it practised in St. Paul's writings, and so diligently is it repeated, that Dr. Martin Luther himself at first wondered about St. Paul, how he could have thought of doing this so seriously. For these are his words on St. Paul's saying to the Galatians, chapter 6[:6 ff.]: 'I remember the time when I wondered why St. Paul so earnestly commanded the Christian churches to feed their teachers and preachers. For I saw in the papacy that everyone was contributing abundantly and with great heaps and giving with both hands to build churches and monasteries; again, that the clergy were only superfluously supplied with interest and income and were increasing. Hence it is that the dignities, glory and goods of the bishops and clergy have risen and increased to such an extent that they have almost taken over the best part of the best cities and lands in Christendom. Therefore I thought it would have been without need that St. Paul should have commanded something of this, because all kinds of superfluous things were given to the clergy and immediately showered upon them. Indeed, I thought that it would have been more necessary to prevent people from giving excessively than to continue to exhort them to give. For I noted that they did no more with their excessive giving than that they made the clergy more and more stingy, etc. But now that I see the reason why the clergy had so much superfluity in the past, and now the poor pastors and preachers have hardly enough of the dear bread, I am never surprised. In times past, when they preached vain error and lies, the devotion of the people was so great and the giving so much that St. Peter's patrimony, that is, inheritance (but he says Acts 3:6: He had neither silver nor gold — how did he come by it?) and the goods of the churches were so well improved

that the pope became emperor, the cardinals and bishops kings and princes of the world. But after the light of the holy Gospel had risen again at this time, the faithful and pious preachers are just as rich as Christ and his disciples were. Dr. Luther writes this and more, and finally concludes with these serious words: 'It is impossible for true Christians to suffer that their pastors should have need and want. But if they not only suffer such things, but laugh in their fist and take hearty pleasure in them when they are in a bad way, and do not give them their due, or, where they must give it, they do it with reluctance and wish them all misfortune in return, it is certain that they are worse than heathens and Turks. In the New Testament, then, this is one of the main sayings of the Christian teachers and preachers Gal. 6: He who is taught with the word shares all good things with him who teaches him. Make no mistake, God is not mocked. To this belong the other sayings 1 Tim. 5; 1 Cor. 9. This detailed and earnest sermon of St. Paul serves against the foolishness and barrenness of those who think to take the Christian teachers to task. To realize then how powerful St. Paul bases his advocacy of the honest maintenance of godly teachers and church ministers, including school servants, and proves it from the command and order of God in the Old and New Testaments, from natural law, indeed, he confirms it with the reason and understanding of every right-minded person, which is called sound reason, and finally he gives evidence from common equity; which is all directed to show that it is rightly necessary and just that the Christian church and congregation should provide a good livelihood for its ministers of the word of God. But what and how much should be given to the ministers, teachers and preachers of the divine word for their sustenance cannot be described and measured by law without the law of righteousness and love. What is right, what is necessary, honest and according to Christian love and equity, that will be given to the servants of Christ.

For just as there is a distinction in the gifts, likewise in the profession or state and the work, since God the Lord Himself has distinguished the persons of the church ministers according to a certain measure and order, it is therefore not unfair that a certain order should be made in wages, salary and maintenance and that a proper distinction should be made. But the law of love requires that each man should be given as much as is necessary for the honest maintenance of himself and his family, especially his household. And this is so that such a servant of Christ may not become impatient and negligent in his profession, office and work through lack and poverty in his household or through famine, and that he may hardly be able to clothe, maintain and support himself and his children, or be caused by such distress to seek and acquire other means of sustenance, or to engage in worldly trades, merchandising and the like. Since at one time and in one place there are many other opportunities than at another, and food and housekeeping become more difficult from day to day, it is just that godly rulers of the churches should also be mindful of this and take account of such circumstances in determining and paying the salaries of Christian teachers and church ministers." (Dedekennus' Thesaurus. 11, 827-30.)

Regarding the teachers in the schools, Porta writes in his Pastorale Lutheri:

"It is also necessary, where the superiors do not do it themselves, that the schoolmasters and school servants are well and honestly maintained from the common class. For the schools are the sweet and beautiful fountains of the holy city of God, as St. David calls them in Psalm 46, from which all welfare must flow and come in the government. And where the schools have to be staffed with inexperienced and unlearned people because of their low maintenance and pay, and thus often have to be withdrawn and retracted, the youth is neglected and such damage occurs that cannot be compensated with any money or good. Therefore, more should be spent on schools than is unfortunately done with great harm and ruin. In addition to the boys' schools, there should also be girls' schoolmistresses in Christian communities, who should also be given a hand from the common fund, especially for the sake of

poor people's children who cannot pay school fees." (p. 1119. 1120.)

In his admonition to the pastors to preach against usury, Luther says what is meant by food and clothing. He writes:

"If we have food and clothing, we should be content. This is said to all Christians, both rich and poor. The reason is this, he says in v. 7: "We have brought nothing into the world, no doubt we will bring nothing out. A prince has food and coverings for his person, and can consume nothing more for his person; he must leave the rest behind him, just as a citizen, peasant and beggar.... His house, castle, land, clothes, and all that is his covering; food, drink, wine, beer is his food. For food here does not mean horse feed, nor a blanket a stable or sack, but every man's need according to his station, with all his goods." (X, 1055. [StL 10, 884 f.])

§ 34.

The congregation is also to provide food, clothing, housing, and all other necessities for the poor, widows, orphans, the elderly, and the infirm, who can neither provide these things for themselves nor have relatives who are especially indebted to them (2 Thess. 3:11-12: "For we have heard that there are some among you who walk disorderly and do not work, but rather labor in pride. But such we command and admonish through our Lord Jesus Christ, that they work quietly and eat their own bread." 1 Timothy 5:16: "But if any believer has widows, let him provide for them, and let not the church be burdened, that those who are widows may have enough." Cf. 1 John 3:17; Matt. 25:35, 36, 40, 42, 43, 46; James 1:27); also in the case of special calamities, fire, famine, famine, robbery, etc., the church should take care of those who are in need (2 Corinthians 8:13-14: "It is not for the

purpose of giving others rest and you affliction, but that it may be the same. Let your abundance therefore serve their want this [precious] time, that their abundance also may afterward serve your want, and that the same thing may come to pass." Romans 12:15: "Rejoice with those who rejoice and weep with those who weep." 1 Cor. 12:26: "And if one member suffers, all the members suffer with it; and if one member is kept glorious, all the members rejoice with it"); so that no brother or sister may be tempted to appeal to the mercy of those who are outside to dishonor the gospel, or even to join with them in secret societies that have the purpose of support as their figurehead. (1 Thess. 4:11-12: "Strive to be quiet, to do your own work, and to labor with your own hands, as we have commanded you, that you may walk uprightly toward those who are without, having no need of them.") The church was to appoint special almoners for this purpose. Acts 6, 1-7.

Note 1: Compare the testimonies from Luther under § 8. He also writes in his writing: Von des christlichen Standes Besserung vom J. 1520:

"It is probably one of the greatest needs that all begging be abolished in all Christendom; no one among the Christians should ever go begging; it would also be easy to make an order about it, if we had the courage and seriousness to do so, namely that every city provided for its poor people and did not allow any foreign beggars, they could be called whatever they wanted, it would be forest brothers or begging orders. Any town could ever feed its own; and if it were too small, the people in the surrounding villages would be exhorted to give as well. Otherwise they would have to feed so many country bumpkins and bad boys under the beggar's name; so one could also know which were truly poor or not. So there should be a steward or guardian who would know all the poor

<pages164>

and tell the council or parish priest what they need, or how this could best be arranged.... But that some think that the poor are not well provided for in this way and that not so large stone houses and monasteries are built, nor so abundantly, I almost certainly believe. But it is not necessary. He who wants to be poor should not be rich; but if he wants to be rich, let him take his hand to the plow and pluck it out of the earth himself. It is enough that the poor be fairly provided for, so that they do not die of hunger or freeze to death. It is not fitting that one should go idle at another's work, be rich and live comfortably in another's misery, as is now the case with perverse abuse. For St. Paul says in 2 Thess. 3:10: He who does not work should not eat. No one is ordained by God to live from other people's goods, but only the preaching and ruling priests (as St. Paul says in 1 Cor. 9:14) for their spiritual work; as Christ also says to the apostles in Luke 10:7: "Every worker is worthy of his reward." (X, 367. 368. [StL 10, 326 f.])

Thus Porta also writes in his instruction for pastors:

"If it is asked: to whom or to which people one should primarily give, you have sufficient teaching and instruction on this in Holy Scripture. For in the 5th book of Moses, chapter 15, it says thus: 'If any of your brothers is poor in any city in your land, the LORD your God will give to you. Here it should be noted that the Lord God says through Moses: "If anyone in your city or your land becomes poor, you shall open your hand and give to him. Therefore the church also sings: Open your hand of kindness to the poor in your land, etc. For with this he wants to command us especially to those poor who are among us, as the sick and infirm, or who cannot earn their bread because of their age or impossibility, or if there are any house-poor, spoiled craftsmen and workers who have not drunk, eaten, gambled away or wantonly killed their own, or have fallen into damage and accident because of special misfortune, even if they work, cannot get away and therefore have to suffer hardship. Again, poor widows, orphans, poor diligent students, who have a good testimony from their preceptoribus, should be given to them in particular."

(Pastorale Lutheri. Edited by Crämer. p. 1082. 1083.)

Further below, the same Porta writes:

"Among these are also those who have served faithfully in the sacred preaching office for a long time and are now no longer able to administer it due to age or infirmity. For they are also to be maintained, as the Lord God himself commanded, that from the fiftieth year the Levites should be free from the office of the ministry, and should no longer serve, but wait for the service of their brethren (that is, attend them with good counsel as tried and trained men) in the tabernacle of the congregation, but they are not to maintain the ministry. 4 Mos. 8 Now if in the Old Testament the old Levites were relieved of their office and yet were left with the others and entertained, why should we Christians not do the same kindness to old emeriti and well-deserving preachers and ministers? For it would be a great shameful ingratitude to cast them out in their old age and beat them into the grass like old horses or dogs, as nevertheless happens now and then with great annoyance." (op. cit. 1119.)

Concerning the survivors of a preacher, the Lower Saxon Church Order contains the following:

"So then it is also Christian, right and just that after the death of the poor pastors their miserable, sorrowful, wretched widows with their abandoned, highly sorrowful, fatherless children may be under roof and have a dwelling; God would certainly not refrain from incessant punishments where Christian providence should not happen in this: so out of gracious paternal will and love, which we bear towards God, his holy word, sacrament, ministry, also pastors and your own, we hereby command and order that every town and parish in the whole principality in a convenient place ... shall build a dwelling house with a fair yard and a cabbage garden, in which such a sorely afflicted poor widow preacher shall have a free dwelling during her widowhood, together with her miserable children, and shall also have the same enjoyment as others of the common pasture, grazing, fattening, and poor firing. Such houses, like the other church buildings in towns and villages, are to be diligently maintained by the churchwardens and church musicians.

Also to such highly afflicted wretched widows' dwellings shall be given from the pastor's fields and meadows for cheap annual interest, or also from the church or chapel land, so that they may have their bread and housekeeping all the better. And the pastor has nothing to deny himself in this respect, as his wife and children can also be expected and enjoy the same. However, as long as there are no widows, the churchwardens shall rent out such a dwelling and use the rent for the best interests of the church and other buildings. However, since there may be two widows, the youngest widow shall (freely) stay with other people and live in the widow's house after the old widow has died. After the death of their blessed lord, these widows shall also have and receive their dwelling in the parsonage for one year and their salary for the whole year unhindered, and the neighboring parish priests shall administer the office and place of the deceased for as long as this period of grace lasts." (Dedekennus' Thesaurus. II, 833. 834.)

Note 2: An older testimony against joining secret societies from our church can be found in a decree of the Hanoverian Consistory to the superintendents from 1745, in which it reads, among other things, as follows:

"We hereby give you to understand the extent to which a certain preacher in this country undertook to join the so-called Masonic Society. But just as a preacher is not at all entitled to do something which in itself might be indifferent (a middle ground) if it causes annoyance or offense to the congregation entrusted to him or to others, but he is bound by Holy Scripture and his conscience to refrain from such things: he is least of all authorized to associate himself with a society whose laws and statutes he does not know and understand beforehand, with oaths or other obligations binding his conscience; even though it may be pretended that the noblest obligation of the society consists in a vinculo caritatis (bond of charity), since Christians have such a strong vinculum caritatis in Holy Scripture that they need no other: so this preacher is not only emphatically

reprimanded for his proceedings, but also ordered to disassociate himself from such a society and to renounce the customary practices." (Acta hist.-eccles. vol. IX, pp. 404. 405.)

§ 35.

The congregation is to prevent its members from being without the necessary help, care by day and night, and refreshment in sickness, Matt. 25:36: "I was sick, and you visited me." Cf. v. 43. 1 Tim. 5:10: "If she has ministered to the afflicted."

Note: Porta imposes the office of nursing on all those who receive care from the community, and writes:

"If a citizen in the city would wish to nurse a brother or sister (in the care home) of a sick person, whether in the event of death or otherwise, no one who is able to do so should refuse. However, if someone would refuse to do so, he or she shall be severely and duly punished by the head of the house." (Pastorale Lutheri, p. 1128.)

§ 36.

The congregation must make arrangements for every member of its community, even the poorest, to be buried properly, honestly and in a Christian manner, Matthew 14:12: "Then his (John's) disciples came and took his body and buried it." Acts 8:2: "But men who feared God sent for Stephanum and made a great lamentation over him." Jer 22:18-19: "Therefore thus saith the LORD concerning Jehoiakim the son of Josiah king of Judah, They shall not lament over him, O brother, O sister! He shall not be lamented, O Lord, O noble one! He shall be buried like an ass, dragged and thrown outside the gates of Jerusalem." Tob. 1:20 [or Tobit 1:18]: "He buried the slain and the dead" (Tobias).

Ludwig Hartmann writes about this:

"A funeral is also to be prepared for the poor, for it is shameful that the poor deceased are carried out without sacred rites; therefore, those who do not leave so much behind that they can be buried at their own expense should be buried for free. Truly many are vexed when they see that the poor are buried without song and sound." (Pastorale evang. p. 1334.)

E.

Fifth section.

On the exercise of a congregation's duty to ensure that everything is done orderly and honestly.

§ 37.

In addition to what has already been mentioned occasionally, this includes the following: The pastor should keep and have custody of two books, in one of which, the soul register, all members of the congregation, both voting and non-voting, are recorded, and in the other, the church register, the official acts performed, baptisms, confirmations, banns, marriages, funerals, with details of the persons, time, place and other important circumstances, as well as the confessors and communicants, are registered. Both books should be purchased by the parish and be its property.

§ 38.

All letters and documents relating to the congregation, addressed to it or emanating from it should, if possible, be kept in the original, if not in a certified copy, together with the minute book by the appointed clerk of the congregation.

§ 39.

For the proper administration of the financial affairs of the congregation, the collection and disbursement of salaries, etc., a member of the congregation who is skilled in such matters and has proven himself as a Christian should be appointed as congregational treasurer, and he should not only render an account either monthly or quarterly in a public congregational meeting, but his account should also be accurately audited from time to time by persons appointed for this purpose, sometimes at certain times and sometimes at unspecified times. 2 Cor. 8, 20, 21: "And prevent this, lest any man should speak evil of us, because of such a rich tax which is paid by us; and see that it is done honestly, not only before the Lord, but also before men."

§ 40.

For the proper care of the poor, widows, orphans, elderly, infirm, sick, etc. in the congregation, one or more persons should be appointed to administer the office of almoner and to ensure that no one is overlooked in terms of the support and assistance they need. Romans 12:8: "If anyone gives, let him give simply. If anyone governs, let him be diligent; he that sheweth mercy, with cheerfulness." Acts 6:1-7. 1 Tim. 3:8-13.

Note: Luther writes in the Church Postil:

"If one wants to establish a common body, one must know what offices are to be held by those who are to preside over the congregation. A bishop is a minister of God who is to distribute the divine and spiritual goods, preach the gospel and provide the people with the word of God; he must have servants, these are

<pages 170>

deacons, who are to serve the congregation in such a way that they keep a register of the poor, provide them with all the necessities of the congregation's money, visit the sick and preside over the goods everywhere." (XI, 2756. [StL 11, 2066])

§ 41.

The congregation should, if its means permit, be anxious to acquire for itself a property serving its communal purposes, such as a well-furnished, spacious church, school, parish and teachers' residence, a churchyard, etc., and to elect men who should not only hold this property as representatives of the congregation (trustees) vis-à-vis the state, but should also supervise it and ensure that damage is averted, that everything is well maintained and that any necessary improvements, extensions, etc. are carried out.

Note 1: Luther writes:

"We should esteem nothing higher and more precious on earth than this good deed, that one can have the dear, blessed Word and be in a place where one may preach and confess it freely and publicly. Therefore a Christian who belongs to a church where God's word is taught should thank God with a joyful heart for his unspeakable grace as often as he enters." (On Ps. 23:1. V, 376. [StL 5, 257])

Note 2: Concerning the buildings of worship, Luther writes:

"We should not look at the outward place only with bodily eyes and like unreasoning animals.... The church is built of stone and wood, and our church, if one does not come together in it, is not a temple or church of God, unless it is called so relatively (or, as it is customary to speak in the schools). But if one preaches in it, administers the sacraments, ordains and confirms the ministers to the teaching ministry, then you should say that it is God's house and the gate of heaven." (On Gen. 28:17. II, 632. [StL 2, 433])

Furthermore, Luther wrote in the year 1539 to the

mayor of Wittenberg, when the raging churchyard was being dishonored:

"Dear mayor, since the abuse of the churchyard is growing longer and longer, that everyone lies there, leads, places and does as he pleases; so that the dear dead (who are baptized in Christ and live, and await the resurrection in the churchyard, as if resting and sleeping in their little bed, as Isaiah 26. Cap.) are almost no more esteemed than if they lay on a shingle corpse or not far from the gallows: so my request is that you will see to it that such other abuses are removed and that the dead, many of whom undoubtedly fall asleep in Christ, are granted a little greater honor and rest. For we cannot dig them all up and put them away so that we can give way to such abuses; we would like to do so if possible, otherwise it would appear that we think nothing of the dead, nor of the resurrection of the dead. The brewing-pan, as of old, we may well suffer thereon, for safety's sake, but the other is too much, that even the carpenters respect no sermon, yea, hew and rumble with their stuff, that no word in the sermon may be heard, thinking it is more needful and cheaper to hear a carpenter's axe than God's word." (XIV, 1862. [StL 14, 790])

§ 42.

The congregation was to procure everything necessary for worship, a church Bible, a hymnal, an agenda, baptismal and communion vessels, ministerial robes, etc., and elect a sexton [custodian] who was to keep these in safekeeping, maintain the church building in cleanliness and order (1 Cor. 11:22: "But have you not houses where you may eat and drink?"), conscientiously provide the elements for baptism and communion, and generally give the pastor the necessary assistance during the service. The seats were not to be rented out, but those in the offices of the congregation were to be assigned special seats and a sacristy was to be provided for the preacher.

§ 43.

The time of the beginning of each assembly of the congregation should be precisely determined and paused.

§ 44.

No important matters should be put to the vote at municipal assemblies without prior joint discussion, debate and consultation.

§ 45.

As a rule, everything that is not decided by God's word, that is, that is neither commanded nor forbidden, should be decided by the majority of votes, as the decision that nature teaches (1 Cor. 11:14).); but if, as a result of enforcing the decision of the majority, a division or other harm should be feared for the sake of the weakness of many, then the majority should give way to the minority for the sake of love and peace, 2 Cor. 10:8: "And even if I should boast a little more of our power, which the Lord has given us to improve you and not to destroy you, I would not be put to shame."

Note.  With regard to the latter case, what the Formula of Concord says is important:

"Accordingly we believe, teach, and confess that the church of God, in every place and at all times, has the opportunity according to good judgment, power, and authority to change, diminish, and increase these (middle things) in an orderly and proper manner, without levity or annoyance, as is at all times considered most useful, beneficial, and best for good order, Christian discipline and discipline, evangelical prosperity, and for the edification of the church.

Paul teaches Romans 14:21, and proves it by his example in Acts 16:3, 21:26; 1 Corinthians 9:19, how even the weak in faith can be yielded and yielded to with a clear conscience in such external matters." (Sol. Decl., X, 9.)

§ 46.

The chairman of the assembly should see to it that only one person speaks at a time (1 Cor. 14:30: "But if a revelation is made to another who is sitting there, let the first be silent"), so that everyone has room to speak his mind where necessary and the discussion is prevented from becoming a quarrel (1 Cor. 11:16: "But if there is anyone among you who has a desire to quarrel, let him know that we do not have such a way, nor do the common people of God."

§ 47.

Before the completion of each election by voting, the names of those capable of voting should be read out and called out, those called out should make their presence known and cast their votes immediately, and those absent among those capable of voting should only be allowed to vote in writing.

§48.

All summonses to the community meeting should be made in writing by a person responsible for this.

§ 49.

All those who hold an office in the church apart from the preacher should have a written instruction drawn up by the church, in which their powers, their scope and limits would be precisely defined; but each member should be willing, where he is able, to

accept such an office offered to him, 1 Peter 4:10-11: "Serve one another, each one with the gift he has received, as good stewards of the manifold grace of God. If anyone speaks, let him speak as the word of God. If anyone has a ministry, let him do it as from the resources God has given him."

Note: When a citizen refused to accept the office of overseer and almoner offered to him, the Wittenberg theological faculty, asked for advice, wrote in 1662:

"Whoever, ignoring his civic duty, the proper election and his relatives' admonitions, refuses such a Christian office ordered by the high authorities, indeed by the holy apostles themselves, for the benefit and preservation of churches and schools, does sin. Therefore, we hold that the above-mentioned Thomas cannot justly and rightly dispense himself from the office of deacon and overseer, 1. partly because it is a common burden of the whole congregation, which each one to whom it is entrusted by ordinary election is bound by custom to administer by virtue of having communion with the church there....  2. Thus, by divine command, everyone is obliged to promote divine service and to serve churches and schools.  3. It cannot be done without annoyance, since others will undoubtedly refer to this example and will endeavor to free themselves from such labor with such pretexts; as a result, this highly necessary and useful office may come into contempt and finally no one will want to be used for it." (Consil. Witeberg. III, 68. 69.)

What Luther says about accepting the call to the preaching office also applies here:

"If people want to force and urge me, and I can do it, or I cannot do it at all, that is desired of me, I do as much as I can. So he drives through men, and so God's commandment is there, that the Holy Spirit also calls me and says Leviticus 19:18: You shall love your neighbor as yourself. No man shall live to himself alone, but shall also serve his neighbor. This commandment

has been given to all, to me and to you. If the same commandment seizes me and is held against me, no defense will help, unless I want to defend myself until I am disgraced by it." (On Ex. 3, 1. III, 1076. 1077. [StL 3, 721])

§ 50.

Any written congregational order should only contain the most necessary and what has already been preserved in congregational life, and no provision made therein concerning something that is neither commanded nor forbidden in God's Word should be unalterable, but can be duly amended and repealed at any time by a significant majority of votes.

Note: In 1534, Luther wrote to Nicolaus Hausmann, then a preacher in Dessau:

"I read your church order and expressed my opinion to Magister Forchemius, namely that its printing and public announcement does not yet seem advisable. For we, too, have long regretted that we made our order known and thereby set an example for all others to come out with theirs. Thus the variety as well as the number of ceremonies grew to such an extent that we would soon surpass the Papists' seas and forests. I therefore advised that the written copy should be retained and that the pastors should be informed of what and how much they should do for this time, so that discipline might be gradually established through use and actual practice without oral or written transmission or rather imposture. Then my advice is that the customs of our congregations introduced here or of the neighboring congregations should be maintained as far as possible, so that we do not open our mouths to the papists and sects to bark and blaspheme, and carry these differences around among us as disagreements." (Luther's letters translated from Schütze's collection. 11, 40. 41. [StL 21, 1894])

Eight days later Luther wrote to the same subject:

"I

was pleased that you wrote that you had not had it in mind. to make known your established church order through printing. For in this way it will come about that in time the priests themselves will organize everything better. Such observances are better to be distinguished after they have been practiced than before. For the law commands many things that are not subsequently observed; but the agenda records what would be observed." (op. cit. p. 44. [StL 21, 1899])

When Luther was sent the church order drafted at the synod of Homberg in Hesse in 1526, he replied to Landgrave Philip and others as follows:

"My loyal and submissive advice is that Your Princely Grace will not allow this order to be printed at this time, because I have not yet and cannot yet be so bold as to make such a pile of laws with such powerful words in our country. That would be my opinion, as Moses did with his laws, which he took, wrote out and ordered almost all of them, as they had already been customary among the people from time immemorial; thus also Your Princely Grace first provided the parishes and schools with good persons and tried beforehand to draw them up with verbal orders or on slips of paper, and all this in the shortest and least possible way, which they should do. And it would be even better if the pastors first began one, three, six, nine among themselves, in a united way in one or three, five, six pieces, until they were practiced and became pregnant, and then further and more, as the matter would probably give itself and force itself, until all pastors would follow suit. Then it could be put into a little booklet; for I know well, and have also experienced it myself, that when laws are made too soon before custom and practice, they are seldom well advised; people are not so skillful as those who sit by themselves and paint with words and thoughts how it should be done. To prescribe and to obey differ greatly. And experience will show that much of this order will have to be changed, and some of it will remain alone. But if

some pieces come into use and custom, then it is easy to do so and to put them in order. Making law is indeed a great, glorious, far-reaching thing, and without God's Spirit nothing good will come of it. Therefore, we must approach God with fear and humility and keep this measure: short and good, little and good, gentle and always on. Afterwards, when they take root, more will follow from the discipline itself than is necessary." (Erlangen edition LVI, p. 170. 171. [StL 21, 915])

§ 51.

Every member of the church must recognize his obligation to contribute proportionately to the maintenance of the church and school and to the feeding of the poor members (Matthew 10:10: "A laborer is worthy of his meat." 1 Cor. 9:14: "So also the Lord has commanded that those who preach the gospel should feed on the gospel." 2 Cor. 8:12: "For if a man is willing, he is acceptable according to what he has, not according to what he has not"); but how much each one is to give proportionately is left to the conscience and voluntary love of each one, 2 Cor. 9:7: "Each one at his own discretion, not unwillingly or under compulsion; for God loves a cheerful giver."

Note: Luther testifies to this in his minor interpretation of the first book of Moses when he writes:

"On the other hand, Jacob vowed: Everything you give me, I will give you a tithe of, namely, that a priest would be kept there who preached and taught. For those who taught the word of God were commanded from the beginning to give tithes; which is not now in the New Testament, but is remitted and set apart for love, that one may not make laws, but give as much as is necessary to feed and keep a preacher. So St. Paul teaches, Gal. 6:6 and 1 Cor. 9:14. Where Christians are, they do not refrain; but since there were no Christians and

the people had to be governed by compulsion and laws, a certain amount had to be determined as to how much they should be given. Those who came before us had to do the same, so that the preachers could not be provided for in this way. Love is too weak among the common people, it does not do it... So we have now seen clearly enough what the temple contains and how they misused it, and how it teaches us that we are also obliged to give to the right service of God, that is, to receive the word; namely, as far as God gives us to be able to do it, and not in the opinion of doing a good work to earn something, but for the benefit of others, so that it is a vow of love more than of faith. So you can also vow to give as much to your neighbor every year as long as you are able, in honor of God, the one who teaches you God's word." (On Gen. 28, 22. III, 677-679. [StL 3, 452 f.])

Hence it is said in the order of a common box of the congregation of Leisnig, drafted by Luther:

"Where also the interest, the collection, the pleasures and the additions to the assets and supplies of our common treasury are not sufficient to maintain and provide for our parish office, sextonage, schools, the needy poor and common buildings, ... we have ... have amicably decided and agreed for ourselves and our descendants by virtue of this fraternal union that every honorable man, citizen and farmer residing in the parish, according to what he has and is able, should contribute money annually for himself, his wife and children, so that the main sum, which a common parish assembly would learn and explore in its deliberations and advice from the annual accounts as being necessary and sufficient, may be fully spent and obtained. For this purpose, as far as our parish extends, all household members, servants, miners of the trades and other persons who do not own a house and yet enjoy and use our parish rights, each person shall pay a silver groschen, allways

for every quarter of a year three new pennies, as the fourth part of the same penny, annually; which every landlord or landlady shall diligently bring in and hand over to the ten rectors on every quarter. And a parish congregation will and should not now and in the future complain of such a small annual allowance and help, for the glory of God and the love of the neighboring Christians; in consideration of the fact that before this, for a long eternal time, both the resident and non-resident have been overburdened and sucked dry by our common parish with excessive, unbearable burdens and deductions of various ways and lists without interruption throughout the year. Which things are now, by the grace of God, again turned and come into true freedom of the Christian spirit, and to prevent every Christian with the utmost diligence from abusing such Christian freedom to cover shameful avarice." (X, 1172. 73. [StL 10, 973 f.])

Concerning the collection of gifts especially for the poor, Porta writes:

"If anyone would like to know how and to what extent alms should be given, or how one could conveniently collect something for the common need and entertainment of the poor, let him read St. Paul's Intercession to the Corinthians, 1 Cor. 16, where he writes thus: On every Sabbath let each one of you lay by himself, and gather according to his ability. Here St. Paul wants people to put something aside from Sabbath to Sabbath or from week to week and collect for the poor. So that if people were to give too much at once, they would not have to complain about it or suffer loss themselves. This is no doubt where the custom comes from that in our time almost everywhere on Sundays the little sack is used or that boxes and basins are placed in front of or outside the churches. Again, that in some places (as also happens here) every quarter something is collected from house to house for the poor people in the hospital, also in the inns, or where otherwise many people come together, there are boxes or little shops and the wandering people are addressed that they should be mindful of the dear poor. So be it as it may, everyone has,

if they so wish, many opportunities to do good and show their compassion. And so that one may not give far after that, a good and useful suggestion is given by some that every merchant, craftsman or otherwise wealthy householder should, according to the admonition of St. Paul, put a money box in his pocket. Pauli, and if he had prayed in the morning and called upon the Lord God for his blessing, he should then throw the first penny, threepence or groschen (depending on whether his trade is large or small) that he receives or redeems from his goods that day into the same money box and thus give the first fruits of his income to the Lord his good.... Now when the year was over, one could come together in a congregation or neighborhood and hand over each one's own, so that it would be placed in the common box or handed over to the appointed overseers and turned over by them to the proper oarsmen and faithfully and honestly calculated in due time. But if each one wanted to distribute it for himself, he could give one part of it to known house-poor, the other to the hospital, the third to diligent students who had a good testimony, or otherwise, if and as the occasion of the time required, to other needy and poor people." (Pastorale Lutheri, pp. 1085-87.)

Balduin testifies that the principle of freewill giving is to be observed in the following words:

"The church ministers should also have the care of the poor, but should not force anyone to contribute something of his goods like a certain annual contribution for the common good, but should persuade by commandments and examples of the Holy Scriptures with entreaties and exhortations how everyone should show works of mercy.... And therefore our Paul would not command, but would give his good pleasure in this, and would stir up the hearts of the Corinthians by reasons, that they might attend to the necessities of the saints." (On 2 Cor. 8:8.)

F.

Sixth section.

On the exercise of the duty of a congregation, also with the orthodox church outside it, to strive for unity in the Spirit in the bond of love and peace.

§ 52.

The congregation should pray diligently for all the saints together, Ephesians 6:18: "And pray always with all prayer and supplication in the Spirit, keeping watch with all perseverance and supplication for all the saints."

Note: Thus Luther writes in his Sermon on Good Works of 1520:

"All this has been said about prayer for one's own needs and in general. But the prayer that actually belongs to this (third) commandment and is called a work of the feast day is much better and greater, which is to be done for the gathering of all Christendom, for all the needs of all people, enemies and friends, especially those of every parish and diocese. So St. Paul commanded his disciple Timothy, 1 Tim. 2:1-3; likewise Jer. 29:7 and Baruch 1:12. This common prayer is precious and the most powerful, for the sake of which we also come together. Whereof also the church is called a house of prayer, Luke 19:46, that we should gather together in one accord and take before us the afflictions of ourselves and all men, and present them to God and call upon him for mercy. But this must be done with heartfelt emotion and earnestness, so that such needs of all people may come to our hearts and so that we may pray for them with true compassion in true faith and trust. For how does it stand and rhyme that we come together bodily into a house of prayer (so that it is indicated that we should call and pray for the whole church together), if we scatter the prayers and divide them in such a way that each one prays only for himself and no one takes care of the other, nor is concerned with anyone's need? How may prayer be called useful, good, pleasant and common, or a work of the feast or of the assembly? How do those who hold their own little prayer, this one for this, that one for that, and have nothing but selfish

prayers, to which God is hostile? This common prayer is still an indication of the old custom when the confession is recited at the end of the sermon and prayers are made for all Christians at the pulpit. However, it should not be used for this purpose, as is now the custom and practice, but should be an exhortation to pray throughout the whole Mass (that is, throughout the whole service) for such need as the preacher incites us to pray for.... O if God willed that any multitude should hear and pray in this way after Mass, that a common, earnest cry of the heart of the whole people should go out to God, how immeasurable virtue and help should follow from prayer! What could be more terrible for all evil spirits? What greater work could be done on earth? That so many pious people might be preserved, so many sinners converted! For truly, the Christian church on earth has no greater power or work than such common prayer against everything it may encounter. The evil spirit knows this well; that is why he does everything he can to hinder this prayer. He lets us build beautiful churches, endow many things, whistle, read and sing, hold many masses, and engage in pomp without all measure; he does not suffer for this, indeed, he helps us to regard such things as the best, and to think that we have done well with them. But that this common, strong, fruitful prayer goes down beside it and remains unnoticed by such glaring, there he has what he seeks. For where prayer lies low, no one will take anything away from it, nor will anyone resist it. But if he were to realize that we wanted to practice this prayer, even if it were under a thatched roof or in a pigsty, he would certainly not let it go, but would be far more afraid of the same pigsty than of all the high, large, beautiful churches, towers and bells that may be there, where such prayer would not be inside. It truly does not depend on places or buildings where we come together, but only on this unconquerable prayer, that we do the same rightly together and let it come before God." (X, 1621-23. [StL 10, 1342-1344])

§ 53.

Just as every true local Evangelical congregation has the same public confession of faith with the whole true Lutheran church has the same public confession of faith, so it should also make every effort to be united with it in life and to speak with it in one mind and one opinion, 1 Cor. 1:10: "But I exhort you, brethren, by the name of our Lord Jesus Christ, that ye all speak the same thing, and let there be no divisions among you, but hold fast one to another in one mind and in one opinion."

Note. Thus our fathers wrote in the preface to our Book of Concord in 1580 concerning the last general Lutheran confession of faith, namely the Formula of Concord: they were thus "minded,"

"to make nothing new by this Concordian work, nor to depart from the divine truth once recognized and known by our godly ancestors and us, as founded in prophetic and apostolic Scripture, and as contained in the three Symbols, also in the Augsburg Confession, delivered to the memory of Emperor Carol the Fifth in 1530, in the Apology which followed, in the Smalcald Articles, and in the great and small Catechism of the highly enlightened man, Dr. Luther. Luther, not to deviate at all, neither in rebus nor phrasibus" (that is, neither in the doctrines themselves, nor in the manner of speaking of them) "but rather, by the grace of the Holy Spirit, to persevere and remain unanimously in it, and also to regulate all religious disputes and their explanations accordingly."

Frederick Balduin also writes:

"Schismata, that is, schisms, are disagreements in the Church, which are aroused because of ceremonies, works and the reputation of persons among those who otherwise agree in the articles of faith, by which disagreements not only arouse offense, but also the Church is extremely wounded. Therefore, although a schism is less than a heresy (or faction), it is something more terrible than

any other crime. The difference between heresy and schism was noted by Augustine in his Book of Faith and Symbols in chapter 10 with these words: 'The heretics, by harboring false beliefs about God, violate the faith itself, but the schismatics (that is, those who make a mere schism in the Church) separate themselves from brotherly love by unjust disagreements, although they believe the same things that we believe.' There are two planting gardens of true unity: speaking the same thing and believing the same thing. These two easily maintain unity in life and faith. In common life, nothing is more detestable than a two-faced man who is convinced otherwise than he speaks; in faith, nothing is more pernicious than an eccentric who has his own particular opinions and ways of speaking, just to make it appear that he has invented something new. In this way a wide window is very easily opened for divisions and factions. We should therefore seek to preserve unity in speech and opinions, in endeavors and customs, and thus we will easily be free from division. Those who bear one and the same name of Christ's name should be one, teach one and the same, and confess one and the same." (On 1 Cor. 1:10.)

§ 54.

Each congregation was to come to an agreement with its neighboring congregation about their mutual local boundaries (Tit. 1:5: "For this reason I left you in Crete, that you should complete what I have left and occupy the cities back and forth with elders, as I commanded you." Gal 2:9: "James and Cephas and John gave me and Barnaba their right hand, and became one with us, that We should preach among the Gentiles, and you among the circumcision.") and those who are within the district of other churches should not be received by any congregation, 1 Peter 4:15: "But let no one among you suffer as a murderer, or a thief,

or an evildoer, or as one who is taking up a strange office." 1 Peter 5:2: "Feed the flock of Christ, as you are commanded." Heb. 10:25: "Let us not forsake our assembly, as some are wont to do."

Note 1: Thus the Wittenberg theological faculty wrote in a concern from the seventeenth century:

"By divine right the ecclesiastical districts or parishes, which are called parishes or dioceses, should and must remain distinct, and once they are distinct, they should not be reunited, concorded and combined, much less mixed and mingled, without considerable cause; for which the testimonies of Holy Scripture Acts 20:28, 1 Peter 5:2 are not unspeakable, also other such commands Tit. 1:5: 'For this reason I left you in Crete, that you should occupy the cities back and forth* (that is, city by city, city by city or each individual city) 'with elders etc.,' as well as examples, such as the various churches in Asia, Judea, Galilee, etc., which are often reported, are not unreasonably attracted, and the old conciliar, the first Nicene, the first Constantinopolitan, the first Carthaginian, and other such prohibitions are duly observed. Nevertheless, there are times and cases in which episcopal residences, parishes, diaconates, and other ecclesiastical offices may be transferred, divided, united, the parishioners divided differently, and such changes made without violating divine honor, commandment, or prohibition, or, since they are made and introduced with the tacit or express consent of those who have to decide on them, they may be made and introduced without violating divine honor, commandment, or prohibition, and are introduced and confirmed with the tacit or express consent of those who have to decide on them, and there is no substantial cause for a change or return to the previous number of parishes, parishioners, audience, place, time and place of worship, the same may be tolerated and confirmed according to the form of things. And the reason is this: that, just as the size and breadth, as well as the distance and uniformity of one diocese is divided with another according to human will and human established right for reasonable reasons,

that in a town or village so and so many houses, those and those should belong to this parish, those to that parish, and the division of the parishioners has generally been established according to the proximity of their dwellings to the places of worship, or in view of the convenience of getting there without ambush and stay; hence the particular parishes have their name, that they are called dioceses, paroikies, from the neighborhood and bordering with the places of worship ...: so the definition has remained; for Diöcesis.. is still called a region of several particular churches belonging to a main church of a notable city or country, but a parish (or parochia) is a region of Christian inhabitants who are assigned to one baptismal font and one altar and receive the holy sacraments there. Just as dioceses and parishes are named and divided according to the proximity and neighborhood of the place where Christians live: So, according to opportunity and change of the neighbors, (that they have either become more, at times more churches have been built and more parishes or parishes have been ordered and divided, but after reduction of the neighbors, that they could not have received their own pastor, some parishes have been merged, or also according to opportunity of the place, when winter time because of snow and water partly parishioners could have built their own church more conveniently or could have entered another neighboring church) such parishes have been changed, moved, united, separated or increased; just as various regulations and ordinances are to be found in church law, namely changes to the parish churches or the abolition of the obligation that the penitent listeners are henceforth absolved from the obligation to go to this or that place of worship if they have been prevented from doing so because of winter, storms or water, or have been unable to get there without great difficulty.... Thus there is also the common sense and rule of law: by which something is collected, that it can also be dissolved by this.... But it would be according to Christian love, equity, prudence and neighborly friendship ... that they leave it ... at the

made decree." (Wittenbergische Consilien, II, 107-109.)

In 1638, the same faculty issued the following ruling:

"As far as the fourth question is concerned, the pastor is not authorized to perform public ministries privately in a foreign diocese, even if a good or the largest part of his parishioners had followed him there, without the consent of the ordinary pastor, because the profession is not only limited to a certain number of parishioners, but also in a certain place, 1 Peter 5:2." (op. cit. p. 57.)

Note 2: Deyling testifies in his Pastoral Theology that those who are in other parishes are not to be received, where he writes:

"In the General Articles it is decreed: 'No pastor or minister shall administer the sacrament to anyone who does not belong to his parish without the permission of the superintendent, except to itinerant sick persons'. The freedom to roam at will and to change one's confessor lightly, which generally arises from hatred and resentment and is highly annoying, is not permitted, especially if one seeks another confessor outside one's parish. For this gives offense and prevents pastoral care, which (then) cannot be properly established and conducted by either pastor; nor can the one who turns his back on his pastor and leaves him with evil intent be considered repentant and admitted to absolution and the use of Holy Communion. Therefore, where there are separate parishes and orthodox pastors, parish priests are generally not at liberty to make use of official acts outside their parish and to choose a different confessor, who does not know the customs of the person who wishes to come to confession and can easily be deceived. The ordinary pastor is also wronged and deprived of part of his salary in this way. Such unstable pastors are therefore rightly rejected and sent back to their former pastor if it can be done easily, or if the case is more difficult, the matter is reported purely to the superintendent and consistory.... Exceptions are those who come from a foreign parish in which the church ministers

<pages 188>

are of a different faith, e.g. Papists. If they are penitent and seek absolution in the neighborhood, it is not to be denied them. Thus Christ received a man excluded from their fellowship by the Pharisees, John 9:38. A church minister, like the apostle Paul, is a debtor to all Romans 1:14, who need his service for lack of others. He cannot refuse to preach the Word or administer the sacraments to the wretched who wish to renounce a false ministry. Nor are fewer to be excluded from the above rule who come from another parish where there are orthodox ministers, but are compelled to be absent from home either for military service or for commercial business or for other reasons, and have evidence of a good way of life. They cannot be refused absolution if they desire it and are penitent, because they do not seek another confessor out of hatred or contempt for their pastor, but out of necessity. The other parishioners are not permitted such a change easily and not even for the sake of disputes which they have with their pastor, unless they are serious and protracted and have arisen through the fault of the pastor and are being nurtured. If, however, good reasons are given, the consistory, after having examined the dispute and attempted reconciliation, is in the habit of changing the confessor once and again or for a time, because it cannot be denied that the confession of sins and the revelation of inner thoughts presupposes great trust in the person into whose bosom we pour out the sorrows of our souls and from whom we expect consolation." (Instit. prud. past. p. 440-443.)

When in 1619 a preacher had refused to baptize the child of a man who had fallen away to the Calvinists, unless the man would declare himself repentant, the said faculty wrote to the preacher:

"We wish from the bottom of our hearts that you will never make such a pass with the said N. N., but that you would have remembered what can be found in our country's church order and cannot be unknown to you, namely in the sixth general article with these words: 'Nor shall any pastor or minister of the church raise young

children with Holy Baptism for the sake of their parents' sin and impenitence, or let them lie and die unbaptized'... And of what importance your given reason could be, experience has shown you, in that many children who are well baptized in their youth fall away to strange teachings in their old age, while many born of erroneous parents finally turn to the true church." (op. cit. p. 114.)

§ 55.

The congregation should request from those who come to it from other orthodox churches a certificate issued to them by the latter, and acknowledge the same; but on the other hand give such a certificate to those who go from it to other churches, Acts 18:27: "When he (Apollo) was about to go to Achaam, the brethren wrote, exhorting the disciples to receive him." 3 John v. 8-9: "We ought therefore to receive such, that we may be helpers of the truth. I have written to the church, but Diotrephes, who wants to be exalted among them, does not receive us." Cf. v. 10.

§ 56.

The church should not receive as brethren those who are lawfully banned from orthodox churches, 1 Tim. 1:20: "Among whom are Hymenaeus and Alexander, whom I delivered to Satan to be chastened to blaspheme no more." Cf. 2 Tim. 4:14-15: "Alexander the smith hath shewed me many wicked things: the Lord reward him according to his works. Beware of him also, for he has greatly resisted our words."

Note: Ludwig Hartmann writes:

"A person banished from one local congregation can in no way be received by another as a member of the congregation unless the congregation

that had banished him is reconciled and agrees. For it is the same person who is to be readmitted who was to be excluded, and those who had to deny fellowship are to judge the cause of the denial, and a judgment that is not passed by their own judge has no validity. This is part of the seriousness of church discipline, so that the obstinate may be cut off from any path by which they often seek to withdraw their necks from wholesome discipline." (Pastorale evangel. p. 873. 874.)

§ 57.

The congregation should receive as its brethren those who are exiles, unlawfully banished or who come as guests from other congregations, and care for them as for its own members, 1 Pet. 4:9: "Be hospitable to one another without murmuring." Cf. Heb. 13:2, Rom. 16:1-2: "But I charge you concerning our sister Phoebe, who is at the service of the church in Cenchrea, that you receive her in the Lord, as is fitting for the saints, and assist her in whatever business she has need of you. For she has also helped many, including myself." 1 Cor. 16:10-11: "When Timothy comes, see to it that he is with you without fear, for he also does the work of the Lord, as I do. Let no one despise him. But lead him in peace, that he may come to me; for I am waiting for him with the brethren." John 16:2: "They will put you under the ban. But the time will come when whoever kills you will think he is doing God a service." Cf. John 9:22-39 — Matt. 25:35: "I was a stranger, and you gave me shelter."

Note: Thus the Wittenberg theological faculty wrote in 1656:

"In sum, everything in the church should be done honestly and properly according to the admonition of the Apostle

Paul 1 Cor. 14:40, namely in such a way and manner that every pastor faithfully takes care of his parishioners, while the parishioners hear his voice and duly obey him in all proper matters. But all this must be understood by well-organized churches and preaching offices, except for persecution and similar cases.... But because it is clear from your question that your congregation is deprived of its proper pastor by papal tyranny and persecution, the whole church is destroyed, so to speak, that one here, the other there in other parishes must seek the sacraments from righteous preachers because of the papal priests, in which case the church is free, no righteous preacher is authorized to expel some blameless person from his congregation, but to accept anyone, whether he comes from the evening or the morning, and to administer the sacraments, if only he is a righteous Christian and does true penance; as our Savior himself says of himself: Everything that comes to meI will not cast out, John 6:37." (Consil. Witeberg. II, 60. 61.)

Thus Luther also writes:

"I also taught that one should not mix the parish rights and lure people from one parish to another, where everything is held uniformly. For what is cheaper? But I never approved that, if the sacrament" (unlawfully) "is denied in one, the same is not allowed to be desired or administered in another." (Letters collected by Schütz, translated into German. I, 336. 337.)

Thus Heshusius further writes:

"If the case arises that other people, who do not belong to our parish, sit either under the antichristian papacy or under false teachers, as Calvinists, Synergists, Majorists, Adiapborists, Schwenkfeldians, for whom a Christian must beware, or are burdened by their tyrannical pastors against their conscience, or are otherwise on a journey, are afflicted with illness, or otherwise need consolation and want to strengthen their conscience through the use of the sacraments, desire our service and seek the sacraments from us: In such

and similar cases, we preachers are at liberty to administer the sacraments to every man, whether he comes from the rising or setting of the sun (provided he repents and believes the Gospel), by virtue of the saying of John 16: The Holy Spirit will give the sacraments to every man. 16: The Holy Spirit will punish the world, that is, the kingdom of Christ and the holy ministry extends over the whole world and is not bound to any place, person, or time. And that Christians who shun their pastors who preach false doctrine and blasphemy or who wish to afflict their consciences against God's Word, and seek the sacraments in other parishes from righteous teachers, are acting in a Christian manner, appears from the words of Christ: "Beware of false prophets, Matt. 7; item Paul: Beware of the evil workers, Phil. 3 and Rom. 16: Beware of those who cause division and dissension apart from the teaching you have learned, and depart from them." (Dedekennus' Thesaurus II, 438.)

Finally, Porta writes:

"Where in well-ordered cities and communities, in addition to the hospitals and almshouses, there is a common box for poor and destitute native and foreign people and special overseers are appointed, they should see and strive with all faithfulness and diligence that poor Lazarus is well and properly cared for. But in order that they may know what kind of people belong among them and to whom they should pay attention, I will tell you recently: ... . 6. neither shall poor expelled and driven out preachers or other stable Christians and confessors be passed over or abandoned, who suffer for the sake of the truth, especially if they have good testimony from their churches or other well-known and proven preachers and pious Christians, and are not countrymen and deceivers, of whom unfortunately more than too many are found under a good appearance at the present time." (Pastorale Lutheri, p. 1133. 1135.)

§ 58.

The congregation, if it wishes to call the preacher of another congregation, should ask the latter for its consent and for the dismissal of its preacher, and seek agreement with it as to the divinity of the call; or

if its preacher is called to another church, examine the call according to God's Word, and if it proves to be divine, let its preacher go willingly, 1 Tim. 3:13: "But they that minister well gain for themselves a good estate."

Note 1: In answer to the question whether a preacher is absolutely not permitted to transfer from one congregation to another, Ch. Kortholt replies that it is certainly permitted under certain circumstances, and proves this as follows:

"For as the Lord of the harvest has power to call his ministers, so also to transfer them from one place to another; and it is not to be doubted that it is according to the will of this Lord, if only all things be lawfully accomplished, so the church thus transfers the ministers of the Word. For 1. the apostle, speaking of the deacons, says 1 Tim. 3:13: But those who serve well acquire for themselves a good degree; which he would not say if those who serve faithfully in lesser offices could not be raised to greater ones. 2. Why should not those who, endowed with excellent gifts, preside over the church for a time in lowly circumstances, be set over more populous congregations, since this would be to the greater advantage of the church? 3. And if such a transfer of ecclesiastical persons were to be regarded as completely unauthorized, then we would hardly have any superintendents etc. and pastors of the largest congregations other than young people who had not previously administered any ecclesiastical offices, which would be quite unsuitable. However, this does not condone the frivolity of those who, without urgent need and without any obvious benefit to the church, frequently change their place of residence, and (as Seneca says of the sick) use the changes as a remedy by leaving their posts, either because of greater and more abundant pay, or to get rid of work they can do, or to gain higher honor, and whatever other temporal advantages are usually considered here. For such turn godliness into a trade (which the apostle forbids in 1 Tim. 6:5). Matthesius wittily

says of them: 'Some do as the sun-dressers do; where the sun shines and he thinks to loose gold, there he unties his knapsack; if a cloudy people come in and the goods want to be more valid at another church consecration, he ties them up again and leaves; God grant that it may remain mother or bride, on which the Son of God has turned as much as she wants. (Pastor fidelis, p. 57-60.)

Note 2: As far as the calling away of the congregation is concerned, the theological faculty of Wittenberg wrote in 1634:

"The judgment as to whether and where a minister can create greater benefit. The faculty of Wittenberg wrote in 1634: "The judgment as to whether and where a preacher who is in service can create greater benefit is not solely in the judgment of the preacher, who may judge of the number and capacity of the listeners, but not of his, which is also required to create greater benefit; nor in the judgment of the vocalizing part; just as it is not in the judgment of the dismissing part; but in their entire and the presbyterii's reasonable opinion. And then, if the probability of greater benefit on the part of the preacher and the audience is made extensive, the dismissal of a preacher who is under obligation must nevertheless either (and justly) be done with prior consent (unless the same were to be refused without sufficient cause for other reasonable persons to judge), or else with the subsequent approval of the church to which the preacher has committed himself." (Consilia Witebergens. II, 59.)

Furthermore, the Brunswick ministry wrote about the same point in 1578 under Martin Chemnitz's presidency:

"Again, church and community consent and will are necessary if one is lawfully called, so no dismissal of the church can take place and be carried out without the church's consent and will. Among our means, other appointments have often been made without their seeking, even to such places where much could have been served to the good Lord; but because this church has not been able or willing to give its consent and will to such a change and dismissal for considerable reasons, those who have been appointed elsewhere have not been willing or able in their conscience to leave this church (to which, as far as official matters are concerned, they cannot attribute any considerable guilt). Thus

we could also show examples that some who leave their churches without their will immediately admit all kinds of inconvenience, even with a burden on their conscience. Thus we also find in the recalling letter that it is attached as a condition if his, Mr. N.'s, church would not dispute such a vocation.
