For this reason we know of nothing else to advise in this matter than what we ourselves would do, if it were our business, namely that Mr. N. should write again to Thuringia" (where he was to be transferred), "that his church did not want to leave (i.e. dismiss) him for good Christian reasons; and because he did not know how to blame them significantly, he could not take it upon his conscience to leave his church without and against their will. But since they would persist with the Junkers and with the congregation at N. with the attraction of moving, considerable causes and wanted it to be done with good will (as he also understood that his present church would not resist hindering God's work in other places when considerable causes were found): he could then feel and hear God's will from this, and either by staying or by leaving, he could show himself that it would be done with a clear conscience and without annoyance." (Dedekennus' Thesaurus, II, 543. 544.) —

Concerning the guilty consent of the congregation when their preacher is called away, the theological faculty of Jena under Joh. Gerhard's chairmanship in 1617:

"Although the wit of those is not to be praised who often change their church services without a proper profession, without necessity, without any great benefit to the church and without any significant cause, the contrary opinion of those who hold that a called church minister cannot in good conscience leave his previous position, in which he was initially placed by God, and accept another profession. This is to be proved from the Word of God, 1 Tim. 3:13, from the proven examples of Timothy and Titi in the first apostolic church, as well as from other pious bishops and ministers in the ancient church, from many church laws of the old councils and fathers, and other immovable reasons.... The other question as to whether the "Junkers" (who

as church patrons had the right of appointment in the name of the congregation) "can with good conscience endure their pastor, or whether they are not rather guilty of dismissing him in favor for the sake of conscience: the same has its decision from the first question. For, firstly 1., if the pastor is in conscience obliged to accept the new vocation as a legitimate, divine calling and one that is for the greater good of the church, it follows that they should not justly withhold it against God's will. First of all, also  2. so that it may not gain the appearance as if they wanted to presume an unconditional rule over the church ministers; but rather recognize themselves guilty of leaving the ordinary divine vocations of the preachers the free way.  3. Because the lords have gladly seen that the princely government has dismissed their pastor from his present service in the church, they should justly allow the same to follow the previous appointment without fail, according to the rule of Christ (Matthew 7:12): Do to others as you would have them do to you.  4. Because experience testifies how it has happened, when preachers have been kept from other ordinations, that they have either not lived long or have otherwise come to harm, as is proved by many examples." (Dedekennus' Thesaurus, II, 537.)

§ 59.

In serious cases, the congregation should seek advice from one or more sister congregations or, if requested, be prepared to provide such advice to the best of its ability. Compare Apostles' Meeting on 16.

Note: Dannhauer testifies that, especially in difficult cases of church discipline, other congregations should also be consulted when he writes:

"The third degree of church discipline is to report it to the congregation, if it consists of more than two or three witnesses; to a whole congregation; hence 2 Cor. 2:6: 'Of many,' and 1 Tim. 5:20: 'Before all. Whether it be either a partial or a

general representation consisting of all classes of individuals; or whether it be the whole church, not exactly of every individual of all classes, though without these being wholly excluded.... Or if the evil is rampant, in the whole gathering of several churches." (Liber consc. 1, 1153. 1154.)

Thus Luther also writes of matters that concern the whole church, as doctrinal disputes, in his treatise Concerning Councils and Churches:

"If an Arius at Alexandria becomes too powerful for his parish priest or bishop, and takes the people to himself, and also mixes in other parish priests and people in the country, so that the parish priest at Alexandria is defeated and his office of judge can no longer defend the right of this realm, that is, the right Christian faith: In such an emergency and at such a time, the other parish priests and bishops should run to the aid of the parish priest of Alexandria with all their might and defend the right faith against Arium and condemn Arium for the salvation of others, so that the misery does not get out of hand. And where the parish priests are unable to do so, the pious Emperor Constantine should use his power to help the bishops together. Just as when a fire breaks out, if the landlord alone cannot extinguish it, all the neighbors should run to help put it out, and where they do not run, the authorities should help and order them to run and anathematize or condemn the fire to save the other houses." (XVI, 2765. 66. [StL 16, 2260])

§ 60.

The congregation was to regard the needs of its sister churches as its own and lend them a helping hand to the best of its ability, 1 Cor. 16:1-2: "But as for the tax that is done to the saints, as I have done to the churches of Galatia, so do you also. Let each one of you, as Sabbath keepers, lay up for himself what he thinks is good, so that when I come, the tax will not be the first thing to be collected." Cf. 2 Cor. 8:1-14. 9:1-15.

Note: Friedrich Balduin writes on 1 Cor. 16:1-2:

"Alms are also to be sent to foreign churches when necessary, just as Paul has a collection organized here among the Corinthians for the Jerusalemites and the Roman church is praised for having, as an example to other churches, sent support to the poor brethren living in other places and reimbursed them for their lack, as Eusebius reports in Book 4, Chapter 13 and Nicephorus in Book 4, Chapter 8. Book 13, Cap. and Nicephorus in Book 4, Cap. 8. We should therefore not be unkind to foreigners who have been impoverished by fire or other misfortune and who ask us to help them. They, too, are comrades in our faith, for whom, as the apostle would have it, we should take the greatest care, Gal. 6:10." (Commentary, in omnes epp. Pauli p. 554.)

Concerning 2 Cor. 9:1-13. he writes:

"The agreement in doctrine must be publicly witnessed, in whatever way it can be done. Our Paul testifies that this can be done through charity towards fellow believers, vv. 12 and 13: 'The giving of this tax not only fulfills the need of the saints, but is also abundant in this, that many give thanks to God for this faithful service of ours and praise God for your submissive confession of the gospel of Christ and for your simple tax to them and to all. For it is unworthy of a Christian to neglect the brethren whom the unity of the faith makes our comrades. This is why Paul elsewhere recommends charity for the most part towards fellow believers, Gal. 6:10." (op. cit. p. 698.)

§ 61.

The congregation should be willing to allow a neighboring congregation, which alone cannot establish the preaching ministry among itself and also cannot be merged with the main congregation, to be served by its preachers as a branch or daughter congregation where possible.

Note: In the old Lower Saxony Church Order it is decreed:

"If it is also found that churches are so poor in

goods and income and have so few assets that each church could not keep and provide for its own pastor and sexton; in this case, our superintendents and visitators shall diligently discuss and conclude in the consistory: whether without disadvantage and neglect of the parishioners such a parish could be placed as a branch of another neighboring parish, incorporated into it, and both churches thus be placed in the charge of one pastor and pastor at the same time, as is often the case." (Dedekennus' Thesaurus II, 838.)

G.

Seventh section.

On the exercise of a congregation's duty to help with its part in building and promoting the church as a whole.

§ 62.

The church should see to it that gifted boys and young men are dedicated to the service of the church and that they are enabled to prepare for it, 1 Cor. 12:7: "In each one the gifts of the Spirit are manifested for the common good."

Note 1: Thus Luther writes in the preface to the Small Catechism:

"In particular, also urge the authorities and parents that they govern well and bring children to school, indicating how they are obliged to do so. And if they do not, what a cursed sin they are committing. For thereby they overthrow and destroy both the kingdom of God and the kingdom of the world, as the worst enemies of both God and man. And consider what terrible harm they do when they do not help to draw children to pastors, preachers, scribes, etc., that God will punish them terribly for this; for it is necessary to preach here; the parents and authorities are sinning in this, so that it cannot be said that the devil also has something cruel in mind."

Luther also wrote in a preface to Menii's Booklet of

Christian Housekeeping from 1529:

"Some, when they get high, think about wealth and honor, how they will become rich, ride high and inherit great wealth for their children, but ask nothing about child rearing. And as some now say: If my son learns so much that he wins the penny, he is learned enough. And if no one wants to raise children in any other way than by wit and art, they think nothing else but that they are free to raise their children as they please, just as if there were no God who had commanded them otherwise, but they themselves are God and lord over their children. But if there were a strictly orderly regime in the world and such harmful evil people were found that they did not want to improve themselves and raise their children differently, then the authorities should punish them in body and property or drive them out into the world. For such people are the most poisonous and harmful people on earth, so that neither Turks nor Tartars can be so harmful. The reason is this: as much as there is in them, they do nothing else than that both spiritual and worldly estate perish and both households and child-rearing are ruined, and remain vain wild beasts and swine in the world, which are good for nothing but eating and drinking. Remember this: if one does not breed children for teaching and art, but makes vain gluttons and drunkards who seek only food, where will one take pastors, preachers and other persons for the word of God, for the church office, for pastoral care and worship? Where will kings, princes and lords, cities and countries take chancellors, counselors, clerks and officials? There is no turf so small that could do without a scribe; we would learn to live in such a way that we do not have to deal with people in the world with whom art and writing are customary and honored; what kind of a desolate, horrible world would that become? Both spiritual, worldly, marital and domestic status would have to come to an end and the world would have to become a purifying stable. But who will help this? Who is guilty of such an abomination, for such abominable, harmful, poisonous parents have children whom they could draw to the service of God, and draw them alone to the service of the belly? Woe upon woe and but woe to them all!

Such evil worms or careless parents and husbands are in great need of reading or listening to this little book, so that they may learn what God gives them and what they owe to God for their children. My dear fellow, if you have a child who is sent to be taught, you are not free to bring him up as you please, nor is it at your discretion to do with it as you please, but you must see that you owe it to God to promote his two governments and to serve him in them; God needs a pastor, preacher, schoolmaster in his spiritual kingdom, and you can give him the same and do not do so: Behold, thou takest not one skirt from the poor, but many thousands of souls from the kingdom of God, and castest them into hell, as much as is in thee; for thou takest away the person who would be able to help such souls. Again, you do not give a skirt to your child who can become a pastor, nor do you found a monastery or churches, but you do something greater: you give a Savior and servant of God who can help many thousands of souls to heaven. Why is it that they are not all saved? Nevertheless, some do. What do you know whether your son will be? Are you not worthy, with all your goods, to help one hour to such a divine foundation and great service, and can you help all your life? But now you do the counterplay, not for one hour, but for the rest of your life. That means, of course, storming and robbing the monasteries, convents and churches, so that the rebellious peasants' storms are hardly a scolding (joke) and a prelude. Tell me, what hell can be deep and hot enough for such your harmful wickedness? O what punishment will come upon us for such misdeeds! Even in worldly government you can serve your lord or city more with the discipline of children than by building castles and cities for him and collecting all the treasures of the world. For what good is all this if you do not have learned, wise, pious people? I will be silent as to what temporal benefit and eternal reward you have from this before God and the world, that your child will also be better nourished here than according to your shameful, sordid advice and nobleness." (XIV, 260-63. [StL 14, 291-293])

Note 2: Concerning the support of poor pupils and students, the following provision is found in Luther's order of a common box of the congregation of Leisnig:

"If among such orphans and poor people of no means there are children, young boys, who would be well sent to school and would be able to understand the liberal arts and writing, they are to be fed and provided for by the heads of the common box alongside the other poor people, and the other boys are to be encouraged to work, crafts and quite a few trades". (X, 1170. [StL 10, 971])

Furthermore, Luther writes in his sermon that the children should be kept at school:

"Therefore, watch here who can watch. The authorities, if they see a good boy, should send him to school. If the father is poor, help with church property. Here the rich should give their wills, as those have done who have donated a number of scholarships; that would be right to give your money to the church. Here you do not redeem the departed souls from purgatory, but by preserving the divine offices you help both the living and the future, who have not yet been born, that they do not enter purgatory, yes, that they are redeemed from hell and go to heaven, and the living that they have peace and comfort. This would be a praiseworthy, Christian testament, for God would take pleasure and delight in it and would bless and honor you in turn, so that you would also take pleasure and delight in it." (X, 532. 533. [StL 10, 459])

Porta also lists the poor pupils and students among those to be received from the church's treasury when he writes:

"Thirdly, the poor students belong here, who study diligently and are of poor parents and have neither clothes nor books; they should not be abandoned, but should be of service and assistance to them, for they generally become people who do much useful work in the spiritual and secular state; as the great and noble man of God Martin Luther himself confesses that he was such a poor party stallion and that he sang the bread row Panem propter Deum (that is, bread for God's sake) in his dear city of Eisenach. Therefore one should not abandon such fine ingenia, and alms are well invested in them." (Pastorale Lutheri, 1133. 1134.)

Note 3 However, Luther reminds us that it is not enough merely to keep one's children studying, but also to establish and maintain the necessary institutions for this purpose, when he writes:

"I would like to see where one wants to take pastors, schoolmasters and sextons for more than three years? If we are not to do this, and especially if the princes are not to ensure that both boys' schools and high schools are properly organized, there will be such a shortage of people that three or four towns will have to be assigned to a pastor and ten villages to a chaplain; can they still be had? The high schools of Erfurt, Leipzig and others are more desolate, as are the boys' schools, so that it is a pity to see; and almost alone the small town of Wittenberg must now do the best." (Sermon that children should be kept at school, 1530. X, 504.505. [StL 10, 437])

In the same sermon Luther writes further:

"If this is how things are to go in German lands, I am sorry that I was born a German or that I have ever spoken or written in German...." (....) In the past, when people served the devil and desecrated the blood of Christ, all bags were open and there was no measure of giving to churches, schools and all abominations.... Now, however, right schools and right churches are to be founded, not founded, but only maintained in the building (for God has founded it and given enough to maintain it), and we know that God's word is and that it is called the right church built, Christ's blood and martyrdom honored: all the bags are closed with chains of iron; no one can give to it, nor can he tear the children away from it, nor grant them that they might be nourished by the church (since we have nothing to give) and come to such salutary offices, in which they are also provided for in time without their doing anything.. I ask God for a merciful hour, that he may take me away from here and not let me see the misery that must befall Germany. For I think that if ten Mosi stood and prayed for us, they would accomplish nothing; so I also feel, when I want to pray for my dear Germany, that my prayer rebounds and I do not want to go up, as I usually do when I pray for other things. For it will be that God will deliver Lot and sink Sodom.

God grant that I may lie and be a false prophet in this matter, which would come to pass if we would mend our ways and honor our Lord's word and his holy blood and death in a different way than has hitherto been done, and help and raise up the young people to the divine offices (as has been said)." (X, 530. 531. [StL 10, 456 f.])

Both of Luther's magnificent writings belong here: "Predigt, dass man die Kinder zur Schule halten soll," from the year 1530, and "Schrift an die Rathsherrn aller Städte Deutschlands, dass sie christliche Schulen aufrichten und halten sollen," from the year 1524. Both writings are printed in "Luther's Volksbibliothek," Volume IV. [StL 10, 416—485; LW 46, 213-258]

§ 63.

The congregation should see to it that the bread of life is broken for their fellow believers who lack spiritual things, and therefore support those who want to carry out this work of love, Acts 11:21-22: "And the hand of the Lord was with them: and a great number believed, and turned unto the Lord. Then tidings of these things came unto the ears of the church which was in Jerusalem: and they sent forth Barnabas, that he should go as far as Antioch.."

Note: John Canut Lenaeus, professor and archbishop of Upsala in Sweden, d. 1669, makes the following remark on Acts 11:22:

"The zeal of the Jerusalem congregation to strengthen the Antiochians in the faith is glorious. For it easily happens that false teachers creep in, who carry away new converts to a different mind, because they are not yet sufficiently fortified; which is what we really see happening here afterward, chap. 15. Thus, when Luther was preaching, Carlstadt, Zwingli, Münzer and the Anabaptists came in, and deceived a great multitude in Germany, France, Belgium, who have not been able to wriggle out of their error to this day. Thus the apostles, when the controversy about the abolition of the ceremonial law was smouldering, were filled with holy concern for the newly founded Antiochians, fearing also, because they had received

certain teachers, that they might at first imbibe an error which would not be easy to remove afterwards. They therefore sent Barnabas, an apostolic man, whose righteousness and integrity in doctrine and soundness were well known to them, that if they lacked anything in the perfection of the gospel doctrine, he might report it." (Comment. in Act.)

§ 64.

The congregation should be zealous for the spreading the written Word of God, 1 Thess. 5:27: "I charge you by the Lord that this epistle be read unto all the holy brethren." Col 4:16: "And when the epistle is read among you, see to it that it is also read in the church at Laodicea, and that you read the one from Laodicea." Cf. 1 Thess. 1:8.

Note: Thus Andreas Quenstedt writes:

"The service which the Church has to perform (with regard to Holy Scripture) is performed: 1. by bearing witness to Scripture, 2. by interpreting the motives for accepting Holy Scripture, 3. by preserving Scripture, 4. by disseminating it and offering it for sale, 5. by explaining it, 6. by defending it, and 7. by distinguishing the true books from the false." (Theol. did.-pol. I. c. 4. f. 130.)

§ 65.

The church should help to ensure that the gospel is also brought to those who still sit in darkness and the shadow of death, the poor Gentiles and Jews, Matthew 28:18-20: "All authority in heaven and on earth has been given to me. Go ye therefore, and teach all nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost. And teach them to observe all that I have commanded you. And lo, I am with you always, even to the end of the age." 1 Pet. 2:9: "But you are the chosen generation, the royal priesthood,

the holy nation, the people of his own possession, that you may proclaim the virtues of him who called you from darkness to his marvelous light." 2 Cor. 11:8: "And have robbed other churches, and taken wages from them, that I might preach unto you." (Cf. Acts 13:1-5).

Note: Veit Ludwig von Seckendorf writes in his "Christian State":

"Every church that has the opportunity to do so should also consider the propagation of the true Christian doctrine among the unbelievers.... Those communes which have the means and opportunity to bring Christian doctrine into such countries in a righteous, holy and good way, sin if they fail to do so, be it through carelessness and indolence, or by saving the money that would have to be spent; and this is an indication that the right zeal of Christian love does not exist, as which, among other things, should also move us to seize every opportunity to increase the glory and praise of God, spread his truth and expand the church, which we can with a good conscience and permissible means have good reason to put into practice." (III, 13, 10. p. 587. 588.)

Of the duty to missionize among the Gentiles, the old witty Conrad Dannhauer further writes in his Book of Conscience:

"As those who walk in darkness are bound in conscience to seek the light, so, according to the same law of conscience, we who, as children of light, enjoy the bright light, should be mindful of the Christian duty. For what else do we first ask of the most gracious God in our daily prayer but that his name may be sanctified by us, among us and abroad, known and spread as far as the sun reaches? But how can he go forth standing in his place without a vehicle, how can he be carried forth without a herdsman and a carter? The kingdom of God shall come as the bread rains from heaven, but not to the idle; no roasted dove falls from the air into anyone's lap. Mortals have a God who is merciful to everyone. The law of love sounds in our ears, that we

should bring back the unreasoning cattle, if they have strayed, how much more man? — That the will of God may be inclined to all, whom he wills not only to save himself, but also that they may be saved through us; that all nations may be taught and brought to him, that the gospel may be preached to every creature. It provokes and awakens the slumbering ones to that intolerable bragging of those, especially from the clan of Ignatius, namely the new apostles, who cry out: 'Who baptized the two Indies? Who subdued Brazil by preaching the Gospel? Who led the Peruvians and Mexicans from idolatry to the Christian religion? Who baptized the inhabitants of the Canary Islands, the Peguans, the Moluccans and Java? Who the inhabitants of the Philippines, the Japanese, the Canadians? Who went to the remotest of men, the Chinese, to teach them the Christian faith, if not the Jesuits, the monks, the papists? Whom should not these boasts inspire to a holy zeal? Could we tolerate that the children of this world should be wiser than the children of light? Shall we, while they watch for the good of their Lord and our destruction, sleep securely on both ears? Shall some of our own seek the days of men, eager to promote their own cause, but not at home for the cause of Christ? Shall we strive for the pulpit so that our teeth do not chatter with hunger? Shall we not only make the quarrelsome doctrines hateful, but also expose them and, if we do anything, hold up empty bags to the enemy's army camps, which, because they shoot peas, only pop? Shall others, of our nobles, when they are led up the mountain and shown the kingdoms of the world and their glory, the empty splendor, do not diligence to fight them, but to covet them? Shall still others go undaunted to distant India, not to make themselves heard, but to procure gold? Shall we bring home peacocks and monkeys, i.e. ourselves? Shall we discuss the free navigation of the sea and the commercial law of the Brazilians, the commercial ventures and advantages, filling thick books, such as that of Barläus, about the right of trade among the Brazilians, about commercial ventures and advantages,

but when it is a question of praising the precious pearl of the Gospel to the barbarians, do it all with three meager lines? Not thoughtlessly, but according to the wisest and holiest counsel of God, the Holy Spirit hovered in the form of a dove over the head of our Savior when he was baptized in the Jordan, so that we might learn to appreciate the splendor of divine goodness after the cleansing of the filth of our birth from this shadow. It was not without thought that he then sat on the disciples of the Redeemer in the cellar at Jerusalem, as on eggs, in order to hatch as many decoy doves for the wood and field doves, when he made not only preachers of the Gospel, but also Christians, according to the fruitfulness which the dove otherwise enjoys. But the call is manifold, one of example, another of the written message, a third of speech, a fourth of sighs of prayer.

1. The example of holy life rightly occupies the first place: — If this, like a fragrant odor, rises sweetly to the nose, it easily captivates the eye and the ear; but if it spreads an evil smell, it immediately repels. Be without deceit like the doves, is the Lord's commandment, given at the moment when he sent the doves out among the vultures to be tamed. The serpent is in the eyes, the dove in the heart. Learn from the dove: the simplicity of the eye, which looks straight at its goal, at God, without a sidelong glance, without a veil of mist; the gentleness, if not without gall, then without horns and teeth; the swiftness, without rushing and proud movement of the wings; the purity with abhorrence of filth, the chastity coupled with faith, the concord with truth. The most effective way to win over unbelievers would be for them to see in us what Christ has taught; for them to feel that we do not covet their kingdoms, thirst for their gold, seek their possessions, but seek nothing but their salvation and Christ's glory. This theology once subdued to Christ both the pride of philosophers and the invincible scepters of princes; without it, it would happen more quickly that we would degenerate into Turks and barbarians than that

we would draw these or those over to our side. Julianus Apostata, who was so cunning, perceived this art, and because it filled him with envy, he made it his own. He writes to Arsacius, a pagan priest in Galatia: 'That the Greek (pagan) religion is not yet gaining ground according to our heart's desire is the fault of those who administer the sanctuaries. Why do we not turn our eyes to that by which the atheism of Christians has grown, namely to the kindness they show to strangers, to the care they take to bury the dead, to the holiness of life they pretend? If the larva of papal-pharisaical holiness, if strict discipline, forced into certain rules of human order, has so much attraction to lure our Eceboli, how much will a sincere piety, measured according to the rule of Christ, be able to call souls from the Babylonian houses of shame into the halls of the apostolic church? — Once this first lure has penetrated the mind and the people, we must try another throw, namely

2. the written message. It is certainly worthy of admiration that the dove of the consul Hircius, which had been trained by him to perform such a service, once performed such work for Brutus at the siege of Mutina. What use were ramparts and vigilant sieges and even the nets stretched across the river to Antony at that time, since the messenger flew through the air? By the same service, the same servant bird helped the Tyrians for a time, who were surrounded by the Christian camps as if by a wreath. The inhabitants of Harlem in the Netherlands were repeatedly refreshed by the same service. The citizens of Lyons owe their life and breath to the same service, as Doucay confesses in his conversation with Lipsius. 'The doves, he says, were for us besieged the interpreters of the secret plans of our allies, these faithful letter carriers and the only heralds of saving help, since our situation was already mourned and our corpse almost mourned’. It is pious to try Satan's camps with the same cunning. They may be so fortified that nothing can be more secure, but they cannot close the entrance from heaven to the letters flying from elsewhere, which are carried by wings printed with ore on

paper, these representatives of the feet of the apostles. At that time things began to go badly with human affairs when the decrees of Gratian were given wings, but well when dove's wings bearing the divine decrees were put on. This happened through the divine invention of the art of printing, which made it impossible for those who lit the pyres to bring all the books that contradicted their cause onto the pyre, even if they wanted to, but were forced to do so against their will, to make room for the black sons of Cadmus (the letters), who quietly sneak in with their wings among the besieged, namely those who bring the Gospels, which breathe divine grace, foreshadow good things, are pleasing to us and proclaim that the clouds have been chased away from the sky and the sun has been restored to us; likewise the written creeds and compendia of the most important and useful disputations, so simply summarized that it is nevertheless learned, so briefly that it is nevertheless clear. The Greek translation of the prophecy of Isaiah 18:1 comes to mind here, where it says: 'Woe to the land that dwells under the shadow of wings, that sends messengers over the waters in papyrus vehicles,' where the Septuagint renders the latter as "paper letters." 'From this reading, says the Spanish Jesuit Caspar Sanctius, our preachers draw marvelous things, and those who think that this concerns the evangelical men who go out with papal letters to convert the barbarians. But this is quite foreign to the whole text, which is of a threatening nature and does not proclaim the sweet gospel, which the Spaniards would seem to be, if they were free by authority of the divine word to subjugate the scattered nations by force of arms and force them into the boundaries of the Church. Paper letters, then, are letters, as Seneca calls them, on papyrus tablets (sheets of paper), or carried out in a papyrus boat, because the Memphite boat is composed of the papyrus that absorbs the water. I think you realize where I am aiming. I wanted the biblical letters written by Almighty God to the human race to be brought everywhere, whether secretly or publicly, on

paper ships, so that Christ's kingdom might be spread far and wide. This holy undertaking has often been successful. Very praiseworthy was once the endeavor of the Tübingen theologians, who saw to it that the Augsburg Confession and the theological compendium of Heerbrand in Greek were brought to the Oriental Patriarch Jeremias in Constantinople, not as the blasphemous tongue of the most poisonous Aristarchus Stanislaus Socolovius, a Polish theologian, later spread, after they had caused a schism in the Occident, to call in a foreign fighter to support their party, but rather that they would roll away the disgrace of innovation spread by Sycophants in the Orient and sow the seeds of a purer faith, which Stephan Gerlach, the companion of the imperial envoy to the Ottoman Gate, the Noble Lord David Ungnad, to grow, so that if somehow, by God's grace, the eyes of the blind were opened and the Greeks renounced the errors that had arisen, many others might also be brought to the kingdom of Christ, so that a close union might take place among them in regard to the right faith, and the Constantinopolitan and Tübingen churches might be bound together by the bond of one Christian faith and love. This is certainly a noble plan and an incentive for us to promote this light.

3. Moreover, there are fraternal speeches to be made to the effect that, if I were a student going somewhere abroad, I should not lack the means to do so; if I were a soldier, I would rather be distinguished by foreign victories than epidemics; if I were attending trade fairs, I would rather bring back Protestant goods than gold; if I were an envoy, an envoy's companion, a companion's servant, I would rather seek a fruitful conversation than a fruitless display; if I were a prince, I would open the gates I could not enter with the dovecot with gold, I would support the enterprise of Pope Gregory XI., I would imitate the enterprise of Pope Gregory XI to establish colleges for the nations, and instead of the fratricidal wars that rage in the bowels of the Christian Church, I would entangle the Greenlanders, the Lapps, and other

barbarians in the sweet cords of the divine word.

4. Finally, if I wished to claim the name of a sincere Christian, I would, with the most tender sighs of prayer, implore from God a happy outcome for my enterprises." (Liber conscientiae. I, p. 886-390.)

§ 66.

The congregation should be prepared to unite with the Evangelical Lutheran congregations in its country, if it has the opportunity to do so and if such union serves and promotes the glory of God and the building up of his kingdom, Eph. 4:3-6: "Be diligent to maintain unity in the Spirit through the bond of peace. One body and one Spirit, just as you were called to one hope in your calling. One Lord, one faith, one baptism, one God and Father (our) of all, who is over you all, and through you all, and in you all." 1 Cor. 12:7: "In each one the gifts of the Spirit are manifested for the common good." Cf. Acts 15.

Note 1: That all churches and preachers have equal authority in themselves, and that therefore no one church is superior or subject to another, Luther testifies when he writes:

"We know that in Christendom it is thus done that all churches are equal, and no more than one church of Christ is in the world, as we pray: I believe in one holy Christian church. The reason is this: For if there be one church in the whole world, it has no other gospel or holy scripture, no other baptism and sacrament, no other faith and spirit, no other Christ and God, no other Lord's Prayer and prayer, no other hope and eternal life, than we have here in our church at Wittenberg, and its bishops are like our bishops or pastors and preachers, no one master or servant of another; they have one mind and heart, and all that belongs to the church is the same." (Against the papacy founded by the devil, dated 1545. XVII, 1398. [StL 17, 1115])

Note 2: Our Confession bears witness to the salvific nature of keeping the congregations and ministers together:

"Therefore the church can never be governed and preserved more than that we all live under one head, Christ, and that the bishops, all equal in office (though unequal in gifts), diligently keep together in sound doctrine, faith, sacraments, prayers, and works of love, etc..., as St. Jerome writes that the priests of Alexandria governed the Church collectively and in common, as did the apostles, and afterward all the bishops in all Christendom, until the pope raised his head above them all." (Smalcald Articles, Part II, Art. IV, par. 9; Trigl., pp. 473 f.)

Further on in the same symbolic writing it says:

"After this Jerome further says: but that one alone is chosen" (from among the presbyters or pastors), "who has others under him, is done to prevent division, so that not one here, the other there would draw a church to himself and the church would thus be torn apart. For at Alexandria (he says) from Mark the Evangelist down to Heraclam and Dionysium, the presbyters have always chosen one of them and held him higher and called him episcopum (a bishop), just as a people of war choose one to be their captain." (“Of the Power and Jurisdiction of Bishops,” pars. 61 f., Trigl., p. 522 [German text].)

Therefore, when once in the year 1614 a preacher was to be urged by his authorities to withdraw with his congregation from the association with a synod, and he asked a preacher in Hamburg for advice, the latter answered him with the approval of the entire Hamburg ministerium:

"Concerning your synod, I advise you faithfully not to separate yourselves from it, and do not cease to ask your beloved authorities to change their opinion of your separation from the synod in a Christian way. For in dangerous times, when the devil wants to stir up false doctrine and other inconveniences, such a laudable church union is of great benefit and can accomplish much in the Lord. Should your church be separated from the others now, it would stand alone in the future in case of need, and would perhaps be scolded for being apostate, from which much evil

could easily arise, about which the descendants would like to malign you, who are now causing it. Nevertheless, you can keep the things that have led your dear authorities to this opinion to yourselves and, where necessary, protest against them. Then you could seek the synod's advice and concerns." (Dedekennus' Thesaurus. II, 464.)

Thus the Leipzig theologian Hülsemann writes:

"It is of the greatest benefit and to a certain extent a moral necessity that, as there are presbyteries in the individual churches for the preservation of the individual churches, so also synods consist of several churches, whether they belong to one or several provinces and realms, for the preservation of several particular churches in unity of confession and respectability of morals." (Breviar. c. 18. th. 1. p. 532.)

Note 3. As earnestly as our theologians have maintained that all ministers of the Word have equal authority, and that therefore the so-called bishops are not by divine right above the other ministers of the Word, they have always conceded that it is salutary and in many respects necessary that there should be subordination and subordination among the ministers of the Church. Thus Johann Gerhard writes:

"We most seriously disapprove of the anarchy of those who abolish the order of the church office, since it is a source of discord and all evil, but we maintain in our churches the order among the church ministers and hold that the same should be maintained, so that some are bishops, others presbyters (pastors), others deacons, etc.... The establishment of an order among the ministers of the Church promotes harmony and unity, prevents the disruptions that are to be feared from the self-love and ambition of lower ministers, and restricts the audacity of those who wish to disturb the peace of the Church. Moreover, from the order and grades of the ministers arises the advantage that those can be tried in lower offices who are to be raised to higher ones, 1 Tim. 3:13." (Loc. de min. eccl. § 205.)

Furthermore, Balthasar Meisner writes:

"Because the distinction of the levels of office belongs to the middle things and is in Christian freedom, therefore we leave each church its freedom to either maintain the different levels of its church ministers,

or to abolish them, if only the weak are taken into account and no one is annoyed. Care must also be taken that this is done properly and honestly and for edification, as the holy apostle Paul wisely reminds us in 1 Cor. 14:26. But we... will not be dissuaded from retaining our bishops, or, as others prefer to call them, superintendents, and will retain them to the end of the world, and that for the sake of great benefit. For if the hearers have cause to complain of the negligence and slothfulness of their pastor in teaching, or of any other fault, then these hearers may apply to the bishop to whom that pastor is subject, accuse the pastor and substantiate their accusation, and he may afterwards, when the matter is heard and decided, if he is guilty, suffer the just punishment of his negligence, so that afterwards the sheep entrusted to him may be more dear to him than before." (Colleg. adiaphorist. Disp. 10. p. 9. 10.)

Thus Luther finally writes in his "Instruction of the Visitators" of 1528 and 1538:

"How divine and salutary a work it is to visit the parishes and Christian congregations by intelligent, skillful people, is sufficiently shown to us by both. New and Old Testament. For thus we read that St. Peter went about in the Jewish country, Act. 9:32, and St. Paul with Barnabas, Acts 15:36, also traveled anew through all the places where they had preached. And in all the epistles he shows how careful he is for all the churches and parishes, writes letters, sends his disciples, and also goes himself. Just as the apostles, Acts 8:14, when they heard how Samaria had received the word, sent Peter and John to them. And in the Old Testament we also read how Samuel went about now to Nama, now to Nobe, now to Galgal, and so on, not for the pleasure of going about, but for the love and duty of his office, and for the need and thirst of the people; as did Elias and Elishaeus, as we read in the books of Kings. This work Christ himself also did most diligently before all; so that for this reason he did not keep a place on earth to lay his head, which would be his own (Matt. 8:20). He also began to do this in his mother's womb, when he went with his mother over the mountains to visit St. John (Luke 1:39). What examples

the ancient fathers, the holy bishops, also diligently practiced in the past.... Who can tell how useful and necessary such an office is in Christendom? One can tell by the harm that has come from it, since it has fallen and been perverted by time. After all, no doctrine or state has remained right or pure, but instead so many abominable factions and sects have arisen as there are monasteries and convents, as a result of which the Christian church has been completely oppressed, faith has died out, love has turned into quarrels and war, evangelion has been put under the bench, and the works of men, doctrine and dreams have ruled instead of the gospel. Of course, the devil had done well, because he had brought such a ministry down and under himself, and had set up vain spiritual larvae and munchkins, so that no one resisted him; so that it was a great effort, even if the ministry went rightly and diligently in the pregnancy, as Paul complains to the Thessalonians, Corinthians and Galatians, that even the apostles themselves had their hands full with it. What good could the idle lazy bellies do here! Accordingly, now that the gospel has mercifully returned to us by the abundant, unspeakable grace of God, and brightness has gone forth again, through which we have seen Christendom confused, scattered, and torn asunder, we would gladly have seen the same true episcopal and visiting office restored, as being most necessary; but because none of us had been called to it or had certain orders, and St. Peter does not want to do anything in Christianity. Peter would not allow anything to be created in Christendom, unless it were certain that it was God's business (1 Peter 4:11), no one was allowed to prevent it before the other. Therefore we have wanted to play our conscience, and to keep to the office of love (which is common and commanded to all Christians), and have humbly and diligently petitioned the Most Serene, Most Reverend Prince and Lord, Mr. John,... as the prince of the land and our certain secular authority, by God's decree: that His Electoral Prince. Graces out of Christian love (for they are not guilty according to worldly authority) and for the sake of God, for the good of the Gospel and for the benefit and salvation of the miserable Christians in His Electoral Grace's lands. Grace's lands for the benefit and salvation of the miserable Christians in His Electoral Grace's lands. Which Your Elector! Graces have thus graciously done and arranged by God's good pleasure.... Although we

cannot let this (the instruction of the visitators) go forth as a strict commandment, so that we do not raise new papal decrees, but as a history or story, and as a testimony and confession of our faith, we nevertheless hope that all pious, peaceful pastors, to whom the Gospel is seriously pleasing, and who have a desire to hold with us unanimously and equally, as St. Paul teaches in Phil. 2. Paul teaches in Phil. 2, 2, that we should do so, will not ungratefully or proudly despise such diligence on the part of our sovereign and most gracious lord, nor our love and good will, but will willingly, without compulsion, after the manner of love, submit to such visitation, and together with us will live peaceably until God the Holy Spirit will do better through them or through us. But where some would willfully set themselves against it, and without good reason make a special desire, as one finds wild heads, who out of sheer malice cannot bear anything common or like, but are unequal and self-willed in heart and life: we must let them go from us as the chaff from the threshing floor, but, for their sake, not leave our like.... But God, the Father of all mercy, grant us through Christ Jesus, his dear Son, the Spirit of unity and the power to do his will. For though we are perfectly united, yet we have our hands full that we may do good and stand in the power of God. What would happen if we wanted to be divided and unequal among ourselves? The devil has not yet become pious or good, nor will he ever be. Therefore let us watch and be careful to maintain spiritual unity (as Paul teaches) in the bond of love and peace, amen." (Erlangen edition XXIII, 3-10. [StL 10, 1628-1632])

Preachers are also to care for the poor 40 — are to be provided with sufficient necessities according to their station 40 41 157-162 — are also not to intrude into a heretical parish 102 — when they are to be dismissed 141, 142, 146, 147 — cannot be dismissed arbitrarily 142-144. — cannot be appointed merely for a certain time 144-146 — are not to be dismissed immediately for the sake of mere weakness in doctrine and life, but are to be admonished in humility and kindness 146 — are not to be made false teachers for the sake of uncomfortable words in right opinion 146. — What process is to be observed in their removal 147. 148. — Where possible, they are not to be removed without the involvement of other righteous preachers 148. 149. — Only those who have become incapable are not to be removed, but their office is to be taken from them 149. — Emeritus pastors are to be retained by the congregation 165. — Their poor survivors are to be provided for by the congregation 165. 166 — They are not authorized to perform public ministries privately in a foreign diocese, even if the majority of their parishioners have followed them there 187 — They are not to accept foreign parishioners for Holy Communion, except in the parish of their own choice. 187. 188. — They are equal to one another, neither master nor servant of the other 212. — They should diligently keep together 213. — Superiority and subordination among them is a matter of freedom, but beneficial to the church 214. 215.

Preaching ministry can be divided into stages 55. 56. — its establishment is commanded to the church 67. — is necessary 67. 68.

Schools, children's schools, every parish was asked to establish 96-99 — are to be maintained from the parish treasury for the sake of the poor 161. 162 — high schools are also to be endowed and maintained 203. 204.

School teachers, if their office is an ecclesiastical one 99. 100. — are to be placed under the supervision of the preacher 100. 101. — are to be kept honest 161. — their frequent dressing and undressing does harm 161.

Soul register is to be created by the preacher 168.

Sunday, the celebration of which is not instituted by God, but to be kept diligently 82-84.

Estates, secular, are not as such in the church 11. 12, (see: State — Authority.

Majority of votes does not decide matters of conscience or what is already decided in God's Word and the confessions 61. 62. — should decide in indifferent matters 62. 63. 172. —  in certain cases the majority should give way to the minority 172. 173.

Voting ability 50-54.
